The \textbf{finite intersection} of the pre-images of these slices are exactly all tuples whose $\alpha_i$-th component is in an open set in the $\alpha_i$-th slice. Note that if $\Lambda$ is finite, this is equivalent to taking all the slices, but setting the open sets in the irrelevant slices to be the entire space.

There is another perspective to look at the product space. Consider $\R^n$. If we take one open interval $I_i$ from each dimension, then their product $\prod_{i = 1}^{n}I_i$ is an $n$-dimensional cuboid or ``box''. So in general, if we take the product of open sets in each dimension, the resultant set in the product space is a union of many such boxes. 
\begin{dfnbox}{Box Topology}{box}
    Let $\left\{\left(X_{\alpha}, \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right)\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a sequence of non-empty topological spaces. The {\color{red} \textbf{box topology}} is the topology generated by the basis 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{\prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha} \colon U_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfnbox}
We may check that the set $\mathcal{B}$ in Definition \ref{dfn:box} is indeed a basis. Take any $\mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}X_{\alpha}$, then for each $\alpha \in \Lambda$, there exists some $U_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}$ such that $x_{\alpha} \in U_{\alpha}$, which implies that 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha}.
\end{equation*}
If $V \coloneqq \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha}$ and $U \coloneqq \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha}$ are such that $\mathbfit{x} \in U \cap V$, then $x_{\alpha} \in U_{\alpha} \cap V_{\alpha}$ for each~$\alpha \in \Lambda$. Clearly, $U_{\alpha} \cap V_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}$, so we can take $W \coloneqq \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha} \cap V_{\alpha}$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbfit{x} \in W \subseteq V \cap U.
\end{equation*}
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        For finite product spaces, the product topology and the box topology are the same. However, they are different in general if the product is infinite.
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
As a counter example to illustrate the above remark, consider $\left\{X_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be a sequence of countably infinitely many pair-wise disjoint topological spaces such that every space contains at least one non-empty proper open set $U_n$. Take 
\begin{equation*}
    U \coloneqq \prod_{n = 1}^{\infty}U_n,
\end{equation*}
then $U$ is clearly open with respect to the box topology but not the product topology. In particular, let $\pi\left(\N^+\right)$ be a permutation of $\N^+$, then each open set with respect to the product topology can be written as 
\begin{equation*}
    U \coloneqq \prod_{i = 1}^{n}U_{\pi\left(\N^+\right)_i} \times \prod_{i = n + 1}^{\infty}X_{\pi\left(\N^+\right)_i}
\end{equation*}
for some $n \in \N^+$, where $U_{\pi\left(\N^+\right)_i}$ is open in $X_{\pi\left(\N^+\right)_i}$. This also shows that the box topology is \textbf{coarser} than the product topology. One product space constructed in a similar manner is the \textit{Hilbert cube} $\left[0, 1\right]^{\N}$.

Observe that by using the box topology, we can easily decompose $\R^n$ into the product of $n$ copies $\R$ while maintaining the topological structure, i.e., the product topology induced on the product space is the standard topology on the product space. However, we can actually generalise this further.
\begin{probox}{Standard Topology Decomposition on Euclidean Spaces}{decompose}
    Let $n \in \Z^+$ and $n = \sum_{i = 1}^km_i$ with $m_i \in \Z^+$ for $i = 1, 2, \cdots, k$. Let $\mathcal{T}_i$ be the standard topology on $\R^{m_i}$ and $\mathcal{T}$ be the product topology induced by the $\mathcal{T}_i$'s, then $\mathcal{T}$ is the standard topology on $\R^n$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{B}_n$ be the standard basis generating the standard topology $\mathcal{T}_n$ over $\R^n$ for each $n \in \N^+$. Take any $B_r^{\left(n\right)}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \in \mathcal{B}_n$. For any $\mathbfit{y} \in B_r^{\left(n\right)}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)$, define $t = r - \norm{\mathbfit{x - y}}$ and consider, for each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, k$,
        \begin{equation*}
            B_t^{\left(m_i\right)}\left(\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right)\right) \in \mathcal{B}_{m_i} \subseteq \mathcal{T}_i
        \end{equation*}
        to be an open ball centred at the projection of $\mathbfit{y}$ to the $m_i$-th factor. Denote 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_t \coloneqq \prod_{i = 1}^{k}B_t^{\left(m_i\right)}\left(\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right)\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Clearly, $B_t$ is contained by the basis for the product topology induced by the $\mathcal{T}_i$'s. For any $\mathbfit{p} \in B_t$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \norm{\mathbfit{p - x}} \leq \norm{\mathbfit{p - y}} + \norm{\mathbfit{x - y}} < r.
        \end{equation*} 
        Therefore, $\mathbfit{y} \in B_t \subseteq B_r^{\left(n\right)}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)$. By Proposition \ref{pro:fineBasis}, $\mathcal{T} \subseteq \mathcal{T}_n$. Conversely, take an arbitrary $U_i \in \mathcal{T}_i$ for each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, k$. Notice that for any $\mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{i = 1}^kU_i$, there is some open ball $B_{r_i}^{\left(m_i\right)}\left(\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)\right) \in \mathcal{B}_{m_i}$, centred at the projection of $\mathbfit{x}$ to the $m_i$-th factor, such that $B_{r_i}^{\left(m_i\right)}\left(\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)\right) \subseteq U_i$. Take
        \begin{equation*}
            r \coloneqq \min_{1 \leq i \leq k}r_i
        \end{equation*}
        and consider the open ball $B_r^{\left(n\right)}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \in \mathcal{B}_n$. For any $\mathbfit{y} \in B_r^{\left(n\right)}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)$, consider 
        \begin{align*}
            \norm{\mathbfit{y - x}} & = \sum_{i = 1}^{k}\norm{\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right) - \pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)} < r \leq \min_{1 \leq i \leq k}r_i.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, for every positive integer $i \leq k$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \norm{\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right) - \pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)} < r_i,
        \end{equation*}
        which implies that $\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right) \in B_{r_i}^{\left(m_i\right)}\left(\pi_{\R^{m_i}}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)\right) \subseteq U_i$. Therefore, $\mathbfit{y} \in \prod_{i = 1}^{k}U_i$ and so $\mathcal{T}_n \subseteq \mathcal{T}$ by Proposition \ref{pro:fineBasis}. Therefore, $\mathcal{T} = \mathcal{T}_n$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
The importance of the product topology is that it is the coarsest topology to ensure the existence of continuous projection maps.
\begin{probox}{Product Topology Guarantees Continuous Projection}{continuousProj}
    Let $\left\{\left(X_{\alpha}, \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right)\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a sequence of non-empty topological spaces. The product topology on $\prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}X_{\alpha}$ is the coarsest topology such that $\pi_{X_{\alpha}}$ is continuous for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{S}$ be the sub-basis generating the product topology and let $U \subseteq X_{\alpha}$ be open, then $\pi^{-1}_{X_{\alpha}}\left(U\right) \in \mathcal{S}$ and therefore is open. Therefore, $\pi_{X_{\alpha}}$ is continuous with respect to the product topology.
        \\\\
        Let $\mathcal{T}$ be any topology on $\prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}X_{\alpha}$ such that $\pi_{X_{\alpha}}$ is continuous, then for any open set $U \in \mathcal{T}$, the pre-image $\pi^{-1}_{X_{\alpha}}\left(U\right) \in \mathcal{S}$ must be open, so $\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{T}$. Notice that this is equivalent to $\mathcal{T}$ containing 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{\bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}\pi^{-1}_{X_{\alpha}}\left(U_{\alpha}\right) \colon \Lambda' \subseteq \Lambda \textrm{ is finite}, U_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        However, $\mathcal{B}$ generates the product topology, which is the coarsest topology containing~$\mathcal{B}$, so $\mathcal{T}$ must be finer than the product topology.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Analogously, we can construct continuous functions over the product space using continuous functions defined on each of the individual spaces.
\begin{probox}{Continuous of Functions over Product Spaces}{continuousOverProd}
    Let $\left\{\left(X_{\alpha}, \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right)\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a sequence of non-empty topological spaces and let $Y$ be a topological space. For any $\alpha \in \Lambda$, define $f_{\alpha} \colon Y \to X_{\alpha}$, then $f \colon Y \to \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}X_{\alpha}$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        f\left(y\right) = \bigl(f_{\alpha}\left(y\right)\bigr)_{\alpha \in \Lambda}
    \end{equation*}
    is continuous if and only if $f_{\alpha}$ is continuous for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $f$ is continuous. Notice that for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$,
        \begin{equation*}
            f_{\alpha}\left(y\right) = \pi_{X_{\alpha}}\bigl(f\left(y\right)\bigr)
        \end{equation*}
        for all $y \in Y$. Therefore, $f_{\alpha} = \pi_{X_{\alpha}} \circ f$. By Proposition \ref{pro:continuousProj}, $\pi_{X_{\alpha}}$ is continuous, and so $f_{\alpha}$ is continuous for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$.
        \\\\
        Conversely, suppose that $f_{\alpha}$ is continuous for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$. Notice that the basis for the product topology is given by 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{\bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}\pi^{-1}_{X_{\alpha}}\left(U_{\alpha}\right) \colon \Lambda' \subseteq \Lambda \textrm{ is finite}, U_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        For any finite $\Lambda' \subseteq \Lambda$, we have 
        \begin{align*}
            f^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}\pi^{-1}_{X_{\alpha}}\left(U_{\alpha}\right)\right) & = \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}f^{-1}\left(\pi^{-1}_{X_{\alpha}}\left(U_{\alpha}\right)\right) \\
            & = \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}\left(\pi_{X_{\alpha}} \circ f\right)^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}\right) \\
            & =  \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}f_{\alpha}^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}\right),
        \end{align*}
        which is open. Therefore, $f$ is continuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Notice that it is important to equip the product space with the product topology but not the box topology. Define $f \colon \R \to \R^{\N}$ such that $f\left(x\right) = \left(x, x, \cdots\right)$. Note that every component of $f\left(x\right)$ is obtained by the identity map which is continuous. We claim that $f$ is not continuous with respect to the box topology. Consider 
\begin{equation*}
    U \coloneqq \prod_{n = 1}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{1}{n}, \frac{1}{n}\right),
\end{equation*}
then clearly $f\left(0\right) = \mathbf{0} \in U$. Suppose on contrary that $f$ is continuous with respect to the box topology, then $f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ should be open, which means that there exists some $\epsilon > 0$ such that $\left(-\epsilon, \epsilon\right) \subseteq f^{-1}\left(U\right)$. This means that $f\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right) \in U$, which is not possible because there exists $N \in \N^+$ such that $\frac{1}{n} < \frac{\epsilon}{2}$ for all $n \geq N$.

Recall that a binary arithmetic operation on a set $Y$ is simply a map $h \colon Y \times Y \to Y$. If there are two continuous maps $f, g \colon X \to Y$, then they are essentially the component maps of the map $F \colon X \to Y \times Y$ defined by 
\begin{equation*}
    F\left(x\right) = \bigl(f\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr).
\end{equation*}
If $h$ is continuous, then we can construct a continuous map $H = h \circ F$, which is fundamentally just applying the binary operation on two continuous maps.
\begin{corbox}{Arithmetic of Continuous Functions}{arithmetic}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and let $f, g \colon X \to \R$ be continuous functions, then the functions $f + g$, $f - g$ and $fg$ are continuous. If $0 \notin g\left(X\right)$, then $\frac{f}{g}$ is continuous.
\end{corbox}
Note that our definition of the product topology relies on a sub-basis, which can be too convoluted when we try to represent an open set in the product space. To simplify this, we propose a way to identify the basis for the product topology.
\begin{probox}{Basis for Finite Product Topology}{prodTopoBasis}
    Let $\left\{\left(X_i, \mathcal{T}_i\right)\right\}_{i = 1}^n$ be a finite family of topological spaces and let $\mathcal{B}_i$ be the basis for $\mathcal{T}_i$ for all~$i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, then $\prod_{i = 1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_i$ is a basis for the product topology over $\prod_{i = 1}^nX_i$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}$ be the topology generated by $\mathcal{B} \coloneqq \prod_{i = 1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_i$ and $\mathcal{T}$ be the product topology generated by the sub-basis 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{S} \coloneqq \left\{\pi_{X_{i}}^{-1}\left(U_{i}\right) \colon i = 1, 2, \cdots, n, U_{i} \in \mathcal{T}_{i}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that the basis induced by $\mathcal{S}$ is 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{S}} \coloneqq \left\{\bigcap_{i \in I}\pi_{X_{i}}^{-1}\left(U_{i}\right) \colon I \subseteq \left\{1, 2, \cdots, n\right\}, U_i \in \mathcal{T}_{i}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Take any $B \coloneqq \prod_{i = 1}^{n}B_i \in \mathcal{B}$ and any $\mathbfit{x} \in B$, then $\pi_{X_i}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \in B_i$ for each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_{\mathcal{S}} \coloneqq \bigcap_{i = 1}^n\pi_{X_i}^{-1}\left(B_i\right) \in \mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{S}},
        \end{equation*}
        then for each $\mathbfit{y} \in B_{\mathcal{S}}$, we have $\pi_{X_i}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right) \in B_i$ for all $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$ and so $\mathbfit{y} \in B$. Therefore, $\mathbfit{x} \in B_{\mathcal{S}} \subseteq B$. Similarly, we can prove that $B \subseteq \mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{S}}$, so by Proposition \ref{pro:fineBasis}, we have $\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}} = \mathcal{T}$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
We can generalise this basis to infinite product spaces:
\begin{probox}{Natural Basis for Product Topology}{prodTopoBasis}
    If $\left\{X_i\right\}_{i \in I}$ is an indexed family of topological spaces and $\mathcal{F}$ is the set of all finite subsets of $I$, then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\left\{\prod_{i \in I \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}U_i \colon U_i \textrm{ is open in } X_i\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    is a basis for $X \coloneqq \prod_{i \in I}X_i$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By definition, $X$ is generated by the sub-basis 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{S} \coloneqq \bigcup_{i \in I}\left\{\pi_{X_i}^{-1}\left(U_i\right) \colon U_i \textrm{ is open in } X_i\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        so it suffices to prove that $\mathcal{S}$ generates $\mathcal{B}$. Take any $i \in I$ and any open set $U_i \in X_i$, it is clear that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{X_i}^{-1}\left(U_i\right) = U_i \times \prod_{j \in F \setminus \left\{i\right\}} X_j \in \mathcal{B},
        \end{equation*}
        so $\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{B}$. However, this means that for any $S_1, S_2 \in \mathcal{S}$, we have $S_1 \cap S_2 \in \mathcal{B}$, which means that $\mathcal{B}$ contains all finite intersections of the sets in $\mathcal{S}$. Take any $B \in \mathcal{B}$, then there exists some finite subset $F \subseteq I$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            B = \prod_{i \in I \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}U_i = \bigcap_{i \in F}\pi_{X_i}^{-1}\left(U_i\right),
        \end{equation*}
        where each $U_i$ is open in $X_i$. Therefore, every $B \in \mathcal{B}$ is a finite intersection of the sets in $\mathcal{S}$. Therefore, $\mathcal{S}$ generates $\mathcal{B}$ and so $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis for $X$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
However, notice that every open set in a topological space can be expressed by the basis of its topology, so we can further transform this construction to the following:
\begin{probox}{Product Space Basis Induced from Factor Space Bases}{prodBasis}
    If $\left\{X_i\right\}_{i \in I}$ is an indexed family of topological spaces where $\mathcal{B}_i$ is the basis for $X_i$. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of all finite subsets of $I$. Define 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{B}_F \coloneqq \left\{\prod_{i \in I \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i \colon B_i \in \mathcal{B}_i\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    for each $F \in \mathcal{F}$, then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\mathcal{B}_F
    \end{equation*}
    is a basis for $X \coloneqq \prod_{i \in I}X_i$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}' \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\left\{\prod_{i \in I \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}U_i \colon U_i \textrm{ is open in } X_i\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then $\mathcal{B}'$ is the basis for $X$ by Lemma \ref{pro:prodTopoBasis}. Observe that $\mathcal{B} \subseteq \mathcal{B}'$, so it suffices to prove that for each $U \in \mathcal{B}'$ and any $\mathbfit{u} \in U$, there exists some $B \in \mathcal{B}$ with $\mathbfit{u} \in B \subseteq U$. Take any 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \coloneqq \prod_{i \in I \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}U_i \in \mathcal{B}'
        \end{equation*}
        where each $U_i$ is open in $X_i$ and consider an arbitrary $\mathbfit{x} \in U$. For all $i \in F$, there exists some $B_i \in \mathcal{B}_i$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{X_i}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \in B_i \subseteq U_i.
        \end{equation*}
        Consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            B \coloneqq \prod_{i \in I \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i \subseteq U_i.
        \end{equation*}
        Notice that $\mathbfit{x} \in B \in \mathcal{B}$, so $\mathcal{B}$ and $\mathcal{B}'$ both generate $X$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Let us combine the product space with subspace topologies. Intuitively, the subspace topology on product space should be the product of subspace topologies.
\begin{probox}{Subspace Topology on Product Spaces}{prodSubTopo}
    Let $\left(X, \mathcal{T}_X\right)$ and $\left(Y, \mathcal{T}_Y\right)$ be topological spaces. Let $A \subseteq X$, $B \subseteq Y$ and $\mathcal{T}_{A \times B}$ be the subspace topology on $A \times B$ induced by the product topology. If $\mathcal{T}_{A \times B}'$ be the product topology induced by the subspace topologies on $A$ and $B$, then $\mathcal{T}_{A \times B} = \mathcal{T}_{A \times B}'$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{T}_A$ and $\mathcal{T}_B$ be the subspace topologies on $A$ and $B$ respectively and $\mathcal{T}_{A \times B}$ be the product topology on $A \times B$, then $\mathcal{T}_{A \times B}$ is generated by the basis 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{A \times B} \coloneqq \left\{U_A \times U_B \colon U_A \in \mathcal{T}_A, U_B \in \mathcal{T}_B\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Let $\mathcal{T}_X$ and $\mathcal{T}_Y$ be the subspace topologies on $X$ and $Y$ respectively and $\mathcal{T}_{X \times Y}$ be the product topology on $X \times Y$, then $\mathcal{T}_{X \times Y}$ is generated by the basis
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{X \times Y} \coloneqq \left\{U_X \times U_Y \colon U_X \in \mathcal{T}_X, U_Y \in \mathcal{T}_Y\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        let $\mathcal{T}$ be the subspace topology on $A \times B$, then $\mathcal{T}$ is generated by the basis 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{\left(U_X \times U_Y\right) \cap \left(A \times B\right) \colon U_X \in \mathcal{T}_X, U_Y \in \mathcal{T}_Y\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Take any $U_A \times U_B \in \mathcal{B}_{A \times B}$, then there exists some $U_X \in \mathcal{T}_X$ and $U_Y \in \mathcal{T}_Y$ such that $U_A = U_X \cap A$ and $U_B = U_Y \cap B$. Therefore,
        \begin{equation*}
            U_A \times U_B = \left(U_X \cap A\right) \times \left(U_Y \cap B\right) = \left(U_X \times U_Y\right) \cap \left(A \times B\right) \in \mathcal{B},
        \end{equation*}
        and so $\mathcal{B}_{A \times B} \subseteq \mathcal{B}$. Take any $\left(U_X \times U_Y\right) \cap \left(A \times B\right) \in \mathcal{B}$. Notice that $U_X \cap A \in \mathcal{T}_A$ and $U_Y \cap B \in \mathcal{T}_B$, so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(U_X \times U_Y\right) \cap \left(A \times B\right) = \left(U_X \cap A\right) \times \left(U_Y \cap B\right) \in \mathcal{B}_{A \times B}.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, $\mathcal{B} \subseteq \mathcal{B}_{A \times B}$ and so $\mathcal{B}_{A \times B} = \mathcal{B}$. Therefore, $\mathcal{T} = \mathcal{T}_{A \times B}$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Lastly, let us discuss some additional properties of the product space for metric spaces. Recall that we have discussed the $L^p$-metric in the previous sections. This metric can be extended to the product space.
\begin{dfnbox}{$L^p$-metric on a Product Space}{Lp}
    Let $\left(X_1, d_{X_1}\right), \left(X_2, d_{X_2}\right), \cdots, \left(X_n, d_{X_n}\right)$ be metric spaces, we define 
    \begin{align*}
        d_1\bigl(\left(x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n\right), \left(y_1, y_2, \cdots, y_n\right)\bigr) & \coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^{n}d_{X_i}\left(x_i, y_i\right) \\
        d_{\infty}\bigl(\left(x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n\right), \left(y_1, y_2, \cdots, y_n\right)\bigr) & \coloneqq \max_{1 \leq i \leq n}d_{X_i}\left(x_i, y_i\right).
    \end{align*}
\end{dfnbox}
It is easy to see that the two metrics are equivalent in the product space by Proposition \ref{pro:metricEquivalence}. In fact, we shall analogously prove that they both induce the product topology.
\begin{probox}{Metric for Product Topology}{prodTopoMetric}
    Let $\left\{\left(X_i, d_{X_i}\right)\right\}_{i = 1}^n$ be a sequence of topological spaces, then $d_1$ and $d_{\infty}$ both induce the product topology over $\prod_{i = 1}^nX_i$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        For any $\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y} \in \prod_{i = 1}^nX_i$, it it clear that 
        \begin{equation*}
            d_{\infty}\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) \leq d_1\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) \leq nd_{\infty}\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right).
        \end{equation*}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:metricEquivalence}, it suffices to prove that $d_{\infty}$ induces the product topology. Let 
        \begin{align*}
            B_r^{\infty}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) & \coloneqq \left\{\mathbfit{y} \in \prod_{i = 1}^nX_i \colon d_{\infty}\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) < r\right\} \\
            & = \left\{\prod_{i = 1}^nB_{r_i}\left(\pi_{X_i}^{-1}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)\right) \colon i = 1, 2, \cdots, n, r_i \leq r\right\}
        \end{align*}
        and
        \begin{align*}
            \mathcal{B}_{\infty} & \coloneqq \left\{B_r^{\infty}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \colon \mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{i = 1}^nX_i, r \in \R^+ \right\} \\
            & = \prod_{i = 1}^n\left\{B_r\left(x\right) \colon x \in X_i, r \in \R^+\right\}.
        \end{align*}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:prodBasis}, $\mathcal{B}_{\infty}$ is clearly a basis for the product topology.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Note that our above construction only applies to finite product spaces. In infinite product spaces, we need to address the possible un-boundedness of the metric we choose. To do this, we can normalise the metric.
\begin{probox}{Normlised Metric Induces the Metrisable Topology}{rho}
    Let $\left(X, d\right)$ be a metric space and define $\rho \colon X \times X \to \R$ by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \rho\left(x, y\right) \coloneqq \frac{d\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d\left(x, y\right)},
    \end{equation*}
    then $\rho$ is a metric on $X$ and $\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}\left(X\right) < 1$. Let $\mathcal{T}_{\rho}$ and $\mathcal{T}_d$ be the topologies on $X$ induced by $\rho$ and $d$ respectively, then $\mathcal{T}_{\rho} = \mathcal{T}_d$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We first show that $\rho$ is a metric. The non-negativity and symmetry are obvious, so it suffices to prove the triangle inequality. Consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(t\right) = \frac{t}{1 + t}.
        \end{equation*}
        One may check that $f$ is an increasing concave function with $f\left(0\right) = 0$. Therefore,
        \begin{equation*}
            \frac{b}{a + b}f\left(a + b\right) = \frac{a}{a + b}f\left(0\right) + \frac{b}{a + b}f\left(a + b\right) \leq f\left(b\right).
        \end{equation*}
        By symmetry, $\frac{a}{a + b}f\left(a + b\right) \leq f\left(a\right)$, and so $f\left(a\right) + f\left(b\right) \geq f\left(a + b\right)$. Therefore, for any $x, y, z \in X$, 
        \begin{align*}
            \rho\left(x, y\right) + \rho\left(y, z\right) & = f\bigl(d\left(x, y\right)\bigr) + f\bigl(d\left(y, z\right)\bigr) \\
            & \geq f\bigl(d\left(x, y\right) + d\left(y, z\right)\bigr) \\
            & \geq f\bigl(d\left(x, z\right)\bigr) \\
            & = \rho\left(x, z\right).
        \end{align*}
        It is clear that $\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}\left(X\right) = \max_{\left(x, y\right) \in X^2}\rho\left(x, y\right) < 1$. Notice that 
        \begin{align*}
            2\rho\left(x, y\right) & = \frac{2d\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d\left(x, y\right)} \\
            & > d\left(x, y\right) \\
            & > \rho\left(x, y\right)
        \end{align*}
        for all $x, y \in X$. Therefore, $\mathcal{T}_d = \mathcal{T}_{\rho}$ by Proposition \ref{pro:metricEquivalence}.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
With the above result, we can now obtain a well-behaved metric over infinite product spaces and therefore extend our discussion to these spaces.
\begin{probox}{Metrisable Topology on Infinite Product}{infProdTopo}
    Let $\left\{\left(X, d_{X_i}\right)\right\}_{i = 1}^{\infty}$ be a sequence of metric spaces and define $\rho_i \colon X_i \times X_i \to \R$ by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \rho_i\left(x, y\right) \coloneqq \frac{d_{X_i}\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d_{X_i}\left(x, y\right)},
    \end{equation*}
    then $d \colon \prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}X_i \times \prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}X_i \to \R$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        d\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) \coloneqq \sup \left\{\frac{\rho_{i}\left(x_i, y_i\right)}{i} \colon i \in \Z^+\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    is a metric inducing the product topology on $\prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}X_i$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{T}_d$ and $\mathcal{T}_{\rho}$ be the topology induced by $d$ and the product topology with respect to the metrisable topologies induced by $\rho_i$'s respectively. Define $\rho_i' = \frac{1}{i}\rho_i$. Let~$\mathcal{B}_d$ be the basis induced by the metric $d$ and $\mathcal{B}_i$ be the basis induced by the metric $\rho_i'$. Take any $B_r^d\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \in \mathcal{B}_d$. For any $\mathbfit{y} \in B_r^d\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \frac{\rho_i\left(x_i, y_i\right)}{i} \leq d\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) < r
        \end{equation*}
        for all $i \in \Z^+$. Therefore, $y_i \in B_r^{\rho_i'}\left(x_i\right)$ and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_r^d\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \subseteq \prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}B_r^{\rho_i'}\left(x_i\right)
        \end{equation*}
        for any $\mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}X_i$. Notice that for any $r > 0$, there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that~$\frac{1}{i} < \frac{r}{2}$ for all $i \geq N$. By Proposition \ref{pro:rho}, $\mathrm{diam}\left(\rho_i\right) < 1$ for all $i \in \N^+$, so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathrm{diam}\left(\frac{1}{i}\rho_i\right) = \frac{1}{i}\mathrm{diam}\left(\rho_i\right) < \frac{r}{2}
        \end{equation*}
        for all $i \geq N$. Therefore, $B_r^{\rho_i'}\left(\mathbfit{x}_i\right) = X_i$ for any $\mathbfit{x}_i \in X_i$ and
        \begin{equation*}
            \sup\left\{\frac{\rho_{i}\left(x_i, y_i\right)}{i} \colon i \geq N\right\} \leq \frac{r}{2} < r.
        \end{equation*}
        Take any $\mathbfit{y} \in \prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}B_r^{\rho_i'}\left(x_i\right)$, we have 
        \begin{align*}
            d\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) & = \sup \left\{\frac{\rho_{i}\left(x_i, y_i\right)}{i} \colon i \in \Z^+\right\} \\
            & = \max\Biggl\{\max\left\{\frac{\rho_{i}\left(x_i, y_i\right)}{i} \colon 0 < i < N\right\}, \sup\left\{\frac{\rho_{i}\left(x_i, y_i\right)}{i} \colon i \geq N\right\}\Biggr\} \\
            & < r,
        \end{align*}
        and so $\mathbfit{y} \in B_r^d\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)$. This means that for any $\mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}X_i$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_r^d\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) = \prod_{i = 1}^{N - 1}B_r^{\rho'_i}\left(x_i\right) \times \prod_{i = N}^{\infty}X_i.
        \end{equation*}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:prodBasis}, the basis of $\mathcal{T}_{\rho}$ is 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{\rho} \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\left\{\prod_{i \in F}B_r^{\rho'_i}\left(x_i\right) \times \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \colon x_i \in X_i, r \in \R^+\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        where $\mathcal{F}$ is the set of all finite subsets of $\N^+$. This means that every open ball in the metrisable topology induced by $d$ is open in the product topology on $\prod_{i = 1}^{\infty}X_i$, and so~$\mathcal{T}_d \subseteq \mathcal{T}_{\rho}$. Take any $N \in \N^+$ and any finite subset 
        \begin{equation*}
            K \coloneqq \left\{k_1, k_2, \cdots, k_{N - 1}\right\} \subseteq \N^+.
        \end{equation*}
        For any $r_1, r_2, \cdots, r_{N - 1} \in \R^+$, take 
        \begin{equation*}
            B \coloneqq \prod_{i = 0}B_{r_i}^{N - 1}\left(x_{k_i}\right) \times \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus K}X_i \in \mathcal{B}_{\rho}.
        \end{equation*}
        Let $r \coloneqq \min\left\{r_1, r_2, \cdots, r_{N - 1}\right\} > 0$, then for any $\mathbfit{y} \in B$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathbfit{y} \in B_r^{d}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \subseteq B,
        \end{equation*} 
        and so $\mathcal{T}_{\rho} \subseteq \mathcal{T}_d$. Therefore, $\mathcal{T}_d = \mathcal{T}_{\rho}$ is the product topology.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Quotient Spaces}
If there are product topologies, then by right there should also be quotient topologies. The term ``quotient'' arises from the quotient of a set by an equivalence relation.

Notice that every equivalence relation induces a partition and every partition also induces an equivalence relation. If $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $X$, then $X \slash \sim$ is a partition of~$X$ by its equivalence classes. On the other hand, if $X^*$ is a partition of a set $X$, then an equivalence relation $\sim$ on $X$ can be defined as $x \sim y$ if and only if there exists some $S \in X^*$ such that~$x, y \in S$.

If $X \slash \sim$ is a partition for a space $X$, our focus is then a map $p \colon X \to X \slash \sim$ such that $x \in p\left(x\right)$, i.e., $p$ is an ``indexing'' map for the components in the partition. 
\begin{dfnbox}{Canonical Projection Map}{canonicalProjection}
    Let $X$ be a set and $X \slash \sim$ be a partition of $X$, then a surjective map $p \colon X \to X \slash \sim$ is a {\color{red} \textbf{canonical projection map}} if $x \in p\left(x\right)$.
\end{dfnbox}
Note that under a canonical projection map $p$, for each $S \in X \slash \sim$, we have $S = p^{-1}\left(S\right)$. This means that an equivalence class in the quotient set $X \slash \sim$ is open if and only if its pre-image is open.
\begin{dfnbox}{Quotient Map}{quotient}
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be topological spaces. A surjective map $p \colon X \to Y$ is a {\color{red} \textbf{quotient map}} if~$V \subseteq Y$ is open if and only if $p^{-1}\left(V\right) \subseteq X$ is open.
\end{dfnbox}
Note that $p\bigl(p^{-1}\left(V\right)\bigr) = V$, so this definition is essentially saying that a quotient map $p$ is such that \textbf{pre-images of open sets are open} and \textbf{pre-images of non-open sets are not open}. Note that this has nothing to do with whether an open set will be sent to an open image!
\begin{dfnbox}{Open and Closed Maps}{openAndClosed}
    A continuous map $f \colon X \to Y$ is {\color{red} \textbf{open}} if $f\left(U\right)$ is open in $Y$ for any open set $U \subseteq X$, and {\color{red} \textbf{closed}} if $f\left(V\right)$ is closed in $Y$ for any closed set $V \subseteq X$.
\end{dfnbox}
There are many edge cases for open and/or closed maps.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item A map that is closed but not open: consider $f \colon \left[0, 1\right] \cup \left[2, 3\right] \to \left[0, 2\right]$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        f\left(x\right) = \begin{cases}
            x & \quad\textrm{if } x \in \left[0, 1\right] \\
            x - 1 & \quad\textrm{if } x \in \left[2, 3\right]
        \end{cases}.
    \end{equation*}
    Take any closed set $A$, then both $A_1 \coloneqq A \cap \left[0, 1\right]$ and $A_2 \coloneqq A \cap \left[2, 3\right]$ are closed. Therefore, both $f\left(A_1\right)$ and $f\left(A_2\right)$ are closed in $\left[0, 2\right]$. Therefore, 
    \begin{equation*}
        f\left(A\right) = f\left(A_1\right) \cup f\left(A_2\right)
    \end{equation*}
    is closed, and so $f$ is closed. However, notice that $\left(0, 1\right]$ is open in $\left[0, 1\right] \cup \left[2, 3\right]$ but 
    \begin{equation*}
        f\bigl(\left(0, 1\right]\bigr) = \left(0, 1\right] \subseteq \left[0, 2\right]
    \end{equation*}
    is not open in $\left[0, 2\right]$. Therefore, $f$ is not open.
    \item A map that is open but not closed: consider $g \colon \left(0, 1\right) \cup \left(2, 3\right) \to \left(0, 2\right)$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        g\left(x\right) = \begin{cases}
            x & \quad\textrm{if } x \in \left(0, 1\right) \\
            x - 1 & \quad\textrm{if } x \in \left(2, 3\right)
        \end{cases}.
    \end{equation*}
    Take any open set $B$, then both $B_1 \coloneqq B \cap \left(0, 1\right)$ and $B_2 \coloneqq B \cap \left(2, 3\right)$ are open. Therefore, both $g\left(B_1\right)$ and $g\left(B_2\right)$ are open in $\left(0, 2\right)$. Therefore, 
    \begin{equation*}
        g\left(A\right) = g\left(A_1\right) \cup g\left(A_2\right)
    \end{equation*}
    is open, and so $g$ is open. However, notice that $\left(0, 1\right)$ is closed in $\left(0, 1\right) \cup \left(2, 3\right)$ but 
    \begin{equation*}
        g\bigl(\left(0, 1\right)\bigr) = \left(0, 1\right) \subseteq \left(0, 2\right)
    \end{equation*}
    is open in $\left(0, 2\right)$. Therefore, $g$ is not closed.
    \item A map that is neither open nor closed: let $X \coloneqq \bigl(\left[0, \infty\right) \times \R\bigr) \cup \bigl(\R \times \left\{0\right\}\bigr)$ and consider~$h \colon X \to \R$ defined by $h\left(x, y\right) = x$. Note that $C_1 \coloneqq \R^+_0 \times \left(1, 2\right)$ is open in~$X$ but $h\left(C_1\right) = \R^+_0$ is closed in $\R$, and that $C_2 \coloneqq \left\{\left(x, \frac{1}{x}\right) \colon x \in \R^+\right\}$ is closed in $X$ but~$h\left(C_2\right) = \R^+$ is open in $\R$. Therefore, $h$ is neither open nor closed.
    \item A map that is both open and closed: let $X$ be a set, then the identity map 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{id}_X \colon \left(X, \left\{\varnothing, X\right\}\right) \to \bigl(X, \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)\bigr)
    \end{equation*}
    is both open and closed.
\end{enumerate}
Note that the maps in the first two examples are continuous and the map in the last example is not continuous, so continuity is not related to the openness of maps in general. 
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        \quad
        \begin{itemize}
            \item If a surjective continuous map is open or closed, then it is a quotient map.   
            \item Quotient map, open map and closed map are all preserved under composition.
        \end{itemize} 
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
Let us re-visit the third example where 
\begin{equation*}
    X \coloneqq \bigl(\left[0, \infty\right) \times \R\bigr) \cup \bigl(\R \times \left\{0\right\}\bigr) 
\end{equation*}
and we consider the projection $\pi_1 \colon X \to \R$ defined by $\pi_1\left(x, y\right) = x$. It is interesting that this function is surjective, continuous, neither open nor closed, but it \textbf{is a quotient map}! For every open set $\left(a, b\right) \in \R$, we have 
\begin{equation*}
    \pi_1^{-1}\bigl(\left(a, b\right)\bigr) = \bigl(\left(a, b\right) \times \R\bigr) \cap X
\end{equation*}
which is open. For every set $A \subseteq \R$ with $\pi_1^{-1}\left(A\right)$ being open, we have $A$ to be open.

One feature of functions which lead to this seemingly strange behaviour is the fact that the pre-image of $f\left(X\right)$ is often a superset of $X$, so with forward mapping, the function fails to ``capture all the information'' in a set. To fix this issue, we restrict our consideration to only a specific type of sets.
\begin{dfnbox}{Saturated Set}{saturate}
    Let $f \colon X \to Y$ be a surjective continuous map. A set $A \subseteq X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{saturated}} with respect to $f$ if $A = f^{-1}\left(S\right)$ for some $S \subseteq Y$.
\end{dfnbox}
Equivalently, this means that $A = f^{-1}\bigl(f\left(A\right)\bigr)$, i.e., a saturated set contains everything that could be mapped to its own image.
\begin{probox}{Equivalent Definition for Quotient Maps}{equivQuotient}
    Let $f \colon X \to Y$ be a surjective continuous map, then $f$ is a quotient map if and only if~$f\left(A\right)$ is open (closed) in $Y$ whenever $A$ is a saturated open (closed) set with respect to~$f$ in $X$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any saturated open set $A \subseteq X$, then $A = f^{-1}\bigl(f\left(A\right)\bigr)$. By definition \ref{dfn:quotient}, since $f^{-1}\bigl(f\left(A\right)\bigr)$ is open, $f\left(A\right)$ must be open in $Y$. Conversely, suppose that $f\left(A\right)$ is open in $Y$ for all saturated open set $A \subseteq X$. Let $U \subseteq Y$ such that $f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ is open in~$X$. Notice that $f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ is saturated. Therefore, $f\bigl(f^{-1}\left(U\right)\bigr) = U$ is open in $Y$. Since $f$ is surjective, it is a quotient map.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Due to the nice property of saturated sets, by intuition a quotient map should be preserved under restriction onto a saturated sub-domain.
\begin{probox}{Restriction of Quotient Map to Saturated Open Sets}{restrictQuotient}
    If $f \colon X \to Y$ is a continuous quotient map and $A \subseteq X$ is a saturated open (closed) set with respect to $f$, then $\left.f\right\rvert_A \colon A \to f\left(A\right)$ is a quotient map.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $B \subseteq A$ be open and saturated with respect to $\left.f\right\rvert_A$, then $B = \left.f\right\rvert_A^{-1}\bigl(\left.f\right\rvert_A\left(B\right)\bigr)$. Note that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left.f\right\rvert_A\left(B\right) = f\left(B \cap A\right) = f\left(B\right)
        \end{equation*}
        for any $B \subseteq A$. Since $B$ is open,by Proposition \ref{pro:equivQuotient}, $f\left(B\right)$ is open. This implies that $\left.f\right\rvert_A$ is a quotient map.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Now, whether a map is a quotient map depends on the topology we choose because we need to be aware of the open and closed sets in the space. Therefore, we may be interested in the types of topologies which can induce a quotient map.
\begin{dfnbox}{Quotient Topology}{quotientTopo}
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be sets and $p \colon X \to Y$ be a surjective map. A topology on $Y$ is a {\color{red} \textbf{quotient topology}} with respect to $p$ if $p$ is a quotient map with respect to the topology.
\end{dfnbox}
Let us consider the canonical projection map $p \colon X \to X \slash \sim$ again. Assuming Axiom of Choice, we can fix a choice function $c \colon X \slash \sim \to X$ to represent each equivalence class $\left[x\right]_{\sim}$ by $c\left(\left[x\right]_{\sim}\right)$. Define $X^* \coloneqq c\left(X \slash \sim\right)$, then $X^* \subseteq X$. This allows us to define a \textit{quotient space}.
\begin{dfnbox}{Quotient Space}{quotientSpace}
    Let $X$ be a topological space with partition $X \slash \sim$. Let $c \colon X \slash \sim \to X$ be a choice function and $X^* \coloneqq c\left(X \slash \sim\right)$. Let $p \colon X \to X^*$ be a surjective map such that $x \in c^{-1}\bigl(p\left(x\right)\bigr)$. If $\mathcal{T}^*$ is a quotient topology on $X^*$, then $\left(X^*, \mathcal{T}^*\right)$ is the {\color{red} \textbf{quotient space}} of $X$.
\end{dfnbox}
The advantage of assuming Axiom of Choice here is that without the choice function, we cannot reduce each equivalence class into a singleton, and so there could be multiple topologies such that the canonical projection $p \colon X \to X \slash \sim$ is a quotient map. To deal with that, we have to define the quotient topology as the finest topology to guarantee the continuity of $p$ as a quotient map. However, with the choice function, we can actually guarantee the uniqueness of this quotient topology!
\begin{probox}{Existence and Uniqueness of Quotient Topology}{exist!quotientTopo}
    Let $p \colon X \to A$ be surjective for some $A \subseteq X$, then there exists a unique topology $\mathcal{T}_A$ such that $p$ is a quotient map.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{T}_A \coloneqq \left\{U \subseteq A \colon p^{-1}\left(U\right) \subseteq X \textrm{ is open}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Observe that $\varnothing, A \in \mathcal{T}_A$. Note that for any index set $\Lambda$ such that $U_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_A$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$,
        \begin{equation*}
            p^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha}\right) = \bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}p^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is open is $X$, so $\bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{T}_A$. Similarly, one may check that $\bigcup_{i = 1}^nU_{i} \in \mathcal{T}_A$ for any~$n \in \N^+$ such that $U_i \in \mathcal{T}_A$ for all $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$. Let $\mathcal{T}'$ be any topology on $A$ such that $p$ is a quotient map. For any $U \subseteq A$, notice that $U \in \mathcal{T}'$ if and only if $p^{-1}\left(U\right) \subseteq X$ is open, i.e., $U \in \mathcal{T}'$ if and only if $U \in \mathcal{T}_A$. Therefore, $\mathcal{T}' = \mathcal{T}_A$, which means that $\mathcal{T}_A$ is unique.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}

\section{Topological Embeddings}
The relationship between a ``container'' space and a ``contained'' space in topological spaces can be quite peculiar. On one hand, we can define such relationship using the classic subset-superset relationship. On the other hand, we see that what defines a topological space is not so much about the actual elements contained in the space, but the structure exhibited by the space, which can be preserved under certain transformations.

In the context of topological spaces, this means that open spaces should be preserved.
\begin{dfnbox}{Homeomorphism}{homeomorphism}
    A function $h \colon X \to Y$ between topological spaces is a {\color{red} \textbf{homeomorphism}} if it is a bijective open map.
\end{dfnbox}
In other words, a bijection $h$ between topological spaces is a homeomorphism if both $h$ and $h^{-1}$ are continuous. Sometimes, $h$ is known as \textit{bi-continuous}. Roughly speaking, a homeomorphism is a reversible deformation such that open sets are still open before and after the deformation. 
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        Since both bijective maps and open maps are preserved under composition, homeomorphism is preserved under composition.
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
One merit of having a homeomorphism is that it helps construct a structure-preserving map that ``projects'' a space into another possibly bigger space.
\begin{dfnbox}{Topological Embedding}{topologicalEmbedding}
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be topological spaces. A continuous injective function $f \colon X \hookrightarrow Y$ is a {\color{red} \textbf{topological embedding}} if it is homeomorphic onto $f\left(X\right)$.
\end{dfnbox}
The utility of topological embeddings is that they allow us to transform an unfamiliar or highly abstract topological space into something we are more comfortable with, without altering the topological structures of the space. Therefore, anything we do with the image will be reflected in the original space in the same way. A good candidate for this ``space that we are comfortable with'' is the Euclidean spaces.

In a metric space, we can further generalise the notion of a structure-preserving map into maps which preserve the distance between points.
\begin{dfnbox}{Isometric Embedding}{isometric}
    Let $\left(X, d_X\right)$ and $\left(Y, d_Y\right)$ be metric spaces. A map $f \colon X \to Y$ is an {\color{red} \textbf{isometric embedding}} if for all $a, b \in X$,
    \begin{equation*}
        d_Y\bigl(f\left(a\right), f\left(b\right)\bigr) = d_X\left(a, b\right).
    \end{equation*}
    If $f$ is surjective, then $f$ is said to be an {\color{red} \textbf{isometry}}.
\end{dfnbox}
\chapter{Countability Axioms}
\section{Second Countable Spaces}
Note that a topological space can be completely captured by its basis, so an important property of a topological space is the countability of its basis. Roughly speaking, the countability of the basis represents how ``easy'' it is to generate all open sets in the space using some fixed open sets.
\begin{dfnbox}{Second Countable Space}{2ndCountable}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{second countable}} if it has a countable basis.
\end{dfnbox}
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        Equivalently, a topological space $X$ is second countable if and only if for every open set $S \subseteq X$, there exists a countable collection $\mathcal{U}_S$ of open sets in $X$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            S = \bigcup_{U \in \mathcal{U}_S}U.
        \end{equation*}
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
It is important to note that the countability of a space's basis does not depend on the countability of the space itself. For example, $\R^n$ is an uncountable space, but its standard topology has a countable basis 
\begin{equation*}
    \left\{B_r\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \colon \mathbfit{x} \in \Q^n, r \in \Q\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Even for the uncountable product space $\R^{\omega}$, there exists a countable basis for the product topology, given by 
\begin{equation*}
    \left\{\prod_{i \in I}\left(a_i, b_i\right) \times \prod_{i \in \Z \setminus I} \colon \left(a_i, b_i\right) \in \Q^2, I \subseteq \Z \textrm{ is finite}\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Since a second countable space can be ``covered'' by countably many open sets, it is not surprising that the density of the space is at most countable.
\begin{probox}{Dense Countable Subspaces in Second Countable Spaces}{denseCountable}
    If $X$ is a second countable space, then there exists a countable subset $A \subseteq X$ such that $A$ is dense in $X$. The converse is true if $X$ is metrisable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose $X$ is second countable and let $\mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_i \colon i \in \N^+\right\}$ be a countable basis for $X$. For each $i \in \N^+$, fix some $x_i \in B_i$ and define $A \coloneqq \left\{x_i \colon i \in \N^+\right\}$. It is clear that~$A$ is countable. For every open set $U \subseteq X$, there exists some $n \in \N^+$ such that~$B_n \subseteq U$. Since $\left\{x_n\right\} \subseteq A \cap B_n$, we have $A \cap U \neq \varnothing$ and so $A$ is dense in $X$. Conversely, let $A \subseteq X$ be a countable dense subset. Define
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_{\frac{1}{n}}\left(a\right) \colon a \in A, n \in \N^+\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        which is countable. We claim that $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis for the metrisable topology on $X$. Note that for any $x \in X$, then there exists some $\epsilon > 0$ such that $x \in B_{\epsilon}\left(x\right)$. Notice that there exists $N \in \N^+$ such that $\frac{1}{N} < \epsilon$. Since $A$ is dense in $X$, there exists some $a \in A$ such that $a \in B_{\frac{1}{N}}\left(x\right)$, which implies that $x \in B_{\frac{1}{N}}\left(a\right) \in \mathcal{B}$, and so $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis for the metrisable topology on $X$. Therefore, $X$ is second countable.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Intuitively, second countability is preserved in subspace topologies because we need fewer basic open sets to cover a subspace.
\begin{probox}{Subspace Topologies Preserve Second Countability}{subspaceSecondCountability}
    If $X$ is a second countable space, then every $Y \subseteq X$ is second countable with respect to the subspace topology.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{B}_X$ be a countable basis for $X$, then clearly 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_Y \coloneqq \left\{B \cap Y \colon B \in \mathcal{B}_{X}\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        is a countable basis for $Y$. Therefore, $Y$ is second countable.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
It is natural that second countability is preserved under finite product. However, countably infinite product spaces still preserve second countability.
\begin{probox}{Countable Product Topologies Preserve Second Countability}{prodSecondCountability}
    If $\left\{X_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a family of second countable spaces and 
    \begin{equation*}
        X \coloneqq \prod_{n \in \N^+}X_n
    \end{equation*}
    is the countable product space equipped with the product topology, then $X$ is second countable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{B}_i$ be a countable basis for $X_i$. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of all finite subsets of $\N^+$, then by Proposition \ref{pro:prodBasis}, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\left\{\prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i \colon B_i \in \mathcal{B}_i\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        is a basis for $X$. Define  $f \colon \mathcal{B}_F \to \prod_{i \in F}\mathcal{B}_i$ by $f\left(U\right) = \left(B_i\right)_{i \in F}$ if and only if 
        \begin{equation*}
            U = \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i.
        \end{equation*}
        It is clear that $f$ is a bijection and so for all $F \in \mathcal{F}$,
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{\prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i \colon B_i \in \mathcal{B}_i\right\} \approx \prod_{i \in F}\mathcal{B}_i,
        \end{equation*}
        which is countable because it is a finite Cartesian product over countable sets. Therefore, $\mathcal{B}$ is countable because it is a countable union of countable sets. Therefore, $X$ is second countable.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox} 
\section{First Countable Spaces}
We can also discuss countability in a ``local'' context. Consider a random point $x$ in some topological space. Suppose that $U$ is an open set containing $x$. Intuitively, we can reduce~$U$ by taking an open subset of $U$ containing $x$. We do this repeatedly until we find some minimal open subset of $U$ containing $x$. Now, we can perform this operation to every open set containing $x$ in the space and collect all these minimal open subsets. The countability of this collection offers a different criterion of space categorisation.
\begin{dfnbox}{Countable Basis}{countableBasis}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. For all $x \in X$, a {\color{red} \textbf{countable basis of $x$}} is a countable collection $\mathcal{B}$ of open sets in $X$ containing $x$ such that for any open set $Y \subseteq X$ containing~$x$, there exists some $B \in \mathcal{B}$ with $B \subseteq Y$.
\end{dfnbox}
The next definition is a ``local'' version of second countable spaces.
\begin{dfnbox}{First Countable Space}{1stCountable}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{first countable}} if every $x \in X$ has a countable basis.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, every second countable space is first countable. Suppose $\mathcal{U}$ is a countable basis for the space, then for each point $x$ in the space, we can simply take $\left\{U \in \mathcal{U} \colon x \in U\right\}$ as a countable basis of $x$.

We claim that all metric spaces are first countable, because for any metric space $X$ and any~$x \in X$, we can take its countable basis as 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_{\frac{1}{i}}\left(x\right) \colon i \in \Z^+\right\}.
\end{equation*}
This above example also helps show the existence of first countable spaces which are not second countable. Let $\R$ be equipped with the discrete metric, then the metrisable space induced has no countable basis because it has the discrete topology, but is obviously first countable.

Consider some uncountable set $Y$ equipped with the co-finite topology. For any $y \in Y$, suppose it has a countable basis $\mathcal{B}$. Note that in the co-finite topology, a set is open if and only if its complement is finite. Therefore, there exists some finite set $F_i \subseteq Y$ such that there exists some $B_i \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $Y \setminus F_i = B_i$. Notice that 
\begin{equation*}
    Y \setminus \left(\left\{y\right\} \cup \bigcup_{i = 1}^{\infty}F_i\right) \neq \varnothing.
\end{equation*}
Take some $z \in Y \setminus \left(\left\{y\right\} \cup \bigcup_{i = 1}^{\infty}F_i\right)$, then $U \coloneqq Y \setminus \left\{z\right\}$ is open such that $y \in U$. Note that for all $B_i \in \mathcal{B}$, we have $z \in B_i$. This means that $B_i \not\subseteq U$ for all $i \in \Z^+$ because otherwise~$y \in U$, which is not possible. Therefore, $\mathcal{B}$ is not a countable basis.

Suppose that $x$ is a random point in a topological space with a countable basis $\mathcal{B}$. Fix any~$B \in \mathcal{B}$, then $x \in B$. Notice that for any open set $U$ containing $x$, it is not possible that $U$ and $B$ are disjoint. Therefore, there exists some open subset of $B$ which contains $x$. This motivates the following natural question: can we always re-construct the countable basis such that for any pair of sets in the basis, one must be a subset of the other?
\begin{probox}{Construction of Countable Basis}{constructCountable}
    Let $x \in X$ be a point with a countable basis $\mathcal{B}$, then there exists some countable basis $\mathcal{B}'$ of $x$ such that for any $B_1, B_2 \in \mathcal{B}'$, either $B_1 \subseteq B_2$ or $B_2 \subseteq B_1$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any $B_1, B_2 \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $B_1 \not\subseteq B_2$ and $B_2 \not\subseteq B_1$, then $B_1 \cap B_2$ is open and $x \in B_1 \cap B_2$. Therefore, for any open set $U$ containing $B_2$, clearly $B_1 \cap B_2 \subseteq U$. Therefore, we can repeat this process until we obtain a countable basis $\mathcal{B}'$ such that for any $B_1', B_2' \in \mathcal{B}'$, we have either $B_1' \subseteq B_2'$ or $B_2' \subseteq B_1'$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Based on this small proposition, we introduce the following result to help us identify spaces which are not first countable:
\begin{probox}{Closure Characterisation of First Countable Spaces}{characterise1stCountableClosure}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $A \subseteq X$. If there exists a sequence $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+} \subseteq A$ such that $x_i \to x$, then $x \in \widebar{A}$. The converse is true if $X$ is first countable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that there exists a sequence $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+} \subseteq A$ such that $x_i \to x$. Notice that $\widebar{A} = A \cup A'$, then it suffices to prove that $x \notin A$ implies $x \in A'$. Suppose that~$x \notin A$. Note that for any open set $U \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U$, there exists some $N \in \N$ such that $x_n \in U$ for all $n \geq N$. In particular, this means that~$x_N \in U \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{x\right\}\right)$. Therefore, $x$ is a limit point of $A$.
        \\\\
        Conversely, suppose that $x \in \widebar{A}$. Since $X$ is first countable, $x$ has some countable basis $\mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_i \colon i \in \N^+\right\}$. By Proposition \ref{pro:constructCountable}, we can assume that $B_j \subseteq B_i$ whenever $j \geq i$. For each $i \in \N^+$, take 
        \begin{equation*}
            x_i \in \bigcap_{j = 1}^{i}\left(B_j \cap A\right),
        \end{equation*}
        then $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i = \N^+}$ is a sequence in $A$. Let $U$ be any open set containing $x$, then there exists some $B_N \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $x \in B_N \subseteq U$. However, notice that for all $i \geq N$, we have~$x_i \in B_N \subseteq U$, and so $x_i \to x$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
In the above result, first countability is crucial for us to construct the desired sequence. 

We can re-write this result using continuous maps.
\begin{probox}{Continuous Map Characterisation of First Countable Spaces}{characterise1stCountableContinuous}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. If $f \colon X \to Y$ is continuous, then for any sequence $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ with $x_i \to x$, then $f\left(x_i\right) \to f\left(x\right)$. The converse is true if $X$ is first countable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $f \colon X \to Y$ is continuous. Let $U \subseteq Y$ be an open set such that~$f\left(x\right) \in U$, then $x \in f^{-1}\left(U\right)$, which is open. Notice that there exists some~$N \in \N^+$ such that $x_i \in f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ for all $i \geq N$, i.e., $f\left(x_i\right) \in U$ for all $i \geq N$. Therefore, $f\left(x_i\right) \to f\left(x\right)$.
        \\\\
        Conversely, suppose that for any sequence $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ with $x_i \to x$, then $f\left(x_i\right) \to f\left(x\right)$. Take any $A \subseteq X$ with $x \in \widebar{A}$. Since $X$ is first countable, by Proposition \ref{pro:characterise1stCountableClosure}, there exists a sequence $\left\{y_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ in $A$ such that $y_i \to x$. This means that $f\left(y_i\right) \to f\left(x\right)$. Since $f\left(y_i\right) \in f\left(A\right)$, this means that $f\left(x\right) \in \widebar{f\left(A\right)}$, and so $f\left(\widebar{A}\right) \subseteq \widebar{f\left(A\right)}$. By Proposition \ref{pro:continuousMapProps}, $f$ is continuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Similar to second countable spaces, first countability should also be ``inherited'' in subspaces.
\begin{probox}{Subspaces Preserve First Countability}{subspaceFirstCountability}
    If $X$ is first countable, then every subspace $Y \subseteq X$ with the subspace topology is first countable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any $y \in Y$ and let $V \subseteq Y$ be any open set in $Y$ containing $y$, then there exists some open set $U \subseteq X$ such that $V = U \cap Y$. Since $X$ is first countable, there exists a countable collection $\mathcal{B}$ of open sets in $X$ containing $y$ such that there exists some $B_y \in \mathcal{B}$ with $y \in B_y \subseteq U$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}' \coloneqq \left\{B \cap Y \colon B \in \mathcal{B}\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then it is clear that $\mathcal{B}'$ is a countable collection of open sets in $Y$ containing $y$. Notice that $B_y \cap Y \in \mathcal{B}'$ is such that $y \in B_y \cap Y \subseteq V$, so $Y$ is first countable.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Without surprise, countable product will preserve first countability as expected.
\begin{probox}{Countable Product Preserves First Countability}{prodFirstCountability}
    If $\left\{X_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a family of first countable spaces and 
    \begin{equation*}
        X \coloneqq \prod_{n \in \N^+}X_n,
    \end{equation*}
    then $X$ is first countable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{B}_i$ be a basis for $X_i$ and define $X \coloneqq \prod_{i \in \N^+}X_i$. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of all finite subsets of $\N^+$, then by Proposition \ref{pro:prodBasis},
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\left\{\prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i \colon B_i \in \mathcal{B}_i\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        is a basis for $X$. Take any $\mathbfit{x} \in X$. By first countability, for each $i \in \N^+$, there exists a countable basis $\mathcal{B}_{X_i} \subseteq \mathcal{B}_i$ at $\pi_{X_i}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)$. Define
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{\mathbfit{x}} \coloneqq \bigcup_{F \in \mathcal{F}}\left\{\prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_{i} \colon B_{i} \in \mathcal{B}_{X_i}\right\} \subseteq \mathcal{B},
        \end{equation*}
        which is countable. Let $U \subseteq X$ be any open set containing $\mathbfit{x}$, then there exists some~$F \in \mathcal{F}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_i \in \mathcal{B}.
        \end{equation*}
        By first countability, for each $i \in F$, there exists some $B_{X_i} \in \mathcal{B}_{X_i}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{X_i}^{-1}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \in B_{X_i} \subseteq B_i,
        \end{equation*}
        and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathbfit{x} \in \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_{X_i} \in \mathcal{B}_{\mathbfit{x}}.
        \end{equation*}
        Notice that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_{X_i} \subseteq \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus F}X_i \times \prod_{i \in F}B_{i} \subseteq U,
        \end{equation*}
        so $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbfit{x}}$ is a countable basis at $\mathbfit{x}$. Therefore, $X$ is first countable.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Lindelof Spaces}
Now, based on some intuition, we see that certain spaces are ``tight'' in a sense that they have a clearly defined boundary, whereas some other spaces like $\R$ can extend indefinitely. One idea to measure the ``tightness'' of a topological space is to consider the number of open sets needed to cover the entire space.

First, notice that given any topological space, we can always partition the space into a collection of open sets in the space.
\begin{dfnbox}{Open Cover}{openCover}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. An {\color{red} \textbf{open cover}} is a collection of open sets $\left\{U_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha} = X.
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfnbox}
The first step here is to check if the space can be covered by countably many open sets.
\begin{dfnbox}{Lindelof Space}{Lindelof}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{Lindelof}} if every open cover of $X$ contains a countable sub-cover.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, a countable basis will induce a countable cover of the space.
\begin{probox}{Second Countable Spaces Are Lindelof}{2ndCountableLindelof}
    Every second countable space $X$ is Lindelof. The converse is true if $X$ is metrisable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $X$ is second countable, then there exists a countable basis 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_i \colon i \in \N^+\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        for $X$ and define
        \begin{equation*}
            I \coloneqq \left\{i \in \N^+ \colon \textrm{there exists some } U \in \mathcal{U} \textrm{ such that } B_i \subseteq U\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        For each $i \in I$, pick some $U_i \in \mathcal{U}$ with $B_i \subseteq U_i$. Note that for any $x \in X$, there exists some $U \in \mathcal{U}$ with $x \in U$. Since $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis, there exists some $i \in \N^+$ such that~$B_i \subseteq U$ with $x \in B_i$, which means that $i \in I$ and so $x \in B_i \subseteq U_i$. Therefore,~$\left\{U_i\right\}_{i \in I}$ is a countable sub-cover for $X$ and so $X$ is Lindelof. Conversely, suppose that $X$ is a metrisable Lindelof space and let 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{V}_n \coloneqq \left\{B_{\frac{1}{n}}\left(x\right) \colon x \in X\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        for each $n \in \N^+$, then clearly each $\mathcal{V}_n$ is an open cover for $X$. For each $n \in \N^+$, fix a countable sub-cover $\mathcal{V}'_n \subseteq \mathcal{V}_n$ and define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \bigcup_{n \in \N^+}\mathcal{V}'_n,
        \end{equation*}
        which is countable. For any open set $U \subseteq X$ and any $x \in U$, there exists some $\epsilon > 0$ such that $B_{\epsilon}\left(x\right) \subseteq U$. Take $N \in \N^+$ with $\frac{1}{N} < \frac{\epsilon}{2}$, then since $\mathcal{V}'_N$ covers $X$, there exists some $y \in X$ such that $x \in B_{\frac{1}{N}}\left(y\right) \in \mathcal{V}'_N \subseteq \mathcal{B}$. Observe that $y \in B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(x\right) \subseteq U$, so this means that $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis for the metrisable topology on $X$ and so $X$ is second countable.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\chapter{Separation Axioms}
The \textit{separation axioms} are a series of conditions imposed on topological spaces to better specify the notion of \textit{disjoint} sets and \textit{distinguishable} points. The motivation comes from the fact that knowing two points are distinct is not enough to differentiate one from the other in a topological space setting. For example, if $x \neq y$ are \textit{distinct} points in a topological space $X$, it is completely possible that every open set containing $x$ also contains $y$ and vice versa. Therefore, though unequal, there is no practical way to seclude $x$ and $y$ into disjoint regions topologically.
\section{$T_1$ Spaces}
$T_1$ spaces define properly the notion of separation in a topological sense. Before we introduce the full definition, it might be useful to consider a few preliminary notions.
\begin{dfnbox}{Distinguishable Points}{distingushi}
    Two points $x$ and $y$ in a topological space are {\color{red} \textbf{topologically distinguishable}} if one of them has an open neighbourhood which does not contain the other.
\end{dfnbox}
Note that being distinguishable alone is not sufficient to ``separate'' two points. Topologically speaking, we think of ``separation'' as the ability to seclude two objects into disjoint open regions.
\begin{dfnbox}{Separated Points}{separated}
    Two points $x$ and $y$ in a topological space are {\color{red} \textbf{separated}} if there exists open neighbourhoods $U, V$ with $x \in U$ and $y \in V$ such that $x \notin V$ and $y \notin U$.
\end{dfnbox}
In other words, two points are separated if each has an open neighbourhood which does not contain the other.
\begin{dfnbox}{$T_1$ Spaces}{T1}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{$T_1$}} if for any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$, there exists an open neighbourhood $U \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U$ but $y \notin U$. 
\end{dfnbox}
It is clear then that a $T_1$ space is a space in which every two distinct points are separated. Intuitively, if we fix any point $x$ in a $T_1$ space, then we can find some open sets to cover every point in the space other than $x$, which means that the isolated singleton $\left\{x\right\}$ is closed.
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of $T_1$ Spaces}{T1Space}
    A topological space $X$ is $T_1$ if and only if for all $x \in X$, the singleton set $\left\{x\right\}$ is closed.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $X$ is $T_1$ and take any $x \in X$. Let $y \in X \setminus \left\{x\right\}$ be any point. Notice that there exists some open set $V_y \subseteq X$ such that $y \in V_y$ but $x \notin V_y$. Clearly, 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \setminus \left\{x\right\} = \bigcup_{y \in X \setminus \left\{x\right\}}V_y
        \end{equation*}
        is open, and so $\left\{x\right\}$ is closed. Conversely, suppose that for all $x \in X$, the set $\left\{x\right\}$ is closed. For any $x \in X$, take any $y \in X$ with $x \neq y$. Notice that $y \in X \setminus \left\{x\right\}$, which is open. Therefore, $X$ is $T_1$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Since $T_1$ spaces guarantee pair-wise distinguishability, it is ``easy'' to preserve the structure of a $T_1$ space under transformations. In fact, recall that in Definition \ref{dfn:topologicalEmbedding}, we defined a structure-preserving map to embed a space into a different space. In a $T_1$ space, we will prove that the existence of such a map is guaranteed.
\begin{thmbox}{Embedding Theorem}{embedding}
    Let $X$ be a $T_1$ space. If $\left\{f_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ is a family of continuous functions from $X$ to $\R$ such that for any $x \in X$ and any open set $U \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$, there exists some $\alpha \in \Lambda$ with~$f_{\alpha}\left(x\right) > 0$ and $f_{\alpha}\left(X \setminus U\right) = \left\{0\right\}$, then the map $F \colon X \to \R^{\Lambda}$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \pi_{\alpha}\bigl(F\left(x\right)\bigr) = f_{\alpha}\left(x\right)
    \end{equation*}
    is a topological embedding.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:continuousProj}, $F$ is continuous because all of its component maps, the~$f_{\alpha}$'s, are continuous. Take any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$. Since $X$ is $T_1$, by Proposition \ref{pro:T1Space}, $X \setminus \left\{y\right\}$ is open and contains $x$. Therefore, there exists some $\alpha \in \Lambda$ with~$f_{\alpha}\left(x\right) > 0$ and $f_{\alpha}\left(y\right) = 0$ because $y \in X \setminus \left(X \setminus \left\{y\right\}\right)$. Therefore, $F\left(x\right) \neq F\left(y\right)$ and so $F$ is injective. In particular, this shows that $F$ is a continuous bijection onto~$F\left(X\right)$, so it suffices to further prove that $F$ is an open map. Take any open set $U \subseteq X$ and any $\mathbfit{z} \in F\left(U\right)$. Let $x \in U$ be such that $F\left(x\right) = \mathbfit{z}$. Note that there exists some $\alpha \in \Lambda$ such that $f_{\alpha}\left(x\right) > 0$ and $f\left(X \setminus U\right) = 0$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            V_{\mathbfit{z}} \coloneqq \pi_{\alpha}^{-1}\bigl(\left(0, \infty\right)\bigr) \subseteq \R^{\Lambda}, \qquad W_{\mathbfit{z}} = V_{\mathbfit{z}} \cap F\left(X\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $V_{\mathbfit{z}}$ is open, so $W_{\mathbfit{z}} \subseteq F\left(X\right)$ is open in $F\left(X\right)$. Consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{\alpha}\left(\mathbfit{z}\right) = f_{\alpha}\left(x\right) > 0,
        \end{equation*}
        so $\mathbfit{z} \in V_{\mathbfit{z}} \subseteq W_{\mathbfit{z}}$. For any $y \in X \setminus U$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{\alpha}\bigl(F\left(y\right)\bigr) = f_{\alpha}\left(y\right) = 0,
        \end{equation*}
        so $F\left(y\right) \in F\left(X\right) \setminus V_{\mathbfit{z}}$, which implies that $F\left(X \setminus U\right) \subseteq F\left(X\right) \setminus V_{\mathbfit{z}}$. Since $F$ is injective, 
        \begin{align*}
            F\left(X\right) \setminus F\left(U\right) & = F\left(X \setminus U\right) \\
            & \subseteq F\left(X\right) \setminus V_{\mathbfit{z}} \\
            & = F\left(X\right) \setminus W_{\mathbfit{z}}.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $W_{\mathbfit{z}} \subseteq F\left(U\right)$, which implies that 
        \begin{equation*}
            F\left(U\right) = \bigcup_{\mathbfit{z} \in F\left(U\right)}W_{\mathbfit{z}}
        \end{equation*}
        which is open in $F\left(X\right)$. Therefore, $F$ is a topological embedding.
    \end{proof}
\end{thmbox}
\section{Hausdorff Spaces}
$T_1$ separation can be strengthened by requiring that distinct points have disjoint open neighbourhoods.
\begin{dfnbox}{Hausdorff Space}{hausdorff}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{$T_2$}} or {\color{red} \textbf{Hausdorff}} if for any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$, there exist open neighbourhoods $U, V \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$ and $y \in V$ such that $U \cap V = \varnothing$.
\end{dfnbox}
It is easy to see that every Hausdorff space is $T_1$. We can check that every metric space is Hausdorff, as for any $x \neq y$, we can always take 
\begin{equation*}
    r \coloneqq \frac{1}{3}d\left(x, y\right),
\end{equation*}
and so the $r$-neighbourhoods of $x$ and $y$ are always disjoint. One can also easily check that the discrete topology of any set is Hausdorff. Hence, the following result is easy to show:
\begin{probox}{Finite Sets in Metric Spaces Are Closed}{finiteSetInMetric}
    Let $X$ be a metric space, then all finite sets in $X$ are closed.
\end{probox}
As another example, we prove that a finite co-finite topological space is always Hausdorff.
\begin{probox}{Separation in Co-finite Topology}{T1Cofinite}
    Let $X$ be a topological space with the co-finite topology $\mathcal{T}$, then $X$ is $T_1$. If $X$ is finite, then~$X$ is Hausdorff.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$. Clearly, $x \in X \setminus \left\{y\right\} \in \mathcal{T}$. Therefore, $X$ is $T_1$. If~$X$ is finite, then $X \setminus \left\{y\right\}$ is finite and so $\left\{y\right\} \in \mathcal{T}$. Therefore, $X$ is Hausdorff.
        \\\\
        We shall also prove that if $X$ is infinite, then $X$ is not Hausdorff. Take any~$x \in X$ and any $U \in \mathcal{T}$ with $x \in U$, then $X \setminus U$ is finite. For all $y \in X$ with $y \neq x$, we have~$y \in X \setminus U$. However, for all $V \subseteq X \setminus U$, we have $V \notin \mathcal{T}$ because $X \setminus V$ is infinite. Therefore, $X$ is not Hausdorff.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Since finite product preserves open sets, it is expected that finite product of Hausdorff spaces is still Hausdorff.
\begin{probox}{Finite Product Preserves Hausdorff Spaces}{finiteProductHausdorff}
    That the product of two Hausdorff spaces is Hausdorff.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ and $Y$ be Hausdorff spaces and take any $\left(x_1, y_1\right), \left(x_2, y_2\right) \in X \times Y$ such that $\left(x_1, y_1\right) \neq \left(x_2, y_2\right)$. Without loss of generality, assume $x_1 \neq x_2$, then there exists open sets $U_1, U_2 \subseteq X$ such that $U_1 \cap U_2 = \varnothing$, $x_1 \in U_1$ and $x_2 \in U_2$. Note that there exists some open sets $V_1, V_2 \subseteq Y$ such that $y_1 \in V_1$ and $y_2 \in V_2$, and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(x_1, y_1\right) \in U_1 \times V_1 \quad \textrm{and} \quad \left(x_2, y_2\right) \in U_2 \times V_2.
        \end{equation*}
        Observe that $U_1 \times V_1$ and $U_2 \times V_2$ both are open in $X \times Y$ and that
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(U_1 \times V_1\right) \cap \left(U_2 \times V_2\right) = \varnothing,
        \end{equation*}
        so $X \times Y$ is Hausdorff.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
An interesting characterisation of Hausdorff spaces makes use of a notion called ``diagonal'', which is an abstraction of the diagonal of polygons in geometry.
\begin{dfnbox}{Diagonal}{diag}
    Let $X$ be a topological space, then the {\color{red} \textbf{diagonal}} of $X$ is defined as 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Delta \coloneqq \left\{\left(x, x\right) \colon x \in X\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfnbox}
The characterisation states the following:
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Hausdorff Spaces with Closed Diagonals}{charHausdorffDiag}
    A topological space $X$ is Hausdorff if and only if its diagonal is closed in $X \times X$ with the product topology.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \Delta' \coloneqq X \times X \setminus \Delta = \left\{\left(x, y\right) \in X \times X \colon x \neq y\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Suppose that $X$ is Hausdorff, then for any $\left(x, y\right) \in \Delta'$, there exist open sets $U_x, U_y \subseteq X$ with $U_x \cap U_y = \varnothing$ such that $x \in U_x$ and $y \in U_y$. Therefore, $\left(x, y\right) \in U_x \times U_y$, which is open in $X \times X$. Notice that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \Delta' = \bigcup_{\left(x, y\right) \in \Delta'}U_x \times U_y,
        \end{equation*}
        so $\Delta'$ is open in $X \times X$. Therefore, $\Delta$ is closed. Conversely, suppose that $\Delta$ is closed, then $\Delta'$ is open. Take any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$, then $\left(x, y\right) \in \Delta'$ and so there exist some open sets $V_x, V_y \subseteq X$ such that $\left(x, y\right) \in V_x \times V_y \subseteq \Delta'$. We claim that $V_x \cap V_y = \varnothing$. Suppose on contrary that there exists some $z \in V_x \cap V_y$, then $\left(z, z\right) \in V_x \times V_y \subseteq \Delta'$ and so $z \neq z$, which is not possible. Therefore, $V_x$ and $V_y$ are disjoint open sets in $X$ such that $x \in V_x$ and $y \in V_y$, and so $X$ is Hausdorff.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Regular Spaces}
Suppose we fix a point $x$ in a $T_1$ space $X$ and use an open set $U \subseteq X$ to seclude $x$ from the rest of the space, then $X \setminus U$ is closed. In a more ideal situation, we may wish to aim for a secluding open set which is as small as possible. 
\begin{dfnbox}{Regular Space}{regular}
    A $T_1$ topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{regular}} or {\color{red} \textbf{$T_3$}} if for any $x \in X$ and any closed set $B \in X$ with $x \notin B$, there exists disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$ and $B \subseteq V$.
\end{dfnbox}
Notice that every regular space is $T_1$. Roughly speaking, if we seclude a point $x$ in a $T_1$ space~$X$ using an open set $U$, then the space is regular if we can ``shrink'' $U$ into a smaller open set $V$ and ``expand'' $X \setminus U$ in to a bigger set which is open.
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Regular Spaces}{regularSpace}
    A topological space $X$ is regular if and only if for any $x \in X$ and any open set $U \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$, there exists an open set $V \subseteq X$ with $x \in V$ such that $\widebar{V} \subseteq U$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $X$ is a regular space. Take any $x \in X$ with any open set $U \subseteq X$ containing $x$, then $B \coloneqq X \setminus U$ is a closed set not containing $x$. By Definition \ref{dfn:regular}, there exists disjoint open sets $V, W \subseteq X$ such that $x \in V$ and $B \subseteq W$. This means that 
        \begin{equation*}
            V \subseteq X \setminus W \subseteq X \setminus B = U.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $X \setminus W$ is closed, so $\widebar{V} \subseteq X \setminus W \subseteq U$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Normal Spaces}
