Note that in a regular space, we are able to separate every point from an arbitrary closed set. A strengthened version of this property is that we can also separate every pair of disjoint closed sets in the space. 
\begin{dfnbox}{Normal Space}{normal}
    A $T_1$ topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{normal}} or {\color{red} \textbf{$T_4$}} if for any disjoint closed sets $A, B \subseteq X$, there exists disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $A \subseteq U$ and $B \subseteq V$.
\end{dfnbox}
Notice that every normal space is $T_1$. In fact, a normal space is regular, a regular space is Hausdorff, and a Hausdorff space is $T_1$. 
\begin{probox}{Relationship between Separation Axioms}{relationshipSepAxioms}
    Every $T_4$ space is $T_3$, every $T_3$ space is $T_2$ and every $T_2$ space is $T_1$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a topological space. Suppose that $X$ is $T_4$. Note that $X$ is $T_1$, so~$\left\{x\right\}$ is closed for all $x \in X$ by Proposition \ref{pro:T1Space}. Take any $x \in X$ and any closed set~$B \subseteq X$ with $x \notin B$, then $\left\{x\right\}$ and $B$ are disjoint closed sets. Therefore, there exist disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $\left\{x\right\} \subseteq U$ and $B \subseteq V$ since $X$ is $T_4$. This means that~$x \in U$, which implies that $X$ is $T_3$.
        \\\\
        Suppose that $X$ is $T_3$, then $X$ is $T_1$. Take any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$, then by Proposition \ref{pro:T1Space}, $\left\{y\right\}$ is closed in $X$ and clearly $x \notin \left\{y\right\}$. Therefore, there exist disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U$ and $\left\{y\right\} \in V$. This means that $y \in V$ and so $X$ is~$T_2$.
        \\\\
        Suppose that $X$ is $T_2$, then for any $x, y \in X$ with $x \neq y$, there exist disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U$ and $y \in V$. Notice that $y \notin U$ and so $X$ is $T_1$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
We can characterise a normal space using the following result:
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Normal Spaces}{normalSpace}
    A topological space $X$ is normal if and only if for any closed set $A \subseteq X$ and any open set~$U \subseteq X$ with $A \subseteq U$, there exists some open set $V \subseteq X$ such that $A \subseteq V$ and $\widebar{V} \subseteq U$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $X$ is normal. Take any closed subset $A \subseteq X$ and any open set $U$ with $A \subseteq U$. Note that $B \coloneqq X \setminus U$ is closed and $A \cap B = \varnothing$, so there exists disjoint open sets $W_A, W_B \subseteq X$ such that $A \subseteq W_A$ and $B \subseteq W_B$. Since $W_A \cap W_B = \varnothing$, we have~$W_A \subseteq X \setminus W_B$. However, $X \setminus W_B$ is closed, and so $\widebar{W_A} \subseteq X \setminus W_B$. Take~$V \coloneqq W_A$, then $A \subseteq V$ and 
        \begin{equation*}
            \widebar{V} = \widebar{W_A} \subseteq X \setminus W_B \subseteq X \setminus B = U.
        \end{equation*}
        Conversely, suppose that for every closed $A \subseteq X$ and every open $U \subseteq X$ with $A \subseteq U$, there exists an open set $V \subseteq X$ with $A \subseteq V$ such that $\widebar{V} \subseteq U$. Let $A, B \subseteq X$ be any disjoint closed sets with $A \cap B = \varnothing$ and take $U \coloneqq X \setminus B$, then there exists some open set $V \subseteq X$ with $A \subseteq V$ and $\widebar{V} \subseteq U$. Take $W \coloneqq X \setminus \widebar{V}$ which is open, then $B \subseteq W$ because $B \cap \widebar{V} \subseteq B \cap U = \varnothing$. Therefore, $X$ is normal.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Recall that we have argued that every metric space is Hausdorff using a simple argument. In fact, we can directly prove a much stronger result that every metric space is normal.
\begin{probox}{Every Metrisable Space Is Normal}{metricSpaceIsNormal}
    Every metrisable space is normal.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\left(X, d\right)$ be a metric space and $A, B \subseteq X$ be disjoint closed sets in the metrisable topology. For each $a \in A$, there exists some $\epsilon_a > 0$ such that $B_{\epsilon_a}\left(a\right) \subseteq A$. Similarly, for each $b \in B$, there exists some $\epsilon_b > 0$ such that $B_{\epsilon_b}\left(b\right) \subseteq B$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \coloneqq \bigcup_{a \in A}B_{\epsilon_a}\left(a\right), \qquad V \coloneqq \bigcup_{b \in B}B_{\epsilon_b}\left(b\right),
        \end{equation*}
        then $A \subseteq U$ and $B \subseteq V$, where both $U$ and $V$ are open. We claim that $U \cap V = \varnothing$. Suppose on contrary that there exists some $x \in U \cap V$, then there exists some $\alpha \in A$ and $\beta \in B$ such that $x \in B_{\epsilon_{\alpha}}\left(\alpha\right) \cap B_{\epsilon_{\beta}}\left(\beta\right) \subseteq A \cap B = \varnothing$, which is a contradiction. Therefore, $X$ is normal.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Notice that we get regularity from a normal space for free. Now in the converse direction, we wish to investigate what is the condition which will lead to normality from a regular space.
\begin{probox}{Second Countable Regular Spaces Are Normal}{regWithCountableBasis}
    Every second countable regular space is normal.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a second countable regular space and $A, B \subseteq X$ be disjoint closed sets. By Definition \ref{dfn:regular}, for every $a \in A$, there exists some open set $U_a \subseteq X$ with $a \in U_a$ but $B \cap U_a = \varnothing$. By Proposition \ref{pro:regularSpace}, there exists some open set $V_a \subseteq X$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            a \in V_a \subseteq \widebar{V_a} \subseteq U_a
        \end{equation*}
        for all $a \in A$. Fix a countable basis $\mathcal{B}$ for $X$, then there exists some $B_a \in \mathcal{B}$ such that~$a \in B_a \subseteq V_a$ for all $a \in A$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{U}_A \coloneqq \left\{B_a \in \mathcal{B} \colon a \in A\right\} \subseteq \mathcal{B},
        \end{equation*}
        then $\mathcal{U}_A$ is a countable open cover of $A$, where $B \cap \widebar{B_a} = \varnothing$ for all $B_a \in \mathcal{U}_A$. Similarly, we can construct a countable open cover $\mathcal{U}_B \subseteq \mathcal{B}$ for $B$ such that $A \cap \widebar{B_b} = \varnothing$ for all~$B_b \in \mathcal{U}_B$. Re-write 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{U}_A \coloneqq \left\{A_i \colon i \in I\right\}, \qquad \mathcal{U}_B \coloneqq \left\{B_j \colon j \in J\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        where $I, J \subseteq \N^+$. Without loss of generality, assume $I \subseteq J$. For each $i \in I$, define 
        \begin{equation*}
            A_i' \coloneqq A_i \setminus \bigcup_{j \leq i}\widebar{B_j}
        \end{equation*}
        and for each $j \in J$, define 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_j' \coloneqq B_j \setminus \bigcup_{i \leq j}\widebar{A_j}.
        \end{equation*}
        Consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            U_A \coloneqq \bigcup_{i \in I}A_i', \qquad U_B \coloneqq \bigcup_{j \in J}B_j',
        \end{equation*}
        which are both open. Notice that $\widebar{B_i} \cap A \neq \varnothing$ for all $i \in I$, so $A_i \cap A = A_i' \cap A$. Therefore,
        \begin{align*}
            A & = \bigcup_{i \in I}\left(A_i \cap A\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I}\left(A_i' \cap A\right) \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in I}A_i' = U_A.
        \end{align*}
        Similarly, $B \subseteq U_B$. We claim that $U_A \cap U_B = \varnothing$. Suppose on contrary that there exists some $x \in U_A \cap U_B$, then there exists some $i \in I$ and $j \in J$ such that $x \in A_i' \cap B_j'$. Without loss of generality, assume that $i \leq j$. This means that $B_j' \cap A_i = \varnothing$ but $A_i' \subseteq A_i$, which is not possible. Therefore, $X$ is normal.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Separation by Continuous Functions}
Through a different perspective, if two sets are distinguishable, we can ``move'' from one set to another via a series of continuous transformations. This motivates us to define separation using continuous functions.
\begin{dfnbox}{Separation by a Continuous Function}{separationByCont}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. For any $A, B \subseteq X$, they are {\color{red} \textbf{separated by a continuous function}} if there exists a continuous function $f \colon X \to \left[0, 1\right]$ such that $f\left(A\right) = \left\{0\right\}$ and $f\left(B\right) = \left\{1\right\}$.
\end{dfnbox}
We see that separation by continuous functions offers a quantitative description for disjointness. With this, we can define the following characterisations:
\begin{dfnbox}{Completely Regular Space}{completelyRegular}
    A $T_1$ topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{completely regular}} if for every $x \in X$ and every closed set $A \subseteq X$ with $x \notin A$, $\left\{x\right\}$ and $A$ are separated by a continuous function.
\end{dfnbox}
Similarly, we can define \textit{completely normal spaces}:
\begin{dfnbox}{Completely Normal Space}{completelyNormal}
    A $T_1$ topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{completely normal}} if any disjoint closed sets $A, B \subseteq X$ are separated by a continuous function.
\end{dfnbox}
As the names suggest, we expect the following to be true:
\begin{probox}{Complete Regularity and Complete Normality}{completeRegAndNorm}
    Every completely regular space is regular and every completely normal space is normal.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a completely regular space. Take any $x \in X$ and any closed set $A \subseteq X$ with $x \notin A$, then there exists a continuous function $f \colon X \to \left[0, 1\right]$ such that $f\left(x\right) = 0$ and $f\left(A\right) = \left\{1\right\}$. Clearly, 
        \begin{equation*}
            A \subseteq f^{-1}\biggl(\left(\frac{1}{2}, 1\right]\biggr), \qquad x \in f^{-1}\biggl(\left[0, \frac{1}{2}\right)\biggr),
        \end{equation*}
        where both pre-images are open in $X$. It is obvious that they are disjoint because otherwise there exists some $x \in X$ such that $f\left(x\right) > \frac{1}{2}$ and $f\left(x\right) < \frac{1}{2}$, which is ridiculous. The case where $X$ is completely normal is similar and is left to the reader as an exercise.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
A truly marvellous aspect of normal spaces is that their normality is ``always complete''.
\begin{lembox}{Urysohn's Lemma}{urysohnLemma}
    Every normal space is completely normal.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        For each $n \in \N^+$, let $Q_n$ be an increasing sequence in $\Q \cap \left[0, 1\right]$ such that for every $x \in Q_n$, we have $x = \frac{y}{n}$ where $y$ and $n$ are co-prime. Define a sequence
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{r_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+} \coloneqq \left\{1, 0\right\} \cup \bigcup_{n = 2}^{\infty}Q_n.
        \end{equation*}
        For every $n \in \N^+$, let $P_n \coloneqq \left\{r_1, r_2, \cdots, r_n\right\}$. Take any disjoint closed sets $A, B \subseteq X$ and define $U_1 \coloneqq X \setminus B$ which is an open set containing $A$. Since $X$ is normal, by Proposition \ref{pro:normalSpace}, there exists some open set $U_0 \subseteq X$ such that $A \subseteq U_0 \subseteq \widebar{U_0} \subseteq U_1$. Suppose that there exists some integer $n \geq 2$ such that for all $p, q \in P_n$ with $p < q$, we have $\widebar{U_p} \subseteq U_q$. Consider $\left\{r_{n + 1}\right\} = P_{n + 1} \setminus P_{n}$ and define 
        \begin{equation*}
            r' \coloneqq \max\left\{r \in P_n \colon r < r_{n + 1}\right\}, \qquad r'' \coloneqq \min\left\{r \in P_n \colon r > r_{n + 1}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Notice that $r_{n + 1} \neq 0, 1$ and so both $r'$ and $r''$ are well-defined. Since $r' < r''$, we have~$\widebar{U}_{r'} \subseteq U_{r''}$. Since $X$ is normal, by Proposition \ref{pro:normalSpace}, there exists some open set~$U_r \subseteq X$ such that $\widebar{U}_{r'} \subseteq U_r \subseteq \widebar{U_r} \subseteq U_{r''}$. Therefore, for all $p, q \in P_{n + 1}$, we have~$\widebar{U_p} \subseteq U_q$ whenever $p < q$. Define $U_p = \varnothing$ for all $p < 0$ and $U_p = X$ for all $p > 1$, then by mathematical induction, there exists a family of open sets $\left\{U_p\right\}_{p \in \Q}$ in $X$ such that $\widebar{U_p} \subseteq U_q$ whenever $p < q$, $B \cap U_1 = \varnothing$ and~$A \subseteq U_0$. For every $x \in X$, define 
        \begin{equation*}
            A_x \coloneqq \left\{p \in Q \colon x \in U_p\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then $\left(1, \infty\right) \subseteq A_x \subseteq \left[0, \infty\right)$. This means that $\inf A_x \in \left[0, 1\right]$ for all $x \in X$. Define a map $f \colon X \to \left[0, 1\right]$ by $f\left(x\right) = \inf A_x$. We claim that $f\left(x\right) \leq p$ for all $x \in \widebar{U_p}$ and that~$f\left(x\right) \geq p$ for all $x \notin U_p$. Suppose that $x \in \widebar{U_p}$, then for all $q \in \Q \cap \left(p, \infty\right)$, we have $x \in U_q$ and so $q \in A_x$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(x\right) = \inf A_x \leq \inf\left\{q \in \Q \colon q > p\right\} = p.
        \end{equation*}
        On the other hand, if $x \notin U_p$, then for every $q < p$, we have $x \notin U_q$ and so $q \notin A_x$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(x\right) = \inf A_x \geq \inf \left\{q \in \Q \colon q > p\right\} = p.
        \end{equation*}
        Now, for any $x \in A$, we have $x \in U_0$ and so $f\left(x\right) \leq 0$. Since $f\left(x\right) \in \left[0, 1\right]$, this means that $f\left(A\right) = \left\{0\right\}$. Similarly, we have $f\left(B\right) = \left\{1\right\}$. Let $\left(a, b\right) \in \R$ be any open interval. If for all $x \in X$, we have $f\left(x\right) \notin \left(a, b\right)$, then $f^{-1}\bigl(\left(a, b\right)\bigr) = \varnothing$ which is open. Otherwise, for every $x \in X$ with $f\left(x\right) \in \left(a, b\right)$, there exists $a', b' \in \Q$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            a < a' < f\left(x\right) < b' < b.
        \end{equation*}
        Let $U_x \coloneqq U_{b'} \setminus \widebar{U_{a'}} \neq \varnothing$, which is open in $X$. For every $y \in U_x$, we have~$y \in U_{b'} \subseteq \widebar{U_{b'}}$ and $y \notin U_{a'}$. Therefore, $a' \leq f\left(y\right) \leq b'$ and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(U_x\right) \subseteq \left[a', b'\right] \subseteq \left(a, b\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f^{-1}\bigl(\left(a, b\right)\bigr) = \bigcup_{x \in f^{-1}\bigl(\left(a, b\right)\bigr)}U_x,
        \end{equation*}
        which is open in $X$. Therefore, $f$ is continuous and so $X$ is completely normal.
    \end{proof}
\end{lembox}
Recall that in Proposition \ref{pro:metricSpaceIsNormal}, we proved that every metrisable space is normal. This in turn demonstrates how metrisability is a very good property in topological spaces. 
\begin{thmbox}{Urysohn's Metrisation Theorem}{urysohnMetrisation}
    Every second countable regular topological space is metrisable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:regWithCountableBasis}, $X$ is normal and so by Lemma \ref{lem:urysohnLemma}, $X$ is completely normal. Fix $\mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_i \colon i \in \N^+\right\}$ as a countable basis for $X$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            \Lambda \coloneqq \left\{\left(i, j\right) \in \Z^+ \times \Z^+ \colon \widebar{B_i} \subseteq B_j\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        which is countable. For each $\left(i, j\right) \in \Lambda$, notice that $\widebar{B_i}$ and $X \setminus B_j$ are disjoint closed sets, and so by Lemma \ref{lem:urysohnLemma}, there exists a map $f_{ij} \colon X \to \left[0, 1\right]$ such that $f\left(\widebar{B_i}\right) = \left\{1\right\}$ and $f\left(X \setminus B_j\right) = \left\{0\right\}$. Take any $x \in X$ and any open set $U \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$. Note that there exists some $B_j \in \mathcal{B}$ is such that $x \in B_j \subseteq U$. Since $X$ is regular, by Proposition \ref{pro:regularSpace}, there exists some open set $V \subseteq X$ such that $x \in V \subseteq \widebar{V} \subseteq B_j$. Note that there exists some $B_i \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $x \in B_i \subseteq V \subseteq \widebar{B_j}$ and so $\left(i, j\right) \in \Lambda$. Since $x \in B_i$, we have $f_{ij}\left(x\right) = 1$ and $f_{ij}\left(X \setminus U\right) = \left\{0\right\}$ because $X \setminus U \subseteq X \setminus B_j$. By Theorem \ref{thm:embedding}, the map $F \colon X \to \R^{\omega}$ defined by 
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{ij}\bigl(F\left(x\right)\bigr) = f_{ij}\left(x\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is a topological embedding and so it is homeomorphic onto $F\left(X\right) \subseteq \R^{\omega}$. Observe that $F\left(X\right)$ is metrisable. Let $d_F$ be the metric on $F\left(X\right)$, then $d \colon X \times X \to \R$ defined by 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(x, y\right) \coloneqq d_F\bigl(F\left(x\right), F\left(y\right)\bigr)
        \end{equation*}
        is a metric on $X$. Therefore, $X$ is metrisable.
    \end{proof}
\end{thmbox}

\chapter{Compactness}
\section{Compact Spaces}
We next introduce the notion of compact spaces, which are an attempt to generalise the notion of \textbf{closed and bounded} subsets in Euclidean spaces. The idea here is to define compactness as ``having no missing end point''.

The intuition here is that if a space is not compact, i.e., it can extend indefinitely without an end, then there is a way to partition the space into infinitely many open subsets. On the other hand, if the space has a clearly defined boundary, then every possible partition must be finite.
\begin{dfnbox}{Compact Space}{compact}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{compact}} if every open cover of $X$ contains a finite sub-cover for $X$.
\end{dfnbox}
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        $Y \subseteq X$ is a compact subspace if and only if for every collection $\mathcal{U}$ of open sets in $Y$ such that $Y \subseteq \bigcup_{U \in \mathcal{U}}U$, there exists a finite sub-collection $\mathcal{U}' \subseteq \mathcal{U}$ such that~$Y \subseteq \bigcup_{U \in \mathcal{U}'}U$.
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
Although compactness is motivated by the closed and bounded subsets in Euclidean spaces, we will see that in general, a closed and bounded subset in a topological space might not be compact.

Consider $\Q$ with the standard topology and $Q \coloneqq \left[-\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{2}\right] \cap \Q$, which is closed and bounded. We claim that the subset is not compact.

Consider $\left\{\left(-\sqrt{2} + \frac{1}{n}, \sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{n}\right) \colon n \in \N^+\right\}$. We claim that it is an open cover for $Q$. Take any $q \in Q$ and let 
\begin{equation*}
    \delta \coloneqq \min\left\{\sqrt{2} - q, q + \sqrt{2}\right\} > 0.
\end{equation*}
Clearly, there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that $\frac{1}{N} < \delta$, and so $q \in \left(-\sqrt{2} + \frac{1}{N}, \sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{N}\right)$. Therefore, 
\begin{equation*}
    Q \subseteq \bigcup_{n \in \N^+}\left(-\sqrt{2} + \frac{1}{n}, \sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{n}\right).
\end{equation*} 
Let $S \subseteq \N^+$ be any finite subset and let $M \coloneqq \max S$, then 
\begin{equation*}
    \bigcup_{n \in S}\left(-\sqrt{2} + \frac{1}{n}, \sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{n}\right) = \left(-\sqrt{2} + \frac{1}{M}, \sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{M}\right).
\end{equation*}
However, there exists some rational number $q \in \left[\sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{M}, \sqrt{2}\right]$, which means that 
\begin{equation*}
    \left\{\left(-\sqrt{2} + \frac{1}{n}, \sqrt{2} - \frac{1}{n}\right) \colon n \in S\right\}
\end{equation*}
is not a sub-cover for any finite $S \subseteq \N^+$. Therefore, $Q$ is not compact.

Note that previously we have shown that a closed set contains all of its limit points. Therefore, it is intuitive that if a closed set is contained by a compact space, this closed set should be compact as well.
\begin{probox}{Closed Subspaces of Compact Spaces Are Compact}{closedCompact}
    Every closed subspace of a compact space is compact.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a compact topological space and $Y \subseteq X$ be a closed subspace. Let~$\left\{U_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be an open cover of $Y$ with $U_{\alpha} \subseteq X$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$, then $\left\{U_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda} \cup \left\{X \setminus Y\right\}$ is an open cover for $X$ because $Y$ is closed. Since $X$ is compact, we can find a finite sub-cover $\mathcal{U} \subseteq \left\{U_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda} \cup \left\{X \setminus Y\right\}$. Clearly, $\mathcal{U} \setminus \left\{X \setminus Y\right\} \subseteq \left\{U_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ is a finite sub-cover for $Y$. Therefore, $Y$ is compact. 
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
One may be tempted to think that compact subspaces must be closed. However, this is not true in general. Consider $X$ to be an infinite topological space equipped with the co-finite topology. For any $Y \subseteq X$, let $\mathcal{U}$ be an open cover for $Y$. Take any $U \in \mathcal{U}$, then clearly $Y \setminus U$ is finite. For each $y \in Y \setminus U$, there exists some $U_y \in \mathcal{U}$ such that $y \in U_y$. Therefore, 
\begin{equation*}
    \left\{U\right\} \cup \left\{U_y \colon y \in Y \setminus U\right\}
\end{equation*}
is a finite sub-cover for $Y$. Therefore, every subset of $X$ is compact. However, $Y \subseteq X$ is closed if and only if $Y$ is finite.
\begin{probox}{Sets in Co-finite Topology Are Compact}{compactCofinite}
    Let $X$ be a topological space equipped with the co-finite topology, then every $Y \subsetneq X$ is compact and it is closed if and only if it is finite.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any $Y \subsetneq X$ and let $\mathcal{U}$ be an open cover for $Y$. Fix some $U \in \mathcal{U}$, since $U$ is open in $X$ with respect to the co-finite topology, $X \setminus U$ is finite. Note that $Y \setminus U \subseteq X \setminus U$, so $Y \setminus U$ is finite. For each $y \in Y \setminus U$, there exists some $U_y \in \mathcal{U}$ such that $y \in U_y$, otherwise $\mathcal{U}$ does not cover $Y$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{U\right\} \cup \left\{U_y \colon y \in Y \setminus U\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        is a finite sub-cover for $Y$. Therefore, every subset of $X$ is compact. If $Y$ is finite, then $X \setminus Y$ is open by definition of the co-finite topology, so $Y$ is closed. Conversely, suppose $Y \subsetneq X$ is closed, then $X \setminus Y$ is an non-empty open set, and so $Y$ is finite.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
In fact, even the closure of a compact subspace may not be compact. A classic counter example makes use of the \textit{particular point topology}.
\begin{dfnbox}{Particular Point Topology}{particularPoint}
    For any set $X$, the {\color{red} \textbf{particular point topology}} on $X$ with respect to some $p \in X$ is defined as 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{T}_p \coloneqq \left\{U \subseteq X \colon p \in U\right\} \cup \left\{\varnothing\right\}.
    \end{equation*}    
\end{dfnbox}
Fix some $n \in \N$ and define $\mathcal{T}_n$ to be the particular topology on $\N$ with respect to $n$. Note that for any open cover $\mathcal{U}$ for $\left\{n\right\}$, we can take any $U_0 \in \mathcal{U}$ to form a finite sub-cover $\left\{U_0\right\}$ because $n \in U_0$. Therefore, $\left\{n\right\}$ is compact. 

We claim that $\widebar{\left\{n\right\}} = \N$ because if on contrary $\widebar{\left\{n\right\}} = \N \setminus X$ for some non-empty $X \in \mathcal{T}_n$, then $n \notin X$ which is not possible. Now consider 
\begin{equation*}
    \bigl\{\left\{m, n\right\} \colon m \in \N, m \neq n\bigr\},
\end{equation*}
which is an open cover for $\widebar{\left\{n\right\}}$ but clearly it does not contain a finite sub-cover. Therefore,~$\widebar{\left\{n\right\}}$ is not compact.

To conclude closed-ness from compactness, we require some additional assumptions.
\begin{probox}{Compact Subspaces of Hausdorff Spaces Are Closed}{compactClosed}
    Every compact subspace of a Hausdorff space is closed.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a Hausdorff space and $Y \subseteq X$ be a compact subspace. Let~$Z \coloneqq X \setminus Y$. Fix any $z \in Z$, then for each $y \in Y$, there exists disjoint open sets $U_y, V_y \subseteq X$ such that $z \in U_y$ and $y \in V_y$. Note that $\left\{V_y \colon y \in Y\right\}$ is an open cover for $Y$, so there exists some finite subset $F \subseteq Y$ such that $\left\{V_y \colon y \in F\right\}$ is a finite sub-cover for $Y$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            U_z \coloneqq \bigcap_{y \in F}U_y,
        \end{equation*}
        then $U_z \cap V_y = \varnothing$ for all $y \in F$, and so $U_z \cap Y = \varnothing$. Note that $U_z$ is an open neighbourhood of $z$, so
        \begin{equation*}
            Z = \bigcup_{z \in Z}U_z
        \end{equation*}
        is open in $X$. Therefore, $Y = X \setminus Z$ is closed in $X$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
A compact space possesses many nice properties, one of which is with regard to the separability of the space. Recall that a normal space is regular and a regular space is Hausdorff, but to go in the converse direction is not so simple. However, for compact spaces, the converse direction will hold.
\begin{probox}{Separability of Compact Spaces}{separabilityCompact}
    If $X$ is a compact space, then $X$ is $T_4$ if and only if it is $T_3$, and it is $T_3$ if and only if it is Hausdorff.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:relationshipSepAxioms}, every $T_4$ space is $T_3$ and every $T_3$ space is Hausdorff, so it suffices to prove that $X$ being Hausdorff implies that $X$ is $T_3$ and $X$ being $T_3$ implies that $X$ is $T_4$. Suppose $X$ is Hausdorff. Take any $x \in X$ and any closed set~$B \subseteq X$ with~$x \notin B$. Note that for every $b \in B$, there exists disjoint open sets~$U_b, V_b \subseteq X$ such that $b \in U_b$ and $x \in V_b$. Note that $\left\{U_b \colon b \in B\right\}$ is an open cover of $B$. Since~$B$ is closed and $X$ is compact, by Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, $B$ is compact and so there exists a finite subset $A \subseteq B$ such that $\left\{U_a \colon a \in A\right\}$ is a finite sub-cover of $B$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            V \coloneqq \bigcap_{a \in A} V_a, \qquad U \coloneqq \bigcup_{a \in A} U_a,
        \end{equation*}
        then both $U$ and $V$ are open in $X$ and $U \cap V = \varnothing$. Note that $x \in V$ and $B \subseteq U$, so~$X$ is $T_3$. Next, suppose that $X$ is $T_3$ and take any disjoint closed sets $A, B \subseteq X$. Since $X$ is compact, by Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, both $A$ and $B$ are compact. For each $a \in A$, since $X$ is~$T_3$, there exists disjoint open sets $U_a, V_a \subseteq X$ such that $a \in U_a$ and $B \subseteq V_a$. Note that $\left\{U_a \colon a \in A\right\}$ is an open cover of $A$, and so there exists some finite subset $C \subseteq A$ such that $\left\{U_c \colon c \in C\right\}$ is a finite sub-cover of $A$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \coloneqq \bigcup_{c \in C} U_c, \qquad V \coloneqq \bigcap_{c \in C} V_c,
        \end{equation*}
        then both $U$ and $V$ are open in $X$ and $U \cap V = \varnothing$. Note that $A \subseteq U$ and $B \subseteq V$, so $X$ is $T_4$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Intuitively, a continuous map should send a compact set to a compact image because open sets, and hence open covers, are preserved by the map.
\begin{probox}{Continuous Map Induces Compact Image}{ContinuousCompact}
    Let $f \colon X \to Y$ be continuous, if $X$ is compact, then $f\left(X\right)$ is compact.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\left\{U_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be an open cover for $f\left(X\right)$. Notice that for every $x \in X$, we have~$f\left(x\right) \in U_{\lambda}$ for some $\lambda \in \Lambda$ and so $x \in f^{-1}\left(U_{\lambda}\right)$ which is open in $X$ because $f$ is continuous. Therefore, $\left\{f^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}\right)\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ is an open cover for $X$. Since $X$ is compact, there exists some finite $I \subseteq \Lambda$ such that $\left\{f^{-1}\left(U_i\right)\right\}_{i \in I}$ is a finite sub-cover for $X$. For any $y \in f\left(X\right)$, there exists some $x_y \in X$ such that $f\left(x_y\right) = y$. Notice that there exists some $j \in I$ such that $x_y \in f^{-1}\left(U_j\right)$, so $y \in U_j$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(X\right) \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in I}U_i
        \end{equation*}
        and so $f\left(X\right)$ is compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Next, we consider the following observation: if $X \coloneqq \left(x_1, x_2\right)$ and $Y \coloneqq \left[y_1, y_2\right]$ are intervals in $\R$, let $N \coloneqq X \times Y$ be a rectangular region in $\R^2$. It is easy to see that if $N$ contains a line segment $\bigl(\left(x_0, y_1\right), \left(x_0, y_2\right)\bigr)$ for some $x_0 \in X$, then $N$ contains a ``tube'' 
\begin{equation*}
    T \coloneqq \left(x_0 - \delta, x_0 + \delta\right) \times \left[y_1, y_2\right]
\end{equation*}
for some $\delta > 0$. This is generalised to the following lemma:
\begin{lembox}{Tube Lemma}{tube}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $Y$ be a compact topological space. If $N \subseteq X \times Y$ is an open set that contains $\left\{\left(x_0, y\right) \colon y \in Y \right\}$, then $N$ contains $W \times Y$ for some open $W \subseteq X$ that contains $x_0$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{B}$ be the basis generating the product topology. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{A} \coloneqq \left\{U \times V \in \mathcal{B} \colon U \times V \subseteq N, x_0 \in U\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Since $N$ is open, for any $y \in Y$, there exists some $U_y \times V_y \in \mathcal{B}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(x_0, y\right) \in U_y \times V_y \subseteq N.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, $\mathcal{A}$ is an open cover for $\left\{x_0\right\} \times Y$. Note that $\left\{x_0\right\} \times Y$ is compact because of the compactness of $Y$. Therefore, there exists a finite sub-cover
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{U_1 \times V_1, \cdots, U_n \times V_n\right\} \subseteq \mathcal{A}
        \end{equation*}
        of $\left\{x_0\right\} \times Y$ where $\bigcup_{i = 1}^nV_i = Y$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            W \coloneqq \bigcap_{i = 1}^nU_i \subseteq X,
        \end{equation*}
        then $W$ is open and $x_0 \in W$. Since $W \subseteq U_i$ for all $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            W \times Y = \bigcup_{i = 1}^n\left(W \times V_i\right) \subseteq \bigcup_{i = 1}^n\left(U_i \times V_i\right) \subseteq N.
        \end{equation*}
    \end{proof}
\end{lembox}
The compactness of $Y$ is crucial for the Tube lemma to hold. Consider 
\begin{equation*}
    S \coloneqq \left\{\left(x, y\right) \in \R^2 \colon \abs{x} < \frac{1}{y^2 + 1}\right\} \subseteq \R^2,
\end{equation*}
which is open and contains $\left\{0\right\} \times \R$, but does not contain a tube.

Applying the Tube Lemma to Cartesian products yields the following result:
\begin{corbox}{Compactness of Cartesian Product}{compactCartProd}
    If $X$ and $Y$ are compact topological spaces, then $X \times Y$ is compact.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an open cover of $X \times Y$, then $\mathcal{A}$ is also an open cover of $\left\{x\right\} \times Y$ for any~$x \in X$. Since $Y$ is compact, there exists some finite subset $\mathcal{A}_x \subseteq \mathcal{A}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{x\right\} \times Y \subseteq \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{A}_x}A \coloneqq N_x.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $N_x$ is open in $X \times Y$. By Lemma \ref{lem:tube}, there exists some open subset $W_x \subseteq X$ such that $x \in W_x$ and $W_x \times Y \subseteq N_x$. Clearly, $\left\{W_x \colon x \in X\right\}$ is an open cover for $X$. Since $X$ is compact, this means that there exists $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n \in X$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            X = \bigcup_{i = 1}^nW_{x_i}.
        \end{equation*}
        Take $\mathcal{A}' \coloneqq \bigcup_{i = 1}^n\mathcal{A}_{x_i} \subseteq \mathcal{A}$ which is finite, then 
        \begin{align*}
            X \times Y & \subseteq \bigcup_{i = 1}^n\left(W_{x_i} \times Y\right) \\
            & \subseteq \bigcup_{i = 1}^nN_{x_i} \\
            & = \bigcup_{i = 1}^n\bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{A}_{x_i}}A \\
            & = \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{A}'}A.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $\mathcal{A}'$ is a finite sub-cover for $X \times Y$ and so $X \times Y$ is compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{corbox}
Compactness is also related to the notion of a \textit{graph} of a function. We will first define this concept in topological terms.
\begin{dfnbox}{Graph of A Function}{graph}
    For any function $f \colon X \to Y$, the set 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Gamma_f = \left\{ \bigl(x, f(x)\bigr) \colon x \in X \right\} \subseteq X \times Y
    \end{equation*}
    is called the {\color{red} \textbf{graph}} of $f$.
\end{dfnbox}
Recall that in elementary real analysis, we have defined continuity at ``end points'' by the existence of one-side limits. This translates to the closure of the function's graph, and in fact is closely related to the compactness of the range.
\begin{probox}{Closure of Graphs of Functions}{clFuncGraph}
    Let $f \colon X \to Y$ be a function. If $X$ is any space and $Y$ is a compact Hausdorff space, then $\Gamma_f$ is closed if and only if $f$ is continuous.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $\Gamma_f$ is closed. Let $V \subseteq Y$ be a closed set, then $X \times V$ is closed in~$X \times Y$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            G \coloneqq \Gamma_f \cap \left(X \times V\right) = f^{-1}\left(V\right) \times f\left(f^{-1}\left(V\right)\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $G$ is closed in $X \times Y$. Take any $x \in X \setminus \pi_X\left(G\right)$, then $\left(x, y\right) \in \left(X \times Y\right) \setminus G$ for all $y \in Y$. Note that $\left(X \times Y\right) \setminus G$ is open and $Y$ is compact, so by Lemma \ref{lem:tube}, there exists some open set $W_x \subseteq X$ with $x \in W_x$ such that $W_x \times Y \subseteq \left(X \times Y\right) \setminus G$. Therefore,~$W_x \subseteq X \setminus \pi_X\left(G\right)$ and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \setminus \pi_X\left(G\right) = \bigcup_{x \in X \setminus \pi_X\left(G\right)}W_x,
        \end{equation*}
        which is open. Therefore, $\pi_X\left(G\right) = f^{-1}\left(V\right)$ is closed in $X$. Therefore, by Proposition \ref{pro:continuousMapProps}, $f$ is continuous. Conversely, suppose that $f$ is continuous, then it suffices to prove that $\Gamma_f = \overline{\Gamma_f}$. Suppose on contrary that $\Gamma_f \neq \overline{\Gamma_f}$. Take
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(x, y\right) \in \overline{\Gamma_f} \setminus \Gamma_f \subseteq X \times Y
        \end{equation*}
        such that $y \neq f\left(x\right)$. Since $Y$ is Hausdorff, there exists disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq Y$ such that $y \in U$ and $f\left(x\right) \in V$. Let $W \coloneqq f^{-1}\left(V\right) \neq \varnothing$, which is open in $X$, and so~$W \times U$ is an open neighbourhood of $\left(x, y\right)$. Note that $\left(x, y\right)$ is a limit point of $\Gamma_f$, so
        \begin{equation*}
            \Gamma_f \cap \left(W \times U \setminus \left\{\left(x, y\right)\right\}\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(x', y'\right) \in \Gamma_f \cap \bigl(W \times U \setminus \left\{\left(x, y\right)\right\}\bigr),
        \end{equation*}
        then $y' = f\left(x'\right) \in U$. However, $f\left(x'\right) \in f\left(W\right) \subseteq V$, so $U \cap V \neq \varnothing$, which is a contradiction. Therefore, $\Gamma_f$ must be closed.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
At another perspective, we can re-formulate the definition of compactness using closed sets. This require us to use something known as the \textit{finite intersection property}.
\begin{dfnbox}{Finite Intersection Property}{finiteIntersect}
    Let $X$ be some set. A collection $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ has the {\color{red} \textbf{finite intersection property}} if for any finite sub-collection $\mathcal{G}' \subseteq \mathcal{G}$, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcap_{G \in \mathcal{G}'}G \neq \varnothing.
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfnbox}
The following result characterises compactness using the finite intersection property:
\begin{probox}{Equivalent Definition of Compactness}{compactFiniteIntersect}
    Let $X$ be a topological space, then $X$ is compact if and only if for any collection of closed sets $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ with the finite intersection property, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcap_{G \in \mathcal{G}}G \neq \varnothing.
    \end{equation*}
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that for any collection $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ of closed sets in $X$ with the finite intersection property, we have $\bigcap_{G \in \mathcal{G}}G \neq \varnothing$. Let $\mathcal{U}$ be an open cover of $X$ and consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{G} \coloneqq \left\{X \setminus U \colon U \in \mathcal{U}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $\mathcal{G}$ is a collection of closed sets and 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcap_{G \in \mathcal{G}}G = \bigcap_{U \in \mathcal{U}}X \setminus U = X \setminus \bigcup_{U \in \mathcal{U}}U = \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        This implies that $\mathcal{G}$ does not have the finite intersection property, i.e., there exists open sets $U_1, U_2, \cdots, U_n \in \mathcal{U}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcap_{i = 1}^nX \setminus U_i = X \setminus \bigcup_{i = 1}^nU_i = \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        This means that $\left\{U_1, U_2, \cdots, U_n\right\} \subseteq \mathcal{U}$ is a finite sub-cover for $X$ and so $X$ is compact. Conversely suppose $X$ is compact and let $\mathcal{G}$ be a collection of closed sets in $X$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcap_{G \in \mathcal{G}}G = \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{U} \coloneqq \left\{X \setminus G \colon G \in \mathcal{G}\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then clearly 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcup_{U \in \mathcal{U}}U = X \setminus \bigcap_{G \in \mathcal{G}}G = X,
        \end{equation*}
        so $\mathcal{U}$ is an open cover of $X$. Since $X$ is compact, there exists $G_1, G_2, \cdots, G_n \in \mathcal{G}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcup_{i = 1}^nX \setminus G_i = X \setminus \bigcap_{i = 1}^nG_i = X.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, $\mathcal{G}$ does not have the finite intersection property.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
An intuitive example of a collection with finite intersection property is \textbf{nested closed intervals} in Euclidean spaces. This can be generalised as the following corollary:
\begin{corbox}{Intersection of Nested Closed Sets in Compact Spaces}{nestedClosedInCompact}
    Let $X$ be a compact topological space and $\left\{G_i\right\}_{i = 1}^{\infty}$ be a sequence of closed sets in $X$ such that $G_{i + 1} \subseteq G_i$ for all $i \in \N^+$, then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcap_{i = 1}^{\infty}G_i \neq \varnothing.
    \end{equation*}
\end{corbox}
Lastly, we discuss the cardinality of compact spaces. Intuitively, a space is unlikely to be countable if its points are \textbf{dense}. To visualise the density of points, it might be helpful to think of \textbf{sparse points} as points around which there is some \textbf{empty space}.
\begin{dfnbox}{Isolated Point}{isolatePt}
    A point $x$ in a topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{isolated}} if $\left\{x\right\}$ is open in $X$.
\end{dfnbox}
We can establish an analogous connection between the density of points distributed in a space and the number of isolated points in the space. Naturally, a space contains ``fewer'' points if there are lots of isolated points.
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Uncountable Topological Spaces}{characteriseUncountable}
    Let $X$ be a non-empty compact Hausdorff space. If $X$ has no isolated point, then $X$ is uncountable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose on contrary that $X$ is countable, then we can write 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \coloneqq \left\{x_i \colon i \in I\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        for some $I \subseteq \N^+$. We consider the following lemma:
        \begin{lembox}{Non-isolated Points in Hausdorff Spaces}{closureNonIso}
            Let $X$ be a Hausdorff space. If $U \subseteq X$ is open and non-empty and $x \in X$ is not isolated, then there exists some open and non-empty subset $V \subseteq U$ with $x \notin \widebar{V}$.
            \tcblower
            \begin{proof}
                Note that $U \setminus \left\{x\right\}$ is open and non-empty because otherwise $\left\{x\right\}$ is open. Fix some $y \in U \setminus \left\{x\right\}$, then since $X$ is Hausdorff, we can find some disjoint open sets $W_{x}, W_{y} \subseteq X$ such that $x \in W_{x}$ and $y \in W_{y}$. Take $V \coloneqq W_y \cap U \subseteq U$, then $V$ is non-empty and open. Note that $X \setminus W_{x}$ is a closed set containing $V$, so $\widebar{V} \subseteq X \setminus W_{x_i}$. This means that $x \notin \widebar{V}$.
            \end{proof}
        \end{lembox}
        By Lemma \ref{lem:closureNonIso}, there exists some open set $V_1 \subseteq X$ such that $x_1 \notin \widebar{V_1}$. For each integer $i \geq 2$, we can find some open set $V_i \subseteq V_{i - 1}$ such that $x_i \notin \widebar{V_i}$. By Corollary \ref{cor:nestedClosedInCompact}, we know that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcap_{i \in I}\widebar{V_i} \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        However, for every $x_i \in X$, there exists some $V_i$ such that $x_i \notin \widebar{V_i}$, which is a contradiction.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
A natural thought is that the product space of compact spaces should be compact. However, it turns out the proof of this innocent claim is not so straightforward. We first define some preliminary concepts.
\begin{dfnbox}{Partial Order and Total Order}{order}
    A {\color{red} \textbf{partial order}} on a set $X$ is a binary relation $\leq$ on $X$ such that 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $x \leq x$;
        \item for any $x, y \in X$, if $x \leq y$ and $y \leq x$, then $x = y$;
        \item if $x \leq y$ and $y \leq z$, then $x \leq z$.
    \end{itemize}
    A {\color{red} \textbf{total order}} on a set $X$ is a partial order on $X$ such that for every $\left(x, y\right) \in X^2$, we have~$x \leq y$ or $y \leq x$.
\end{dfnbox}
Note that a partial order is not necessarily applicable to any pair of elements in the set. However, we can always purposefully choose a subset such that the restricted order becomes a total order. 
\begin{dfnbox}{Chain}{chain}
    Let $X$ be a set with a partial order $\leq$. A {\color{red} \textbf{chain}} in $X$ is a set $\mathcal{C} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ such that $\leq$ restricted to $\mathcal{C}$ is a total order.
\end{dfnbox}
An important result in set theory is \textit{Zorn's lemma}:
\begin{lembox}{Zorn's Lemma}{zorn}
    Let $X$ be a set and let $\leq$ be a partial order on $X$. If every chain $\mathcal{C}$ in $X$ has an upper bound in $X$, then $X$ contains a $\leq$-maximal element.
\end{lembox}
Zorn's lemma helps prove the following result:
\begin{probox}{Maximal Collection with the Finite Intersection Property}{maximalFIP}
    Let $X$ be a set and $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ have the finite intersection property, then there exists some maximal $\mathcal{D} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ with the finite intersection property such that $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{D}$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{A}} \coloneqq \left\{\mathcal{U} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right) \colon \mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{U} \textrm{ and } \mathcal{U} \textrm{ has the finite intersection property} \right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then $\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{A}}$ is a partially ordered set by $\subseteq$. Let $\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}} \subseteq \mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{A}}$ be any chain and define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}} \coloneqq \bigcup_{\mathcal{U} \in \mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}}\mathcal{U}.
        \end{equation*}
        Let $U_1, U_2, \cdots, U_n \in \mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}$ for some $n \in \N^+$, then for each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, there exists some $\mathcal{U}_i \in \mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}$ such that $U_i \in \mathcal{U}_i$. Note that $\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}$ is totally ordered, so there exists some~$\mathcal{U}_0 \in \mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}$ such that $\mathcal{U}_i \subseteq \mathcal{U}_0$ for all $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$. Therefore, for all $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, we have $U_i \in \mathcal{U}_0 \in \mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{A}}$, and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcap_{i = 1}^nU_i \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}$, so $\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}} \in \mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{A}}$. Clearly, $\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{A}}$ is an upper bound for $\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}$. By Lemma \ref{lem:zorn}, there exists a $\subseteq$-maximal element $\mathcal{D} \in \mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{A}}$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Following this, we can show that such maximal containing collections exhibit certain special properties. 
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of the Maximal Containing Collection}{charMaximal}
    Let $X$ be a set and $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ be a collection with the finite intersection property. For each maximal collection $\mathcal{D} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ with $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{D}$ with the finite intersection property,~$\mathcal{D}$ is closed under finite intersection and for all $A \subseteq X$ with $A \cap D \neq \varnothing$ for all $D \in \mathcal{D}$, we have $A \in \mathcal{D}$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $D_1, D_2, \cdots, D_n \in \mathcal{D}$ for some $n \in \N^+$ and define $D \coloneqq \bigcap_{i = 1}^nD_i$. Take any~$D_1', D_2', \cdots, D_m' \in \mathcal{D}$ for some $m \in \N^+$ and consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            D \cap \bigcap_{i = 1}^mD_i' = \left(\bigcap_{i = 1}^nD_i\right) \cap \left(\bigcap_{i = 1}^mD_i'\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, $\mathcal{D} \cup \left\{D\right\}$ has a finite intersection property and $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{D} \cup \left\{D\right\}$. By maximality of $\mathcal{D}$, we must have $D \in \mathcal{D}$, and so $\mathcal{D}$ is closed under finite intersection. Let $A \subseteq X$ be such that $A \cap D \neq \varnothing$ for all $D \in \mathcal{D}$, then for any $D_1, D_2, \cdots, D_k \in \mathcal{D}$ where $k \in \N^+$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            A \cap \bigcap_{i = 1}^kD_i \neq \varnothing,
        \end{equation*}
        which means that $\mathcal{D} \cup \left\{A\right\}$ contains $\mathcal{A}$ and has the finite intersection property. By maximality of $\mathcal{D}$, this means that $A \in \mathcal{D}$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
All of the above results combined together lead to the following important major theorem:
\begin{thmbox}{Tychonoff's Theorem}{Tychonoff}
    If $\left\{X_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ is a family of compact topological spaces, then $X \coloneqq \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}X_{\alpha}$ is compact.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ have the finite intersection property. By Proposition \ref{pro:maximalFIP}, there exists a maximal collection $\mathcal{D} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X\right)$ containing $\mathcal{A}$, which has the finite intersection property. For every $\alpha \in \Lambda$, define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{\widebar{\pi_{\alpha}\left(D\right)} \colon D \in \mathcal{D}\right\} \subseteq \mathcal{P}\left(X_{\alpha}\right),
        \end{equation*}
        which has the finite intersection property. Since $X_{\alpha}$ is compact, by Proposition \ref{pro:characteriseCompact}, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \Pi_{\alpha} \coloneqq \bigcap_{D \in \mathcal{D}}\widebar{\pi_{\alpha}\left(D\right)} \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*} 
        For each $\alpha \in \Lambda$, pick some $x_{\alpha} \in \Pi_{\alpha}$ and define $\mathbfit{x} \coloneqq \left(x_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$. Let $U \subseteq X$ be an open neighbourhood for $\mathbfit{x}$, then there exists an open neighbourhood $U_{\alpha} \subseteq X_{\alpha}$ for $x_{\alpha}$ for each $\alpha \in \Lambda$ such that $U = \prod_{\alpha \in \Lambda}U_{\alpha}$. Note that for all $D \in \mathcal{D}$,
        \begin{equation*}
            \widebar{\pi_{\alpha}\left(D\right)} \cap U_{\alpha} \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore,
        \begin{equation*}
            \pi_{\alpha}\left(D\right) \cap U_{\alpha} \neq \varnothing,
        \end{equation*}
        and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            D \cap \pi_{\alpha}^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:charMaximal}, $\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}\right) \in \mathcal{D}$ and for all finite $I \subseteq \Lambda$,
        \begin{equation*}
            U \subseteq \bigcap_{i \in I}\pi_{i}^{-1}\left(U_{i}\right) \in \mathcal{D}.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, for all $D \in \mathcal{D}$, we have $U \cap D \neq \varnothing$ and so $\mathbfit{x}$ is a limit point of every $D \in \mathcal{D}$. This means that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathbfit{x} \in \bigcap_{D \in \mathcal{D}}\widebar{D} \subseteq \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{A}}\widebar{A} = \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{A}}A \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:characteriseCompact}, $X$ is compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{thmbox}
\section{Limit Point and Sequentially Compact Spaces}
Let us re-visit the motivation of compactness: a space with no missing end points (i.e., end points which are infinitely far away). Now, another way to interpret this statement is that if we fix an infinite subset in the space, then we can always find a limit point of the subset in the space.
\begin{dfnbox}{Limit Point Compactness}{limPtCompact}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{limit point compact}} if every infinite subset of $X$ has a limit point in $X$.
\end{dfnbox}
Note that it is equivalent to say that every subset of a limit point compact space which has no limit point in the space must be finite.

It is possible for a non-compact space to be limit point compact. However, a compact space must be limit point compact.
\begin{probox}{Compactness Implies Limit Point Compactness}{compactToLimPtCompact}
    Any compact topological space is limit point compact.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a compact topological space and $A \subseteq X$ be a subset without any limit point in $X$, then it suffices to prove that $A$ is finite. Notice that $A = \widebar{A}$, so $A$ is closed in $X$. By Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, $A$ is compact. For every $a \in A$, since $a$ is not a limit point, by Definition \ref{dfn:limPt} there exists some open set $U_a \subseteq X$ with $a \in U_a$ but 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(A \setminus \left\{a\right\}\right) \cap U_a = \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Clearly, this means that $U_a \cap A = \left\{a\right\}$. Therefore, $\left\{a\right\}$ is open in $A$. This means that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{A} \coloneqq \bigl\{\left\{a\right\} \colon a \in A\bigr\}
        \end{equation*}
        is an open cover of $A$. Since $A$ is compact, there exists $a_1, a_2, \cdots, a_n \in A$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            A \subseteq \bigcup_{i = 1}^n\left\{a_i\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        and so $A$ is finite.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
We can further strengthen the condition by changing ``existence of limit points'' into convergence.
\begin{dfnbox}{Sequential Compactness}{seqCompact}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{sequentially compact}} if every sequence in $X$ has a convergent subsequence.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, sequential compactness should be a stronger result than limit point compactness because not every limit point is the limit of some sequence. 
\begin{probox}{Sequential Compactness Implies Limit Point Compactness}{seqCompactToLimPtCompact}
    Any sequentially compact topological space is limit point compact, but the converse is not true.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be sequentially compact and let $A \subseteq X$ be an infinite subset. Note that there exists a sequence $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N}$ in $A$, and so we can find a convergent subsequence~$\left\{a_{n_i}\right\}_{i \in \N}$ such that $\lim_{i \to \infty}a_{n_i} = a \in X$. It is clear that $a$ is a limit point of $A$ and so $A$ is limit point compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
However, the converse of this statement is false.
\begin{probox}{Limit Point Compactness Is Weaker}{limButNotSeq}
    There exists a topological space $X$ which is limit point compact but not sequentially compact.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take $X = \mathbb{R}$ and define
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{R} \coloneqq \left\{\left(a, \infty\right) \colon a \in \mathbb{R}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        We first check that $\mathcal{R}$ is a basis for a topology on $\mathbb{R}$. It is clear that for any $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we have $x \in \left(x - 1, \infty\right) \in \mathcal{R}$. Take any $x \in \mathbb{R}$ and any $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x \in \left(a, \infty\right) \cap \left(b, \infty\right)$. Without loss of generality, assume that $a < b$ and so $x \in \left(b, \infty\right) \subseteq \left(a, \infty\right) \cap \left(b, \infty\right)$. Therefore, $\mathcal{R}$ is a basis. Let $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be any infinite subset. Fix any $a \in A$, then there exists some $\left(a', \infty\right) \subseteq A$ such that $a \in \left(a', \infty\right)$. Take $\ell \coloneqq \frac{a + a'}{2}$, then for any open neighbourhood $U$ of $\ell$, there exists some $u < \ell$ such that $\ell \in \left(u, \infty\right) \subseteq U$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{a\right\}\right) \subseteq \left(u, \infty\right) \cap \left(a', \infty\right) \neq \varnothing,
        \end{equation*}
        and so $\ell$ is a limit point of $A$. Therefore, $X$ is limit point compact. Consider the sequence $\left\{-n\right\}_{n \in \N}$. We claim that the sequence has no convergent subsequence. Suppose on contrary that $\left\{-n_k\right\}_{k \in \N}$ is a convergent subsequence with 
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{k \to \infty}-n_k = N,
        \end{equation*}
        then $\left(\left\lfloor N - 1 \right\rfloor, \infty\right)$ is an open neighbourhood of $N$ but for all $k > -\left\lfloor N - 1 \right\rfloor$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            -n_k \leq -k < \left\lfloor N - 1 \right\rfloor,
        \end{equation*}
        which is a contradiction. Therefore, $\left\{-n\right\}_{n \in \N}$ has no convergent subsequence and so~$X$ is not sequentially compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Totally Bounded Spaces}
Suppose we construct an open cover for a space $X$, then intuitively, we could use some open sets which are ``big'' enough to cover the space, such that all the ``small'' subsets of the space can be contained by some single open set in the cover.
\begin{dfnbox}{Lebesgue Number}{LebesgueNum}
    Let $\mathcal{U}$ be an open cover for a metric space $X$, then $\delta > 0$ is called a {\color{red} \textbf{Lebesgue number}} for $\mathcal{U}$ if for all $S \subseteq X$ with $\mathrm{diam}\left(S\right) < \delta$, there exists some $U \in \mathcal{U}$ such that $S \subseteq U$. 
\end{dfnbox}
The existence of Lebesgue number is not guaranteed in general topological spaces. For example, for each~$n \in \Z$, define $A_n \colon \left(n, n + 1\right)$ and 
\begin{equation*}
    B_n \coloneqq \begin{cases}
        \left(-1, 1\right) & \text{ if } n = 0 \\
        \left(n - \frac{1}{n}, n + \frac{1}{n}\right) & \text{ otherwise}
    \end{cases}.
\end{equation*}
Clearly, $\left\{A_n \colon n \in \Z\right\} \cup \left\{B_n \colon n \in \Z\right\}$ is an open cover for $\R$. However, for any $\delta > 0$, there exists some $N \in \Z^+$ such that $\frac{2}{N} < \delta$. Take 
\begin{equation*}
    S \coloneqq \left(N + 1 - \frac{1}{N}, N + 1 + \frac{1}{N}\right) \subseteq \R.
\end{equation*}
Clearly, $\mathrm{diam}\left(S\right) = \frac{2}{N} < \delta$. Note that $N \leq N + 1 - \frac{1}{N} < N + 1$ and $N + 1 < N + 1 + \frac{1}{N} \leq N + 2$, so there does not exist any $n \in \Z$ such that $S \subseteq A_n$. Since $N > 0$, we have $S \not\subseteq B_0$. Notice that if $n - \frac{1}{n} \leq N + 1 - \frac{1}{N}$, then $n + \frac{1}{n} < N + 1 + \frac{1}{N}$, so $S \not\subseteq B_n$ for all $n \in \Z$. Therefore,~$\delta$ is not a Lebesgue number and so $\left\{A_n \colon n \in \Z\right\} \cup \left\{B_n \colon n \in \Z\right\}$ is an open cover of $\R$ that does not have a Lebesgue number.

However, it can be shown that Lebesgue number always exists for sequentially compact spaces.
\begin{probox}{Sequentially Compact Spaces Guarantee Lebesgue Number}{seqCompactLeb}
    Every open cover of a sequentially compact metric space has a Lebesgue number.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be sequentially compact. Suppose on contrary that there exists some open cover $\mathcal{U}$ of $X$ without a Lebesgue number, then for every $n \in \N^+$, there exists some~$S_n \subseteq X$ with $\mathrm{diam}\left(S_n\right) < \frac{1}{n}$ but $S_n \not\subseteq U$ for all $U \in \mathcal{U}$. For each~$n \in \N^+$, pick some $x_n \in S_n$, then the sequence $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ has a convergent subsequence $\left\{x_{n_i}\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$. Let 
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{i \to \infty}x_{n_i} = x \in U
        \end{equation*}
        for some $U \in \mathcal{U}$, then there exists some $\epsilon > 0$ such that $B_{\epsilon}\left(x\right) \subseteq U$. Note that there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that $\frac{1}{N} < \frac{\epsilon}{2}$ and $x_{n_i} \in B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(x\right)$ for all $i > N$. Note that for all~$i > N$, we have $n_i \geq i > N$, so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathrm{diam}\left(S_{n_i}\right) < \frac{1}{n_i} < \frac{1}{N} < \frac{\epsilon}{2}.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            S_{n_i} \subseteq B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(x_{n_i}\right) \subseteq B_{\epsilon}\left(x\right) \subseteq U,
        \end{equation*}
        which is a contradiction.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Suppose that we have constructed an open cover with a Lebesgue number $\delta$. Clearly, we can partition any space into subsets with diameter strictly less than $\delta$. This means that for each of the subset, we can first fix some $U$ containing it, and find an open ball containing $U$ in the space. In a more ideal case, such open balls will have their radius upper-bounded by an arbitrarily small number. Intuitively, this means that we will be able to cover the space with balls which are ``small'' enough.
\begin{dfnbox}{Totally Bounded Space}{totalBound}
    A metric space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{totally bounded}} if for all $\epsilon > 0$, there exists a finite cover of $X$ by open balls of radius $\epsilon$.
\end{dfnbox}
Previously, we have seen that sequential compactness is closely linked to the existence of Lebesgue numbers. Therefore, it is not so surprising that sequential compactness leads to total boundedness.
\begin{probox}{Sequential Compactness Implies Total Boundedness}{seqCompactTotalBound}
    Every sequentially compact metrisable topological space is totally bounded.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We shall prove the contrapositive. Suppose that $X$ is a metric that is not totally bounded, then there exists some $\epsilon > 0$ such that $X$ cannot be covered with open $\epsilon$-balls. This means that for any $n \in \N$ and any $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n \in X$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \setminus \bigcup_{i = 1}^nB_{\epsilon}\left(x_i\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Now, fix some $x_1 \in X$ and for each $i = 2, 3, \cdots$, pick some
        \begin{equation*}
            x_i \in X \setminus \bigcup_{j = 1}^iB_{\epsilon}\left(x_j\right),
        \end{equation*}
        then $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ is a sequence in $X$. Note that for any $i \neq j$, we have $x_j \notin B_{\epsilon}\left(x_i\right)$ and so for all $x \in X$, the open ball $B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(x\right)$ contains at most one of $x_1, x_2, \cdots$. Therefore, the sequence contains no convergent subsequence and so $X$ is not sequentially compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
A nice thing about Propositions \ref{pro:seqCompactLeb} and \ref{pro:seqCompactTotalBound} is that they apply to any compact metric space in general, which is a result of the following equivalence:
\begin{probox}{Compactness of Metrisable Spaces}{metricCompact}
    If $X$ is a metrisable topological space, then the followings are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $X$ is compact;
        \item $X$ is limit point compact;
        \item $X$ is sequentially compact.
    \end{enumerate}
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        (1) $\implies$ (2) is by Proposition \ref{pro:compactToLimPtCompact}. Now, suppose $X$ is limit point compact and let $\left\{x_i\right\}_{i \in \N}$ be any sequence in $X$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            A \coloneqq \left\{x_i \colon i \in \N\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        If $A$ is finite, then there exists some $x_k$ which appears countably infinite times in the sequence, and so $\left\{x_k\right\}_{i \in \N}$ is a convergent subsequence. If $A$ is infinite, then it has a limit point $x$. Since $X$ is a metric space, it is first countable and $T_1$. Fix a countable basis $\mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B_i \colon i \in \N^+\right\}$ for $x$ such that $B_{i + 1} \subseteq B_i$ for all $i \in \N^+$, then 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_i \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{x\right\}\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        We claim that $B_i \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{x\right\}\right)$ is infinite. Suppose on contrary that it is finite, then we can write 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_i \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{x\right\}\right) = \left\{a_1, a_2, \cdots, a_n\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        for some $n \in \N^+$. Since $X$ is $T_1$, for each $k = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, there exists some~$B_{j_k} \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $x \in B_{j_k}$ but $a_k \notin B_{j_k}$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            B \coloneqq B_i \cap \bigcap_{k = 1}^nB_{j_k} \subseteq X
        \end{equation*}
        is open but $B \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{x\right\}\right) = \varnothing$, which is a contradiction. For each $i \in \N^+$, pick some~$x_{n_i} \in B_i \cap \left(A \setminus \left\{x\right\}\right)$, and so $\left\{x_{n_i}\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ is a convergent subsequence. Therefore,~$X$ is sequentially compact.
        \\\\
        Suppose that $X$ is sequentially compact and let $\mathcal{U}$ be an open cover for $X$. By Proposition \ref{pro:seqCompactLeb}, $\mathcal{U}$ has a Lebesgue number $\delta > 0$. By Proposition \ref{pro:seqCompactTotalBound}, there exists a finite cover 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{B_{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(x_i\right) \colon i = 1, 2, \cdots, n\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        for $X$. For every $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathrm{diam}\left(B_{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(x_i\right)\right) = \frac{2\delta}{3} < \delta,
        \end{equation*}
        and so there exists some $U_i \in \mathcal{U}$ such that $B_{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(x_i\right) \subseteq U_i$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{U_i \colon i = 1, 2, \cdots, n\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        is a finite sub-cover for $X$ and so $X$ is compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox} 
By exploiting this equivalence in metric spaces, we can obtain the following result:
\begin{probox}{Compact Spaces Induce Uniform Continuity}{compactUniContinuous}
    Let $\left(X, d_X\right)$ and $\left(Y, d_Y\right)$ be metric spaces and $f \colon X \to Y$ be continuous. If $X$ is compact, then $f$ is uniformly continuous.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        For any $\epsilon > 0$, define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{\epsilon} \coloneqq \left\{B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(y\right) \colon y \in Y\right\}, \qquad \mathcal{F}_{\epsilon} \coloneqq \left\{f^{-1}\left(B\right) \colon B \in \mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}$ is an open cover for $Y$. Since $f$ is continuous, $\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}$ is an open cover for $X$. Since $X$ is compact, by Proposition \ref{pro:metricCompact}, $X$ is sequentially compact and by Proposition \ref{pro:seqCompactLeb}, $\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}$ has a Lebesgue number $\delta > 0$. For any $x_1, x_2 \in X$ with $d_X\left(x_1, x_2\right) < \delta$, there exists some open set $S \subseteq X$ such that $\mathrm{diam}\left(S\right) < \delta$ and $x_1, x_2 \in S$. Therefore, there exists some $B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(y\right) \in \mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}$ such that $x_1, x_2 \in f^{-1}\left(B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(y\right)\right)$. This means that~$d_Y\bigl(f\left(x_1\right), f\left(x_2\right)\bigr) < \epsilon$ and so $f$ is uniformly continuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
An intuition regarding totally bounded spaces is that: since we can cover the entire space with finitely many balls, then the ``size'' of the space should not be too large.
\begin{probox}{Totally Bounded Spaces Have Finite Diameter}{finDiam}
    If a metric space $X$ is totally bounded, then $\mathrm{diam}\left(X\right)$ is finite.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Fix some $\epsilon > 0$, then we can find a finite cover with open $\epsilon$-balls $\left\{B_1, B_2, \cdots, B_k\right\}$ for $X$. For each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, k$, pick some $x_i \in B_i$ and define 
        \begin{equation*}
            M \coloneqq \max\left\{d\left(x_i, x_j\right) \colon i, j = 1, 2, \cdots, k\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        For all $x, y \in X$, there exist $B_i, B_j$ with $x \in B_i$ and $y \in B_j$. Therefore,
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(x, y\right) \leq d\left(x, x_i\right) + d\left(x_i, x_j\right) + d\left(x_j, y\right) \leq M + 2\epsilon.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, $\mathrm{diam}\left(X\right) \leq M + 2\epsilon$ is finite.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Intuitively, bi-Lipschitz spaces should preserve totally bounded properties because they induce the same topology.
\begin{probox}{Bi-Lipschitz Spaces Preserve Totally Bounded Spaces}{biLipschitzTotalBound}
    If $\left(X, d\right)$ and $\left(X', d'\right)$ are bi-Lipschitz, then $\left(X, d\right)$ is totally bounded if and only if $\left(X', d'\right)$ is totally bounded.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:metricEquivalence}, $d$ and $d'$ induce the same topology, and so $\left(X, d\right)$ is totally bounded if and only if $\left(X, d'\right)$ is totally bounded.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Recall that every metric can be normalised to $\left[0, 1\right]$, so it is natural that a totally bounded metric space is still totally bounded after such normalisation.
\begin{corbox}{Normalisation Preserves Totally Bounded Spaces}{normaliseTotalBound}
    If $\left(X, d\right)$ is a metric space and $\rho\left(x, y\right) = \frac{d\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d\left(x, y\right)}$, then $\left(X, \rho\right)$ is totally bounded if and only if $\left(X, d\right)$ is totally bounded.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:rho}, $\frac{1}{2}\rho\left(x, y\right) \leq \rho\left(x, y\right) < d\left(x, y\right) < 2\rho\left(x, y\right)$ for all $x, y \in X$. Therefore, $\left(X, \rho\right)$ and $\left(X, d\right)$ are bi-Lipschitz. By Proposition \ref{pro:biLipschitzTotalBound},  $\left(X, \rho\right)$ is totally bounded if and only if $\left(X, d\right)$ is totally bounded.
    \end{proof}
\end{corbox}
Lastly, in Euclidean spaces, it is intuitive that every bounded space is totally bounded. We can push this result further by generalising to all $L^p$-metrics.
\begin{probox}{Bounded Subspaces of $L^p$ Spaces Are Totally Bounded}{propsTotalBound}
    Every subspace $X$ of $\left(\R^n, \ell_p\right)$ is totally bounded if and only if $X$ is bounded.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:finDiam}, it is clear that every totally bounded subspace $X \subseteq \R^n$ is bounded. Therefore, by Corollary \ref{cor:LpMetricGenerateStandardTopology}, it suffices to prove that every bounded subspace $X$ of $\left(\R^n, \ell_{2}\right)$ is totally bounded. Since $X$ is bounded, there exists some closed ball $B_{M}\left(\mathbfit{x}_0\right)$ for some $\mathbfit{x}_0 \coloneqq \left(x_0, x_0, \cdots, x_0\right) \in \R^n$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \subseteq B_M\left(\mathbfit{x}_0\right) \subseteq \left[x_0 - M, x_0 + M\right]^n.
        \end{equation*}
        For any $\epsilon > 0$, consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            N \coloneqq \left\{\mathbfit{x}_0 + \frac{\epsilon}{2}\left(k_1, k_2, \cdots, k_n\right) \colon k_2, \cdots, k_n \in \Z,  \frac{\epsilon}{2}k_i \leq M \textrm{ for all } i = 1, 2, \cdots, n\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $N$ is finite. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{B}_{\epsilon} \coloneqq \left\{B_{\epsilon}\left(\mathbfit{x}_N\right) \colon \mathbfit{x}_N \in N\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        which is a finite cover for $X$. Therefore, $X$ is totally bounded.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Complete Spaces}
In elementary real analysis, we examine the notion of convergence with Cauchy sequences. Recall that in $\R^n$, every Cauchy sequence is convergent. However, in general metric spaces, this might not be the case. 

For example, consider the sequence $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ defined by 
\begin{equation*}
    a_n \coloneqq \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^n,
\end{equation*}
which is a Cauchy sequence. However, $\lim_{n \to \infty}a_n = \e$, so the sequence converges in $\R$ but not in $\Q$ with respect to the standard metric. 

Informally, such behaviour is caused by the fact that there are ``break points'' in $\Q$ but not in $\R$, so in a sense $\R$ is a ``more complete'' space.
\begin{dfnbox}{Complete Space}{complete}
    A metric space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{complete}} if every Cauchy sequence in $X$ is convergent.
\end{dfnbox}
To characterise a complete metric space, it is useful to examine the conditions under which a Cauchy sequence is convergent. 
\begin{probox}{Convergence of Cauchy Sequences}{cauchyConverge}
    A Cauchy sequence converges if and only if it has a convergent subsequence.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Since every sequence is its own subsequence, it suffices to prove that a Cauchy sequence converges if it has a convergent subsequence. Let $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be a Cauchy sequence in a metric space $\left(X, d\right)$, then for any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $N_1 \in \N^+$ such that for all $p, q > N_1$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(a_p, a_q\right) < \frac{\epsilon}{2}.
        \end{equation*}
        Suppose that $\left\{a_{n_k}\right\}_{k \in \N^+}$ is a convergent subsequence. Let 
        \begin{equation*}
            L \coloneqq \lim_{k \to \infty}a_{n_k},
        \end{equation*}
        then for any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $N_2 \in \N^+$ such that $d\left(a_{n_k}, L\right) < \frac{\epsilon}{2}$ for all $k > N_2$. Take $N \coloneqq \max\left\{N_1, N_2\right\}$. Notice that for all $k > N$, we have $n_k \geq k > N$ and so
        \begin{align*}
            d\left(a_k, L\right) & \leq d\left(a_k, a_{n_k}\right) + d\left(a_{n_k}, L\right) \\
            & < \frac{\epsilon}{2} + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \\
            & = \epsilon.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $\lim_{n \to \infty}a_n = L$ and so $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Note that whether a sequence is Cauchy depends on the metric we choose to equip the space with. The following result shows an important connection between Cauchy sequences and bi-Lipschitz spaces.
\begin{probox}{Bi-Lipschitz Spaces Preserve Cauchy Sequences}{CauchyBilipschitz}
    If $\left(X, d\right)$ and $\left(X', d'\right)$ are bi-Lipschitz, then a sequence is Cauchy in $\left(X, d\right)$ if and only if it is Cauchy in $\left(X', d'\right)$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Since $\left(X, d\right)$ and $\left(X, d'\right)$ are bi-Lipschitz, there exists some $A > 1$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \frac{1}{A}d\left(x, y\right) < d'\left(x, y\right) < Ad\left(x, y\right)
        \end{equation*}
        for all $x, y \in X$. Let $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be a Cauchy sequence in $\left(X, d\right)$, then for any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that for all $m, n > N$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(a_m, a_n\right) < \frac{\epsilon}{A}.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, for all $m, n > N$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            d'\left(a_m, a_n\right) < Ad\left(a_m, a_n\right) < \epsilon,
        \end{equation*}
        which means that $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is Cauchy in $\left(X, d'\right)$. Conversely, let $\left\{b_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be a Cauchy sequence in $\left(X, d'\right)$, then for any $\epsilon' > 0$, there exists some $N' \in \N^+$ such that for all~$m, n > N'$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            d'\left(b_m, b_n\right) < \frac{\epsilon'}{A}.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, for all $m, n > N'$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(b_m, b_n\right) < Ad'\left(b_m, b_n\right) < \epsilon',
        \end{equation*}
        and so $\left\{b_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is Cauchy in $\left(X, d\right)$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Using these concepts, we shall re-visit the Euclidean space $\R^n$. First, let us argue that $\R^n$ is complete with respect to the $L^p$-metric for $p \geq 1$.

Take any Cauchy sequence $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ in $\R^n$. It is useful to prove that every Cauchy sequence must be bounded. 
\begin{probox}{Every Cauchy Sequence Is Bounded}{CauchyBounded}
    If $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a Cauchy sequence in $\left(X, d\right)$, then there exists some $M > 0$ such that for all $n \in \N^+$, we have $d\left(x_n, 0\right) \leq M$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Fix some $\epsilon > 0$, then there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that for all $m, n \geq N$, we have $d\left(x_m, x_n\right) < \epsilon$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            M \coloneqq \max\left\{d\left(x_n, 0\right) \colon n = 1, 2, \cdots, N\right\} + \epsilon,
        \end{equation*}
        then for any integer $n > N$, we have 
        \begin{align*}
            d\left(x_n, 0\right) \leq d\left(0, x_N\right) + \left(x_N, x_n\right) \leq M, 
        \end{align*}
        and so $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is bounded.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
With this result, it is clear that for any Cauchy sequence $\left\{\mathbfit{x}_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ in $\R^n$ and for all $i \in \N^+$, we have $\mathbfit{x}_i \in \widebar{B_M\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}$ for some $M > 0$. Note that $\widebar{B_M\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}$ is a compact ball in $\R^n$ and so it must be sequentially compact by Proposition \ref{pro:metricCompact}. Therefore, $\left\{\mathbfit{x}_i\right\}_{i \in \N^+}$ has a convergent subsequence and so it is convergent by Proposition \ref{pro:cauchyConverge}. Therefore, $\R^n$ is indeed complete with respect to the $L^p$-metric for $p \geq 1$.

Lastly, recall that in Proposition \ref{pro:infProdTopo}, we defined a metric over the infinite product space $\R^{\omega}$. We shall prove that this infinite product space is still complete.
\begin{probox}{Completeness of Infinite Product Euclidean Space}{infProductComp}
    Let $d$ be the standard metric on $\R$ and define $\rho$ to be a metric on $\R$ by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \rho\left(x, y\right) = \frac{d\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d\left(x, y\right)}.
    \end{equation*}
    Define $D \colon \R^{\omega} \to \R$ to be a metric by 
    \begin{equation*}
        D\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) = \sup\left\{\frac{\rho\bigl(\pi_k\left(\mathbfit{x}\right), \pi_k\left(\mathbfit{y}\right)\bigr)}{k} \colon k \in \Z^+\right\},
    \end{equation*}
    then $\left(\R^{\omega}, D\right)$ is complete.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\left\{\mathbfit{a}_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be a Cauchy sequence in $\mathbb{R}^{\omega}$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            p_{k_n} \coloneqq \pi_k\left(\mathbfit{a}_n\right).
        \end{equation*}
        We claim that $\left\{p_{k_n}\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is convergent in $\left(\R, \rho\right)$ for all $k \in \N^+$. Take any $\epsilon > 0$ and any $k \in \N^+$, then there exists some $N_k \in \N^+$ such that for all $m, n > N_k$, we have~$D\left(\mathbfit{a}_m, \mathbfit{a}_n\right) < \frac{\epsilon}{k}$. Therefore,
        \begin{equation*}
            \rho\left(p_{k_m}, p_{k_n}\right) = \rho\bigl(\pi_k\left(\mathbfit{x}\right), \pi_k\left(\mathbfit{y}\right)\bigr) \leq kD\left(\mathbfit{a}_m, \mathbfit{a}_n\right) < \epsilon.
        \end{equation*}
        This means that $\left\{p_{k_n}\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is Cauchy in $\left(\R, \rho\right)$ for all $k \in \N^+$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            B \coloneqq \left\{y \in \R \colon \rho\left(x, y\right) \leq 1\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $\rho\left(x, y\right) < 1$ for all $x, y \in \R$, so $p_{k_n} \in B$ for all $k, n \in \N^+$. Note that $B$ is compact and so sequentially compact in $\R$, which means that $\left\{p_{k_n}\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is convergent in $\left(\R, \rho\right)$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            L_k \coloneqq \lim_{n \to \infty}p_{k_n}.
        \end{equation*}
        We claim that $\left\{\mathbfit{a}_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges to $\mathbfit{a}$ where
        \begin{equation*}
