            \pi_k\left(\mathbfit{a}\right) = L_k. 
        \end{equation*}
        Observe that for any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $K \in \N^+$ such that $0 < \frac{1}{K} < \epsilon$, so for all~$k > K$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \frac{\rho\left(p_{k_n}, L_k\right)}{k} \leq \frac{1}{k} < \frac{1}{K} < \epsilon
        \end{equation*}
        for all $n \in \N^+$. Notice that for all $k \in \N^+$, for all $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $M_k \in \N^+$ such that for all~$n > M_k$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \rho\left(p_{k_n}, L_k\right) < k\epsilon.
        \end{equation*}
        Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            M \coloneqq \max\left\{M_1, M_2, \cdots, M_K\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then for all $n > M$, we have 
        \begin{align*}
            D\left(\mathbfit{a}_n, \mathbfit{a}\right) & = \sup\left\{\frac{\rho\left(p_{k_n}, L_k\right)}{k} \colon k \in \N^+\right\} \\
            & < \epsilon.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $\left\{\mathbfit{a}_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges to $\mathbfit{a}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{\omega}$. Therefore, $\mathbb{R}^{\omega}$ is complete.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Another thing we wish to investigate is whether completeness can be inherited through subspaces. It turns out that we only require some small additional condition to make this work.
\begin{probox}{Closed Subspaces Preserve Completeness}{closedComp}
    A subspace of a complete metric space is complete if and only if it is closed.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\left(X, d\right)$ be a complete metric space and $A \subseteq X$ be a subspace. Suppose that $A$ is closed, then $A = \widebar{A}$ contains all of its limit points. Let $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be any Cauchy sequence in $A \subseteq X$, then since $X$ is complete, the sequence is convergent. Let $a \coloneqq \lim_{n \to \infty}a_n$, then $a$ must be a limit point of $A$ and so $a \in \widebar{A} = A$, which means that $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is convergent in $A$. Therefore, $A$ is complete.
        \\\\
        Conversely, suppose that $A$ is complete, we shall prove that $A$ is closed by considering the contrapositive statement. Suppose that $A$ is not closed, then there exists some $a \in \widebar{A} \setminus A$ which is a limit point of $A$. Notice that $B_{\frac{1}{n}}\left(a\right) \cap A \neq \varnothing$ for all~$n \in \N^+$. For each $n \in \N^+$, pick some $a_n \in B_{\frac{1}{n}}\left(a\right) \cap A$. Note that for any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that for all $n \geq N$, we have $\frac{1}{n} < \frac{\epsilon}{2}$, and so for all $m, n \geq N$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(a_m, a_n\right) \leq d\left(a_m, a\right) + d\left(a, a_n\right) < \frac{\epsilon}{2} + \frac{\epsilon}{2} = \epsilon.
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a Cauchy sequence in $A$ but it does not converge in $A$, which means that $A$ is not complete.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
In an elementary level, something we commonly say is that an interval is compact if and only if it is closed and bounded. By now, it should become clear that completeness is a generalisation for closed-ness, and that totally boundedness is a generalisation for boundedness.
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Compact Metric Spaces}{characteriseMetricCompact}
    A metric space $\left(X, d\right)$ is compact if and only it is complete and totally bounded.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $\left(X, d\right)$ is compact, then by Proposition \ref{pro:metricCompact}, $X$ is sequentially compact and so every Cauchy sequence in $X$ has a convergent subsequence in $X$. By Proposition \ref{pro:cauchyConverge}, this means that every Cauchy sequence in $X$ is convergent and so~$X$ is complete. Notice that for any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists an open cover for $X$ with open balls of radius $\epsilon$. Since $X$ is compact, there exists a finite sub-cover with open balls of radius $\epsilon$ and so $X$ is totally bounded.
        \\\\
        Conversely, suppose that $\left(X, d\right)$ is complete and totally bounded. Let $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be any sequence in $X$. Since $X$ is totally bounded, there exists some $y_0 \in X$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_1\left(y_0\right) \cap \left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+} \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Similarly, for each $n \in \N^+$, there exists some $y_n \in X$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            B_{2^{-n}}\left(y_n\right) \cap \left(B_{2^{-\left(n - 1\right)}}\left(y_{n - 1}\right) \cap \left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Pick some $x_{k_n} \in B_{2^{-n}}\left(y_n\right)$ where $k_n \in \N^+$. For any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that for all $n \geq N$, we have $2^{-n} < \epsilon$. Therefore, for any $m, n > N$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\left(x_m, x_n\right) \leq 2^{-N} < \epsilon,
        \end{equation*}
        and so $\left\{x_{k_n}\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a Cauchy subsequence. Since $X$ is complete, the subsequence is convergent and so $X$ is sequentially compact. By Proposition \ref{pro:metricCompact}, $X$ is compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
With this very sophisticated result, we can now turn back and prove something which appears to be very na\"{i}ve (but surprisingly hard to prove):
\begin{thmbox}{Heine-Borel Theorem}{HeineBorel}
    A subspace $G \subseteq \R^n$ is compact if and only if it is closed and bounded.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Note that $\R^n$ is complete. By Proposition \ref{pro:closedComp}, $G$ is complete if and only if it is closed. By Proposition \ref{pro:propsTotalBound}, $G$ is totally bounded if and only if it is bounded. By Proposition \ref{pro:characteriseMetricCompact}, $G$ is compact if and only if it is complete and totally bounded, and so $G$ is compact if and only if it is closed and bounded.
    \end{proof}
\end{thmbox}
\section{Compactification}
In the previous section in the study of compactness, we have been treating a space as a single entity. However, another perspective is to view the space as a collection of many singleton ``points''. This motivates us to define compactness point-wise.
\begin{dfnbox}{Local Compactness}{localCompact}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{locally compact}} at $x \in X$ if there exists a compact set $K \subseteq X$ and an open set $U \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U \subseteq K$. We say that $X$ is locally compact if it is locally compact at every $x \in X$.
\end{dfnbox}
An interpretation of local compactness at a point $x$ is that we can find a strictly wider compact neighbourhood that contains $x$.

Clearly, $\R^n$ is locally compact because every $\mathbfit{x} \in \R^n$ is contained in $B_{\epsilon}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right) \subseteq \widebar{B_{\epsilon}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right)}$ which is compact for any $\epsilon > 0$. 

However, $\Q$ with respect to the standard topology is not locally compact. Let $U \subseteq \Q$ be any open set, then there exists $a, b \in \Q$ with $a < b$ such that $\left(a, b\right) \cap \Q \subseteq U$. Since $\Q$ is dense in~$\R$, there exists some $x \in \Q^c$ such that there is a sequence $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ in $\Q$ converging to $x$. This means that every convergent subsequence of $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges to $x$. Therefore, for any subset $C \subseteq \Q$ with $U \subseteq C$, it contains a sequence which does not have any convergent subsequence in itself. Therefore, $C$ is not compact, and so $\Q$ is not locally compact.

We can also show that $\R^{\omega}$ with the product topology is not locally compact. Let $U \subseteq \R^{\omega}$ be open. We claim that every set $C \subseteq \R^{\omega}$ containing $U$ is not compact. Suppose on contrary that there exists some compact $C \subseteq \R^{\omega}$ with $U \subseteq C$. By Propositions \ref{pro:metricSpaceIsNormal} and \ref{pro:relationshipSepAxioms}, since~$\R^{\omega}$ is metrisable, it is $T_4$ and therefore Hausdorff. By Proposition \ref{pro:compactClosed}, this means that $C$ is closed in $\R^{\omega}$. By Proposition \ref{pro:prodTopoBasis}, there exists some finite subset $I \subseteq \N^+$ and $\left(a_i, b_i\right) \subseteq \R$ for each $i \in I$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
    B \coloneqq \prod_{i \in I}\left(a_i, b_i\right) \times \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus I}\R \subseteq U \subseteq C.
\end{equation*}
This implies that 
\begin{equation*}
    \widebar{B} = \prod_{i \in I}\left[a_i, b_i\right] \times \prod_{i \in \N^+ \setminus I}\R \subseteq C.
\end{equation*}
By Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, $\widebar{B}$ is compact. However, the sequence $\left\{\mathbfit{x}_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ in $\widebar{B}$ defined by 
\begin{equation*}
    \pi_{i}\left(\mathbfit{x}_n\right) = \begin{cases}
        a_i & \textrm{if } i \in I \\
        n & \textrm{if } i \in \N^+ \setminus I
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
does not have any convergent subsequence in $\widebar{B}$. Therefore, $\widebar{B}$ is not sequentially compact, which is a contradiction.

Is there any connection between locally compact spaces and compact spaces? In fact, by going one step further, we may wish to find a way to transform a general space into a compact Hausdorff space. Such a transformation should preserve the structure of the pre-image space. 
\begin{dfnbox}{Compactification}{compactification}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $Y$ be a compact Hausdorff space, then $Y$ is a {\color{red} \textbf{compactification}} of $X$ if there exists a map $h \colon X \to Y$ which is homeomorphic onto $h\left(X\right)$ such that $\widebar{h\left(X\right)} = Y$. If $Y \setminus h\left(X\right)$ is a singleton set, then $Y$ is a {\color{red} \textbf{one-point compactification}} of $X$.
\end{dfnbox}
In Hausdorff spaces, it is then relatively easy to construct a compactification.
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Locally Compact Hausdorff Spaces}{characteriseLocallyCompactHausdorff}
    A topological space $X$ is locally compact and Hausdorff if and only if there exists a compact Hausdorff space $Y$ and a map $h_Y \colon X \to Y$ such that $h_Y$ is homeomorphic onto~$h_Y\left(X\right)$ and $Y \setminus h_Y\left(X\right)$ is a singleton set.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $X$ is locally compact and Hausdorff. Fix a topology $\mathcal{T}_X$ on $X$ and let $Y \coloneqq X \cup \left\{p\right\}$ for some $p \notin X$ and define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{A} \coloneqq \left\{Y \setminus C \colon C \textrm{ is compact in } X\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        We claim that $\mathcal{T}_Y \coloneqq \mathcal{T}_X \cup \mathcal{A}$ is a topology on $Y$. Let $K_1, K_2, \cdots, K_n$ be compact sets in~$X$, then $\bigcup_{i = 1}^nK_i$ is compact in $X$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcap_{i = 1}^n\left(Y \setminus K_i\right) = Y \setminus \bigcup_{i = 1}^nK_i \in \mathcal{A}.
        \end{equation*}
        Let $\left\{K_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a collection of compact sets in $X$, then $\bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}K_{\alpha}$ is closed in $K_{\alpha}$ for any~$\alpha \in \Lambda$ and so compact in $X$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}\left(Y \setminus K_{\alpha}\right) = Y \setminus \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}K_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{A}.
        \end{equation*}
        Take any $T \in \mathcal{T}_X$ and any compact set $K \subseteq X$, then $K \setminus T \subseteq K$ is closed and so compact in $X$. Therefore, 
        \begin{align*}
            T \cup \left(Y \setminus K\right) & = Y \setminus \left(K \setminus T\right) \in \mathcal{A}, \\
            T \cap \left(Y \setminus K\right) & = T \setminus K \in \mathcal{T}_X.
        \end{align*}
        It is obvious that $Y, \varnothing \in \mathcal{T}_Y$. Therefore, $\mathcal{T}_Y$ is a topology on $Y$. Define $h_Y \colon X \to Y$ by $h_Y\left(x\right) = x$, then clearly $h_Y$ is homeomorphic onto $h_Y\left(X\right)$ and $Y \setminus h_Y\left(X\right) = \left\{p\right\}$ is a singleton set. Now, let $\mathcal{U} \subseteq \mathcal{T}_Y$ be an open cover for $Y$, then there exists some $U_0 \in \mathcal{U}$ such that $p \in U_0$. Notice that $U_0 \in \mathcal{A}$, so there exists some compact set $K_0 \subseteq X$ such that $U_0 = Y \setminus K_0$. Note that $\mathcal{U}$ is an open cover for $K_0$, so there exists a finite sub-cover $U_1, U_2, \cdots, U_n$ for $K_0$. Therefore, $U_0, U_1, \cdots, U_n$ is a finite sub-cover for $Y$, and so $Y$ is compact. Take any $x \in X$, then since $X$ is locally compact, there exists some open set~$U \in \mathcal{T}_X$ and compact set $K \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U \subseteq K$. Clearly, $p \in Y \setminus K$, which is open in $Y$ and disjoint from $U$. Therefore, $Y$ is Hausdorff.
        \\\\
        Conversely, suppose that $Y$ is a compact Hausdorff space $Y$ such that there exists a map $h_Y \colon X \to Y$ which is homeomorphic onto $h_Y\left(X\right)$ such that $Y \setminus h_Y\left(X\right) = \left\{p\right\}$ is a singleton set. Note that $h_Y\left(X\right) \subseteq Y$ must be Hausdorff. Since $h_Y$ is homeomorphic onto $h_Y\left(X\right)$, we know that $X$ is Hausdorff. Take any $x \in X$, then there exist disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq Y$ such that $x \in U$ and $p \in V$. Note that $U \subseteq Y \setminus V$, which is closed and so compact since $Y$ is compact. Therefore, $X$ is locally compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Essentially, the above proposition is basically saying that if we add a new point to a locally compact Hausdorff space, the space will become a compact Hausdorff space. Intuitively, this new point can be anything, so all such spaces should be homeomorphic to each other by just re-mapping the new points.
\begin{probox}{Uniqueness of One-Point Compactification}{existenceUniquenessOnePt}
   If $X$ is a locally compact Hausdorff space and $Y$ and $Y'$ are compact Hausdorff spaces such that there exist maps $h_Y \colon X \to Y$ and $h_{Y'} \colon X \to Y'$ homeomorphic onto $h_Y\left(X\right)$ and $h_{Y'}\left(X\right)$ respectively such that $Y \setminus h_Y\left(X\right)$ and $Y' \setminus h_{Y'}\left(X\right)$ are singleton sets, then there exists a homeomorphism $f \colon Y \to Y'$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \left.f\right\rvert_{h_Y\left(X\right)} = h_{Y'} \circ \left.h_{Y}^{-1}\right\rvert_{h_Y\left(X\right)}.
    \end{equation*}
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Note that both $h_Y\left(X\right)$ and $h_{Y'}\left(X\right)$ are open in $Y$. Let 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left\{p\right\} = Y \setminus h_Y\left(X\right), \qquad \left\{p'\right\} = Y' \setminus h_{Y'}\left(X\right)
        \end{equation*}
        and define $f \colon Y \to Y'$ by 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(y\right) = \begin{cases}
                p' & \textrm{if } y = p \\
                h_{Y'}\left(h_Y^{-1}\left(y\right)\right) & \textrm{if } y \in h_Y\left(X\right)
            \end{cases}.
        \end{equation*}
        Clearly, $f$ is a bijection with 
        \begin{equation*}
            f^{-1}\left(y\right) = \begin{cases}
                p & \textrm{if } y = p' \\
                h_{Y}\left(h_{Y'}^{-1}\left(y\right)\right) & \textrm{if } y \in h_{Y'}\left(X\right)
            \end{cases}.
        \end{equation*}
        Let $U \subseteq Y'$ be any open set. If $p' \notin U$, then $U$ is open in $h_{Y'}\left(X\right)$. Therefore, $h_{Y'}^{-1}\left(U\right)$ is open in $X$, and so $f^{-1}\left(U\right) = h_Y\left(h_{Y'}^{-1}\left(U\right)\right)$ is open in $h_Y\left(X\right)$. Since $h_Y\left(X\right)$ is open in $Y$, this implies that $f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ is open in~$Y$. If $p' \in U$, define $K \coloneqq Y' \setminus U$ which is closed and therefore compact in $Y'$ by Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}. It is clear that $K$ is compact in $h_{Y'}\left(X\right)$. By Proposition \ref{pro:ContinuousCompact}, $f^{-1}\left(K\right) = h_Y\left(h_{Y'}^{-1}\left(K\right)\right)$ is compact in $h_Y\left(X\right)$ and so compact in $Y$. Since $Y$ is Hausdorff, by Proposition \ref{pro:compactClosed}, $f^{-1}\left(K\right)$ is closed in $Y$, and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            f^{-1}\left(U\right) = f^{-1}\left(Y' \setminus K\right) = Y \setminus f^{-1}\left(K\right),
        \end{equation*}
        which is open in $Y$. Therefore, $f$ is continuous. Similarly, $f^{-1}$ is continuous, and so~$f$ is a homeomorphism.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
It is no surprise that such a space $Y$ is a one-point compactification for $X$, provided that $X$ is not already compact.
\begin{probox}{Compactification of Hausdorff Spaces}{compactificationHausdorff}
    Let $X$ be a non-compact locally compact Hausdorff space and $Y$ be a compact Hausdorff space. If a map $h_Y \colon X \to Y$ is homeomorphic onto $h_Y\left(X\right)$ and $Y \setminus h_Y\left(X\right)$ is a singleton set, then $Y$ is a one-point compactification of $X$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:existenceUniquenessOnePt}, it suffices to consider $h_Y\left(x\right) = x$ and 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{T}_Y \coloneqq \mathcal{T}_X \cup \left\{Y \setminus C \colon C \textrm{ is compact in } X\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        as the topology on $Y$, where $\mathcal{T}_X$ is the topology on $X$. Let $Y = X \cup \left\{p\right\}$ for some $p \notin X$. We claim that $p$ is a limit point of $X$. Suppose on contrary that $p$ is not a limit point of $X$, then there exists some open set $U \subseteq Y$ with $p \in U$ but $U \cap X = \varnothing$. This means that $U = \left\{p\right\}$, and so $X = Y \setminus U$ is compact, which is a contradiction.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
One-point compactification has important geometric implications. Define 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{S}^2 \coloneqq \left\{\mathbfit{x} \in \R^3 \colon \norm{\mathbfit{x}} = 1\right\}
\end{equation*}
to be the $2$-dimensional unit sphere and 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{D} \coloneqq \left\{\mathbfit{x} \in \R^2 \colon \norm{\mathbfit{x}} < 1\right\}
\end{equation*}
to be the open unit disc, we can prove that both $\widebar{\mathbb{D}}$ and $\mathbb{S}^2$ are the one-point compactification for $\mathbb{D}$. In fact, since $\mathbb{D} \approx \R^2$ due to the existence of the bijection $f \colon \R^2 \to \mathbb{D}$ by
\begin{equation*}
    f\left(\mathbfit{v}\right) = \frac{\mathbfit{v}}{1 + \norm{\mathbfit{v}}},
\end{equation*}
both $\widebar{\mathbb{D}}$ and $\mathbb{S}^2$ are the one-point compactification for $\R^n$ for all $n \in \N^+$.

Since compactification is closely related to Hausdorff spaces and locally compact spaces, it is not surprising that we can make use of it to check if a Hausdorff space is locally compact.
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Locally Compact Spaces}{characteriseLocallyCompact}
    A Hausdorff space $X$ is locally compact if and only if for any $x \in X$ and any open set~$U \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$, there exists an open compact set $V \subseteq X$ such that $x \in V \subseteq U$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:characteriseLocallyCompactHausdorff}, define $Y \coloneqq X \cup \left\{p\right\}$ to be compact and 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{T}_Y \coloneqq \mathcal{T}_X \cup \left\{Y \setminus K \colon K \textrm{ is compact in } X\right\}
        \end{equation*}
        as the topology on $Y$, where $\mathcal{T}_X$ is the topology on $X$. Take any $x \in X$ and any open set~$U \subseteq X$ with $x \in U$, then $K \coloneqq Y \setminus U$ is closed and so compact because $Y$ is compact. Note that $x \notin K$. By Proposition \ref{pro:separabilityCompact}, $Y$ is $T_3$. Therefore, there exist disjoint open sets~$V, W \subseteq Y$ such that $x \in V$ and $K \subseteq W$. Since $p \in K \subseteq W$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \setminus W = Y \setminus W,
        \end{equation*}
        which is compact and so closed in $X$. Note that $V \subseteq Y \setminus W = X \setminus W$, so 
        \begin{equation*}
            x \in V \subseteq \widebar{V} \subseteq X \setminus W \subseteq X \setminus K = U,
        \end{equation*}
        which implies that $X$ is locally compact. The converse is trivial and is left to the reader as an exercise.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
This result can be utilised to check if a subspace of a locally compact space is locally compact.
\begin{corbox}{Local Compactness of Subspaces}{localCompactSubspace}
    Let $X$ be a locally compact space and $A \subseteq X$ be a subspace, then $A$ is locally compact if 
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A$ is closed, or
        \item $A$ is open and $X$ is Hausdorff.
    \end{enumerate}
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $A$ is closed. For any $x \in A \subseteq X$, there exists an open set $U \subseteq X$ and a compact set $K \subseteq X$ such that $x \in U \subseteq K$. Note that $A \cap K$ is closed in $K$, so by Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, $A \cap K$ is compact in $K$. Therefore, $A \cap K$ is compact in $X$, and so it is compact in $A$. Note that $A \cap U$ is open and $x \in A \cap U \subseteq A \cap K$, so $A$ is locally compact. 
        \\\\
        Otherwise, suppose that $A$ is open and $X$ is Hausdorff, then for any $x \in A \subseteq X$, by Proposition \ref{pro:characteriseLocallyCompact}, there exists an open compact set $V \subseteq X$ for every open neighbourhood $U \subseteq X$ of $x$ such that $x \in V \subseteq U$. Note that $A \cap V$ is open and compact in $A$ and $A \cap U$ is open in $A$, so $x \in A \cap V \subseteq A \cap U$. By Proposition \ref{pro:characteriseLocallyCompact}, $A$ is locally compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{corbox}
By using the above results, we propose the last conclusion:
\begin{corbox}{Locally Compact Hausdorff Spaces and Homeomorphisms}{characteriseLocallyCompactHausdorffHomeomorphism}
    A topological space $X$ is locally compact and Hausdorff if and only if there exists a compact Hausdorff space $Y$ such that $X$ is homeomorphic to some open subset $A \subseteq Y$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that there exists a compact Hausdorff space $Y$ such that $X$ is homeomorphic to some open subset $A \subseteq Y$. Note that by Corollary \ref{cor:localCompactSubspace}, $A$ is locally compact and so is $X$. Conversely, suppose that $X$ is locally compact, then by Proposition \ref{pro:existenceUniquenessOnePt}, there exists a compact Hausdorff space $Y \coloneqq X \cup \left\{p\right\}$ for some $p \notin X$ such that $X$ is homeomorphic to $X \subseteq Y$ which is open in $Y$.
    \end{proof}
\end{corbox}
\section{Metric Completion}
Consider $f \colon X \to Y$ to be a map. It is clear that we can write $f$ as a set of ordered pairs from $X \times Y$. By treating $X$ as an index set, every such map $f$ can be represented as a point from the product space 
\begin{equation*}
    Y^X \coloneqq \prod_{x \in X}Y.
\end{equation*}
Using the projection map, we can write 
\begin{equation*}
    f\left(x\right) = \pi_x\left(f\right).
\end{equation*}
Clearly, $Y^X$ is a topological space when equipped with the product topology. Therefore, we can define a suitable metric on it to ``measure'' the distance between maps.
\begin{dfnbox}{Uniform Metric and Uniform Topology}{uniTopo}
    Let $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space and $\rho$ be a metric on $Y$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \rho\left(x, y\right) = \frac{d\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d\left(x, y\right)}.
    \end{equation*}
    The {\color{red} \textbf{uniform metric}} on $Y^{\Lambda}$ is defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \widebar{\rho}\left(\mathbfit{x}, \mathbfit{y}\right) = \sup\left\{\rho\bigl(\pi_{\alpha}\left(\mathbfit{x}\right), \pi_{\alpha}\left(\mathbfit{y}\right)\bigr) \colon \alpha \in \Lambda\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    The {\color{red} \textbf{uniform topology}} on $Y^{\Lambda}$ is the topology generated by $\widebar{\rho}$.
\end{dfnbox}
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        The uniform topology on $\R^{\Lambda}$ is finer than the product topology and coarser than the box topology. 
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
Note that the uniform topology, product topology and box topology are pair-wise unequal on $\R^{\omega}$.

Note that $\widebar{\rho}$ is a metric on $Y^X$ because for any two maps $f, g \in Y^X$, we have 
\begin{equation*}
    \widebar{\rho}\left(f, g\right) = \sup\left\{\rho\bigl(f\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) \colon x \in X\right\},
\end{equation*}
i.e., we measure the distance between maps by taking the maximum distance between corresponding points.

The following result examines the completeness of the uniform metric spaces:
\begin{probox}{Completeness of the Uniform Metric Spaces}{uniformCompleteness}
    If $\left(Y, d\right)$ is a complete metric space, then $\left(Y^{\Lambda}, \widebar{\rho}\right)$ is complete for any index set $\Lambda$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Note that $\left(Y, d\right)$ and $\left(Y, \rho\right)$ are bi-Lipschitz, so it suffices to prove that $\left(Y, \rho\right)$ being complete implies that $\left(Y^{\Lambda}, \widebar{\rho}\right)$ is complete. Suppose that $\left(Y, \rho\right)$ is complete and let~$\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ be a Cauchy sequence. Note that for any $m, n \in \N^+$ and every $\alpha \in \Lambda$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \widebar{\rho}\left(f_m, f_n\right) = \sup\left\{\rho\bigl(f_m\left(\lambda\right), f_n\left(\lambda\right)\bigr) \colon \lambda \in \Lambda\right\} \geq \rho\bigl(f_m\left(\alpha\right), f_n\left(\alpha\right)\bigr),
        \end{equation*}
        so $\left\{f_n\left(\alpha\right)\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a Cauchy sequence in $\left(Y, \rho\right)$. Since $\left(Y, \rho\right)$ is complete, there exists some $y_{\alpha} \in Y$ for each $\alpha \in \Lambda$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{n \to \infty}f_n\left(\alpha\right) = y_{\alpha}.
        \end{equation*}
        Take $f \in Y^{\Lambda}$ with $f\left(\alpha\right) = y_{\alpha}$. Note that for every $\epsilon > 0$, there exists some $N_1 \in \N^+$ such that for all $m, n > N_1$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \rho\bigl(f_m\left(\alpha\right), f_n\left(\alpha\right)\bigr) \leq \widebar{\rho}\left(f_m, f_n\right) < \frac{\epsilon}{2}
        \end{equation*}
        for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$. Notice that there exists $N_2 \in \N^+$ such that for all $m > N_2$, we have~$\rho\bigl(f_m\left(\alpha\right), y_{\alpha}\bigr) < \frac{\epsilon}{2}$. Therefore, whenever $m, n > \max\left\{N_1, N_2\right\}$, 
        \begin{align*}
            \widebar{\rho}\left(f_n, f\right) & \leq \rho\bigl(f_n\left(\alpha\right), f\left(\alpha\right)\bigr) \\
            & \leq \rho\bigl(f_n\left(\alpha\right), f_m\left(\alpha\right)\bigr) + \rho\bigl(f_m\left(\alpha\right), y_{\alpha}\bigr) \\
            & < \epsilon,
        \end{align*}
        and so $\lim_{n \to \infty}f_n = f$. Therefore, $\left(Y^{\Lambda}, \widebar{\rho}\right)$ is complete.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Usually, given a topological space $X$ and a metric space $\left(Y, d\right)$, we denote $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ to be the set of all continuous maps from $X$ to $Y$ and $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ to be the set of all bounded maps (i.e., maps $f \colon X \to Y$ such that $\mathrm{diam}\bigl(f\left(X\right)\bigr)$ is finite) from $X$ to $Y$.
\begin{probox}{Closedness and Completness of $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ and $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$}{closedCompCandB}
    Both $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ and $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ are closed with respect to the uniform topology on $Y^{\Lambda}$. If~$\left(Y, d\right)$ is complete, then both $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ and $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ are complete.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        By Proposition \ref{pro:uniformCompleteness}, $\left(Y^X, \widebar{\rho}\right)$ is complete. By Proposition \ref{pro:closedComp}, it suffices to prove that both $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ and $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ are closed in $\left(Y^X, \widebar{\rho}\right)$. Note that both $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ and $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ are metrisable and so they are first countable. Let $f \in \widebar{\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)} \setminus \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ be a limit point. By Proposition \ref{pro:characterise1stCountableClosure}, there exists a sequence $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ in $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ such that $\lim_{n \to \infty}f_n = f$. Note that this means that $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges uniformly. By Proposition \ref{pro:uniformThenContinuous}, $f$ is continuous and so $f \in \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$. Therefore, $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is closed. Similarly, if $g \in \widebar{\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)}$, then there exists a sequence $\left\{g_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ in $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ which converges uniformly to $g$, which means that there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that for all $x \in X$,
        \begin{equation*}
            \rho\bigl(g_N\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) \leq \widebar{\rho}\left(g_N, g\right) < \frac{1}{2}.
        \end{equation*}
        Since $\rho\left(x, y\right) = \frac{d\left(x, y\right)}{1 + d\left(x, y\right)}$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\bigl(g_N\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) < \frac{\frac{1}{2}}{1 - \frac{1}{2}} = 1
        \end{equation*}
        for all $x \in X$. Notice that there exists some $M > 0$ such that $d\bigl(g_N\left(a\right), g_N\left(b\right)\bigr) < M$ for all $a, b \in X$. Therefore, for all $a, b \in X$,
        \begin{align*}
            d\bigl(g\left(a\right), g\left(b\right)\bigr) & \leq d\bigl(g\left(a\right), g_N\left(a\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(g_N\left(a\right), g_N\left(b\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(g_N\left(b\right), g\left(b\right)\bigr) \\
            & < M + 2,
        \end{align*}
        which implies that $g \in \mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$, and so $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ is closed.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
For any two bounded maps $f, g \in \mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$, it is clear that $d\bigl(f\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr)$ is bounded. Therefore, it makes sense to talk about its supremum.
\begin{dfnbox}{Supremum Metric}{supMetric}
    The {\color{red} \textbf{supremum metric}} on $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$ is defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        d_{\sup}\left(f, g\right) = \sup\left\{d\bigl(f\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) \colon x \in X\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfnbox}
With some computation, one can show that 
\begin{equation*}
    \widebar{\rho}\left(f, g\right) = \frac{d_{\sup}\left(f, g\right)}{1 + d_{\sup}\left(f, g\right)},
\end{equation*}
and so if $\left(Y, d\right)$ is complete, this implies that $\bigl(\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right), d_{\sup}\bigr)$ is complete.

Notice that if $X$ is compact, then every continuous function has a compact range and so 
\begin{equation*}
    C\left(X, Y\right) \subseteq \mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right).
\end{equation*}
Since $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is closed in $Y^X$, this means that $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is closed in $\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$. In this case, we can restrict $d_{\sup}$ to make $\bigl(\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right), d_{\sup}\bigr)$ complete as well when $\left(Y, d\right)$ is complete.

Recall that in Definition \ref{dfn:isometric}, we defined an analogous notion of homeomorphic maps in metric spaces which preserves distances. While an isometry preserves distances, it does not necessarily retain the ``size'' of the spaces. Therefore, as a further step, we may wish to construct a ``good'' isometry such that the image of the domain is not too far away from the co-domain.
\begin{dfnbox}{Metric Completion}{metricCompletion}
    Let $\left(X, d_X\right)$ be a metric space. The {\color{red} \textbf{metric completion}} of $X$ is a complete metric space~$\left(Y, d_Y\right)$ with an isometric embedding $\phi \colon X \to Y$ such that $\widebar{\phi\left(X\right)} = Y$.
\end{dfnbox}
We will show that a metric completion always exists, and it is in fact unique up to isometry.
\begin{thmbox}{Existence and Uniqueness of Metric Completion}{existenceUniquenessMetricCompletion}
    For every metric space $\left(X, d_X\right)$, there exists a complete metric space $\left(Y, d_Y\right)$ and an isometric embedding $\phi \colon X \to Y$ such that $\phi\left(X\right)$ is dense in $Y$. Furthermore, for any complete metric space $\left(Y', d_{Y'}\right)$ and any isometric embedding $\phi' \colon X \to Y'$ with~$\widebar{\phi'\left(X\right)} = Y'$, there exists an isometry $f \colon Y \to Y'$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \left. f\right\rvert_{\phi\left(X\right)} = \phi' \circ \phi^{-1}.
    \end{equation*}
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Fix some $x_0 \in X$. For every $a \in X$, define $\phi_{a} \colon X \to \R$ by 
        \begin{equation*}
            \phi_a\left(x\right) = d_X\left(x, a\right) - d_X\left(x, x_0\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Note that 
        \begin{align*}
            d\left(x, a\right) + d\left(a, x_0\right) & \geq d\left(x, x_0\right), \\
            d\left(x, x_0\right) + d\left(a, x_0\right) & \geq d\left(x, a\right),
        \end{align*}
        so we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            -d\left(a, x_0\right) \leq d\left(x, a\right) - d\left(x, x_0\right) \leq d\left(a, x_0\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, for every $x \in X$, we have $\abs{\phi_a\left(x\right)} \leq d\left(a, x_0\right)$, and so $\phi_a \in \mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right)$. Define a map $\phi \colon \left(X, d_X\right) \to \bigl(\mathcal{B}\left(X, Y\right), d_{\sup}\bigr)$ by $\phi\left(a\right) = \phi_a$. Take any $a, b \in X$ and consider 
        \begin{align*}
            d_{\sup}\left(\phi_a, \phi_b\right) & = \sup\left\{\abs{\phi_a\left(x\right), \phi_b\left(x\right)} \colon x \in X\right\} \\ 
            & = \sup\left\{\abs{d_X\left(x, a\right) - d_X\left(x, b\right)} \colon x \in X\right\} \\
            & \leq d_X\left(a, b\right) \\
            & = \abs{d_X\left(b, a\right) - d_X\left(b, b\right)} \\ 
            & \leq \sup\left\{\abs{d_X\left(x, a\right) - d_X\left(x, b\right)} \colon x \in X\right\} \\
            & = d_{\sup}\left(\phi_a, \phi_b\right).
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $d_{\sup}\left(\phi_a, \phi_b\right) = d_X\left(a, b\right)$ and so $d_{\sup}$ is an isometric embedding. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            Y \coloneqq \widebar{\phi\left(X\right)} \quad\textrm{and}\quad d_Y \coloneqq \left.d_{\sup}\right\rvert_{\widebar{\phi\left(X\right)}},
        \end{equation*}
        then by Propositions \ref{pro:closedComp} and \ref{pro:closedCompCandB}, $Y$ is complete and $\phi$ is an isometry onto $Y$. Take any complete metric space $\left(Y', d_{Y'}\right)$ and any isometric embedding $\phi' \colon X \to Y'$ such that $\widebar{\phi'\left(X\right)} = Y'$. Let $\mathcal{S}$ be the collection of all Cauchy sequences on $\phi\left(X\right)$ and define~$\sim$ to be an equivalence relation on $\mathcal{S}$ such that $\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+} \sim \left\{b_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ if and only if $\lim_{n \to \infty}a_n = \lim_{n \to \infty}b_n$. Consider $\mathcal{A} \coloneqq \mathcal{S} \slash \sim$ and define $m \colon \mathcal{A}^2 \to \R$ by 
        \begin{equation*}
            m\left(\left[\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}, \left[\left\{b_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}\right) = d_Y\left(\lim_{n \to \infty}a_n, \lim_{n \to \infty}b_n\right).
        \end{equation*}
        One may check that $m$ is a metric. Define $g \colon \mathcal{A} \to Y$ by 
        \begin{equation*}
            g\left(\left[\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}\right) = \lim_{n \to \infty}a_n.
        \end{equation*}
        One may check that $g$ is an isometry. Define $\mathcal{A}'$, $m'$ and $g'$ on $\phi'\left(X\right)$ similarly and consider $F \colon \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{A}'$ defined by 
        \begin{equation*}
            F\left(\left[\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}\right) = \left[\left\{\phi'\bigl(\phi\left(x_n\right)\bigr)\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim} \coloneqq \Phi_x.
        \end{equation*}
        One can check that $F$ is well-defined because $\phi' \circ \phi \colon \phi\left(X\right) \to \phi'\left(X\right)$ is an isometry. Consider 
        \begin{align*}
            m'\left(\Phi_a, \Phi_b\right) & = d_Y\left(\lim_{n \to \infty}\phi'\bigl(\phi\left(a_n\right)\bigr), \lim_{n \to \infty}\phi'\bigl(\phi\left(b_n\right)\bigr)\right) \\
            & = \lim_{n \to \infty}d_Y\Bigl(\phi'\bigl(\phi\left(a_n\right)\bigr), \phi'\bigl(\phi\left(b_n\right)\bigr)\Bigr) \\
            & = \lim_{n \to \infty}d_Y\left(a_n, b_n\right) \\
            & = d_Y\left(\lim_{n \to \infty}a_n, \lim_{n \to \infty}b_n\right) \\
            & = m\left(\left[\left\{a_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}, \left[\left\{b_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}\right).
        \end{align*}
        Define $f \coloneqq g' \circ F \circ g^{-1}$, then $f$ is an isometry. For each $y \in \phi\left(X\right)$, we have 
        \begin{align*}
            f\left(x\right) & = g'\Bigl(F\left(\left[\left\{x\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}\right)\Bigr) \\
            & = g'\left(\left[\left\{\phi'\bigl(\phi\left(x\right)\bigr)\right\}_{n \in \N^+}\right]_{\sim}\right) \\
            & = \phi'\bigl(\phi\left(x\right)\bigr).
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $\left. f\right\rvert_{\phi\left(X\right)} = \phi' \circ \phi^{-1}$.
    \end{proof}
\end{thmbox}
\section{Equicontinuity}
Recall that in Definition \ref{dfn:pointwiseAndUniform}, we defined the notion of uniform convergence. The idea of uniform convergence is that as $n \to \infty$, the sequence of functions will stay within a \textbf{small and confined} neighbourhood from the limit. In general topological spaces, this ``confined-ness'' can be described using compact sets.
\begin{dfnbox}{Compact Convergence}{compactConvergence}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space. A sequence of functions~$\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ from $X$ to $Y$ is said to {\color{red} \textbf{converge compactly}} to $f \colon X \to Y$ if for every compact subset $K \subseteq X$, 
    \begin{equation*}   
        \lim_{n \to \infty}\sup_{x \in K}d\bigl(f_n\left(x\right), f\left(x\right)\bigr) = 0.
    \end{equation*} 
\end{dfnbox}
\begin{notebox}
    \begin{remark}
        Equivalently, $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges compactly to $f$ if and only if $\left\{\left.f_n\right\rvert_K\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges uniformly to $\left.f\right\rvert_K$.
    \end{remark}
\end{notebox}
Note that compact convergence alone does not guarantee that $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ will converge in~$Y^X$. However, we can define a special type of topologies to establish the correspondence.
\begin{dfnbox}{Topology of Compact Convergence}{compactConv}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space. For each $f \in Y^X$ and each compact set $K \subseteq X$, define 
    \begin{equation*}
        B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right) \coloneqq \left\{g \in Y^X \colon d_{\sup}\left(\left.f\right\rvert_K, \left.g\right\rvert_K\right) < \epsilon\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    where $\epsilon > 0$. The topology generated by
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{B} \coloneqq \left\{B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right) \colon f \in Y^X, K \subseteq X \textrm{ is compact}, \epsilon > 0\right\}
    \end{equation*} 
    is called the {\color{red} \textbf{topology of compact convergence}} on $Y^X$.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, each basic open ball $B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right)$ can be thought of the collection of all the functions~$g \in Y^X$ which, when restricted to $K$, are within a distance of $\epsilon$ from $f$. This topology is so named because $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N}$ in $Y^X$ converges to $f$ with respect to the topology if and only if $\left\{\left. f_n \right\rvert_K\right\}_{n \in \N}$ converges to $\left. f\right\rvert_K$ uniformly for any compact set $K \subseteq X$. 

Now, to describe the convergent behaviour of functions, we need to confine their images into some ``regions'' in the co-domain. The following definition provides one tool to do this.
\begin{dfnbox}{Compact-Open Topology}{compactOpen}
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be topological spaces. For every compact set $K \subseteq X$ and open set $U \subseteq Y$, define 
    \begin{equation*}
        S\left(K, U\right) \coloneqq \left\{g \in \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right) \colon g\left(K\right) \subseteq U\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    The topology generated by the sub-basis
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{S} \coloneqq \left\{S\left(K, U\right) \colon K \subseteq X \textrm{ is compact}, U \subseteq Y \textrm{ is open}\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    is the {\color{red} \textbf{compact-open topology}} on $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, the compact-open topology consists of continuous functions in $Y^X$ where the images of compact sets are contained in an open neighbourhood. With some well-defined metric, every topology of compact convergence restricted to continuous functions is actually a compact-open topology, which makes sense because we would expect a compactly convergent function to have images confined within open sets for every compact pre-image.
\begin{probox}{Topology of Compact Convergence over Continuous Maps}{compactConvToCompactOpen}
    Let $X$ is a topological space and $\left(Y, d\right)$ is a metric space. Let $\mathcal{T}_K$ be the topology of compact convergence and $\mathcal{T}_O$ be the compact-open topology on $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$, then $\mathcal{T}_K = \mathcal{T}_O$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any $B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right) \subseteq \mathcal{T}_K$. For every $x \in X$, let $V_x \subseteq X$ be an open set defined by 
        \begin{equation*}
            V_x \coloneqq f^{-1}\left(B^d_{\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\bigl(f\left(x\right)\bigr)\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Since $f$ is continuous, by Proposition \ref{pro:continuousMapProps}, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(\widebar{V_x}\right) \subseteq \widebar{f\left(V_x\right)} \subseteq B^d_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\bigl(f\left(x\right)\bigr).
        \end{equation*}
        Notice that $\left\{V_x \colon x \in K\right\}$ is an open cover for $K$. Since $K$ is compact, there exists a finite sub-cover $\left\{V_{x_1}, V_{x_2}, \cdots, V_{x_n}\right\}$ for $K$. Define $K_i \coloneqq K \cap \widebar{V_{x_i}}$ for each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, which is closed and thus compact in $K$. Consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \coloneqq \bigcap_{i = 1}^nS\left(K_i, B^d_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\bigl(f\left(x_i\right)\bigr)\right),
        \end{equation*}
        which is a basic open set in $\mathcal{T}_O$. Note that for every $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(K_i\right) \subseteq f\left(\widebar{V_{x_i}}\right) \subseteq B^d_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\bigl(f\left(x_i\right)\bigr),
        \end{equation*}
        which means that $f \in U$. For every $g \in U$ and every $x \in K$, we have $x \in K_i$ for all~$i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, and so 
        \begin{align*}
            d\bigl(g\left(x\right), f\left(x\right)\bigr) & \leq d\bigl(g\left(x\right), f\left(x_i\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(f\left(x_i\right), f\left(x\right)\bigr) \\
            & < \frac{\epsilon}{2} + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \\
            & = \epsilon.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, $d_{\sup}\left(\left.g\right\rvert_K, \left.f\right\rvert_K\right) < \epsilon$ and so $g \in B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right)$. Therefore, $f \in U \subseteq B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right)$, and so we have $\mathcal{T}_K \subseteq \mathcal{T}_O$. Take any compact set $K \subseteq X$ and any open set $U \subseteq Y$. For every~$f \in S\left(K, U\right)$, since $f$ is continuous, $f\left(K\right)$ is compact by Proposition \ref{pro:ContinuousCompact}. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            \epsilon_f \coloneqq \inf\left\{d\left(x, y\right) \colon x \in f\left(K\right), y \in Y \setminus U\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        We claim that $\epsilon_f > 0$. Suppose on contrary that $\epsilon_f = 0$, then for every $n \in \N^+$, there exists some $x_n \in f\left(K\right)$ and some $y_n \in Y \setminus U$ such that $d\left(x_n, y_n\right) < \frac{1}{n}$. Since $f\left(K\right)$ is a metrisable compact space, it is sequentially compact by Proposition \ref{pro:metricCompact}, and so there exists a convergent subsequence $\left\{x_{k_n}\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ of $\left\{x_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$. Let $x \coloneqq \lim_{n \to \infty}x_{k_n}$. Consider 
        \begin{equation*}
            0 \leq d\left(x, y_{k_n}\right) \leq d\left(x, x_{k_n}\right) + d\left(x_{k_n}, y_{k_n}\right).
        \end{equation*}
        By Squeeze Theorem, this means that $x$ is a limit point of $Y \setminus U$. However, $Y \setminus U$ is closed, which means that $x \in \left(Y \setminus U\right) \cap f\left(K\right)$. This is not possible as~$f\left(K\right) \subseteq U$. Notice that for every $g \in B\left(K, f, \epsilon\right) \subseteq \mathcal{T}_K$, we have $d\bigl(f\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) < \epsilon_f$ for all $x \in K$ and so $g\left(K\right) \subseteq U$ by the definition of $\epsilon_f$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f \in B\left(K, f, \epsilon_f\right) \subseteq S\left(K, U\right),
        \end{equation*}
        and so $\mathcal{T}_O \subseteq \mathcal{T}_K$. In conclusion, $\mathcal{T}_K = \mathcal{T}_O$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Both compact-open topology and topology of compact convergence are deeply related with compact sets in a space. More strictly, we define a type of spaces in which the open sets are coherent in the space itself and in the compact subspaces.
\begin{dfnbox}{Compactly Generated Space}{compactGen}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{compactly generated}} if $A$ is open in $X$ if and only if $A \cap K$ is open in $K$ for every compact set $K$ in $X$.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, we can flip the condition in the above definition to base on closed sets.
\begin{probox}{Alternative Definition for Compactly Generated Spaces}{altCompactGen}
    A topological space $X$ is compactly generated if $A$ is closed in $X$ if and only if $A \cap K$ is closed in $K$ for every compact set $K$ in $X$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $U \subseteq X$ be any open set, then $X \setminus U$ is closed. Therefore, $\left(X \setminus U\right) \cap K$ is closed in $K$, and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \cap K = K \setminus \bigl(\left(X \setminus U\right) \cap K\bigr)
        \end{equation*}
        is open in $K$. Conversely, suppose that $U \subseteq X$ is such that $U \cap K$ is open in $K$, then 
        \begin{equation*}
            K \setminus U \cap K = K \cap \left(X \setminus U\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is closed in $K$. This means that $X \setminus U$ is closed in $X$, and so $U$ is open in $X$. Therefore,~$X$ is compactly generated.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Let us now try to characterise compactly generated spaces.
\begin{probox}{Sufficient Conditions for Compactly Generated Spaces}{suffCondCompactGen}
    A topological space $X$ is compactly generated if it is locally compact or first countable.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $X$ is locally compact. Take any $A \subseteq X$ such that $A \cap K$ is open in $K$ for every compact set $K \subseteq X$. For every $a \in A$, let $U_a \subseteq X$ be any open neighbourhood of $a$, then since $X$ is locally compact, there exists some compact set $K_a \subseteq X$ be a compact set such that $a \in U_a \subseteq K_a$, then 
        \begin{equation*}
            A \cap U_a = \left(A \cap K_a\right) \cap U_a
        \end{equation*}
        is open in $U_a$, and so $A \cap U_a$ is open in $X$. Notice that $a \in A \cap U_a \subseteq A$, so 
        \begin{equation*}
            A = \bigcup_{a \in A}\left(A \cap U_a\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is open in $X$. Therefore, $X$ is compactly generated.
        \\\\
        Suppose that $X$ is first countable and take $B \subseteq X$ such that $B \cap K$ is closed in~$K$ for every compact set $K \subseteq X$. Take any $b \in \widebar{B}$. Since $X$ is first countable, by Proposition \ref{pro:characterise1stCountableClosure}, there exists a sequence $\left\{b_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ in $B$ such that $\lim_{n \to \infty}b_n = b$. take
        \begin{equation*}
            K \coloneqq \left\{b_n \colon n \in \N^+\right\} \cup \left\{b\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then $K$ is compact and so $B \cap K$ is closed. Therefore, since $b$ is a limit point for $B \cap K$, we have $b \in B \cap K$ and so $b \in B$. This means that $\widebar{B} = B$, and so $B$ is closed. By Proposition \ref{pro:altCompactGen}, $X$ is compactly generated.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
The name ``compactly generated'' suggests that such a space is completely characterised by its compact subsets. This has important implications on how we could characterise continuity over the space.
\begin{probox}{Continuity in Compactly Generated Spaces}{contCompactGen}
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be topological spaces. If $X$ is compactly generated, then a map $f \colon X \to Y$ is continuous if and only if for each compact set $K \subseteq X$, $\left.f\right\rvert_K$ is continuous.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        The converse direction is trivial, so it suffices to prove for the sufficiency. Suppose that $\left.f\right\rvert_K$ is continuous for any compact set $K \subseteq X$. Take any open set $V \subseteq Y$, then for any compact set $K \subseteq X$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            f^{-1}\left(V\right) \cap K = \left.f\right\rvert_K^{-1}\left(V\right),
        \end{equation*}
        which is open in $K$. Since $X$ is compactly generated, this means that $f^{-1}\left(V\right)$ is open in $X$, and so $f$ is continuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
This leads to the following corollary:
\begin{corbox}{The Set of Continuous Maps Is Closed}{contMapSetClosed}
    Let $X$ be a compactly generated space and $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space, then~$\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right) \subseteq Y^X$ is closed under the topology of compact convergence.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $f \in Y^X$ be any limit point of $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$. It suffices to show that $f \in \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$, i.e., $f$ is continuous. Note that $B\left(K, f, \frac{1}{n}\right)$ is a basic open neighbourhood of $f$ in $Y^X$ for every compact set $K \subseteq X$ and every $n \in \N^+$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            B\left(K, f, \frac{1}{n}\right) \cap \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right) \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        This means that for each $n \in \N^+$, there exists some $f_n \in B\left(K, f, \frac{1}{n}\right) \cap \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$. Clearly, $\left\{\left.f_n\right\rvert_K\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges to $\left.f\right\rvert_K$ uniformly, and so by Proposition \ref{pro:uniformThenContinuous}, $\left.f\right\rvert_K$ is continuous. By Proposition \ref{pro:contCompactGen}, $f$ is continuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{corbox}
To aim for a finer control quantitatively for a family of functions, we investigate a way to describe the ``variation'' of the functions in a given neighbourhood. In the ideal scenario, all the functions in the family have the same variation, which is formally captured by the following definition:
\begin{dfnbox}{Equicontinuity}{equicontinuity}
    Let $X$ be a topological space, $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space and $\mathcal{Y} \subseteq \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$. We say that~$\mathcal{Y}$ is {\color{red} \textbf{equicontinuous at $x_0 \in X$}} if for all $\epsilon > 0$, there exists an open set $U \subseteq Y$ such that for all $f \in \mathcal{Y}$, 
    \begin{equation*}
        d\bigl(f\left(x_0\right), f\left(x\right)\bigr) < \epsilon
    \end{equation*}
    for all $x \in U$. We say that $\mathcal{Y}$ is {\color{red} \textbf{equicontinuous}} if it is equicontinuous at every $x \in X$. 
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, a family of functions is equicontinuous around a point $x$ if they can be arbitrarily close to the point when evaluated in a carefully chosen neighbourhood. Naturally, this implies that the distance between the functions must be upper bounded, which motivates the following sufficient condition:
\begin{probox}{Sufficient Condition for Equicontinuity}{suffCondEquicontinuity}
    Let $X$ be a topological space, $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space and $\widebar{\rho}$ be the uniform metric on~$\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$. If $\mathcal{Y} \subseteq \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is totally bounded with respect to $\widebar{\rho}$, then it is equicontinuous.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Take any $x_0 \in X$ and any $\epsilon > 0$. Define $\delta \coloneqq \frac{\epsilon}{1 + \epsilon} > 0$. Since $\mathcal{Y}$ is totally bounded, there exists $f_1, f_2, \cdots, f_n \in \mathcal{Y}$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{Y} \subseteq \bigcup_{i = 1}^nB_{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(f_i\right)
        \end{equation*}
        for some $n \in \N^+$. For each $i = 1, 2, \cdots, n$, since $f_i$ is continuous, 
        \begin{equation*}
            U_i \coloneqq f^{-1}_i\left(B^{\rho}_{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(x_0\right)\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is an open neighbourhood of $x_0$. Note that for all $x \in U_i$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \rho\bigl(f_i\left(x\right), f_i\left(x_0\right)\bigr) < \frac{\delta}{3}.
        \end{equation*}
        Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            U \coloneqq \bigcap_{i = 1}^nU_i \subseteq X
        \end{equation*}
        which is an open neighbourhood of $x_0$. Take any $f \in \mathcal{Y}$. Notice that $f \in B_{\frac{\delta}{3}}\left(f_k\right)$ for some $k \in \left\{1, 2, \cdots, n\right\}$, so for all $x \in U$, we have 
        \begin{align*}
            \frac{d\bigl(f\left(x\right), f\left(x_0\right)\bigr)}{d\bigl(f\left(x\right), f\left(x_0\right)\bigr) + 1} & = \rho\bigl(f\left(x\right), f\left(x_0\right)\bigr) \\
            & \leq \rho\bigl(f\left(x\right), f_i\left(x\right)\bigr) + \rho\bigl(f_i\left(x\right), f_i\left(x_0\right)\bigr) + \rho\bigl(f_i\left(x_0\right), f\left(x_0\right)\bigr) \\
            & < \frac{\delta}{3} + \frac{\delta}{3} + \frac{\delta}{3} \\
            & = \frac{\epsilon}{\epsilon + 1}.
        \end{align*}
        Since $x \mapsto \frac{x}{x + 1}$ is strictly increasing, we have $d\bigl(f\left(x\right), f\left(x_0\right)\bigr) < \epsilon$, and so $\mathcal{Y}$ is equicontinuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Referring back to the proof of Proposition \ref{pro:infProductComp}, we see that if $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a sequence in $Y^X$ and that every component $\left\{\pi_x\left(f_n\right)\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges, then $\left\{f_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ converges in the product space, where the components of the limit are the limits of the component sequences. However, notice that $\pi_x\left(f_n\right) = f_n\left(x\right)$, so convergence in the product space with respect to the product topology is essentially point-wise convergence. Therefore, the product topology is sometimes referred to as the \textit{topology of point-wise convergence}. In particular, each basic open ball centred at $f$ in the product topology can be written as 
\begin{equation*}
    B_f \coloneqq \prod_{x \in F}B_{\epsilon_i}\bigl(f\left(x\right)\bigr) \times \prod_{x \in X \setminus F}X
\end{equation*}
for some finite $F \subseteq X$. Note that $F$ is compact and this is the same as $B\left(F, f, \epsilon\right)$. On the other hand, for every open ball $B\left(F, f, \epsilon\right)$, we can easily find a basic open set in the product topology to contain it.

On the other hand, it is easy to see that convergence with respect to the uniform topology is essentially uniform convergence.

In an equicontinuous family, however, since the functions have equal variation, we expect both types of convergence to be equivalent.
\begin{probox}{Convergence of Equicontinuous Families}{equicontinuousFamiliesConverge}
    Let $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ be an equicontinuous family. If $\mathcal{T}_K$ and $\mathcal{T}_P$ are the topology of compact convergence and the topology of point-wise convergence respectively on $\mathcal{G}$, then $\mathcal{T}_K = \mathcal{T}_P$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        It is clear that $\mathcal{T}_K \subseteq \mathcal{T}_P$. Let $g \in \mathcal{G}$ and consider the basic open set $B\left(K, g, \epsilon\right)$ for any compact set $K \subseteq X$ and any $\epsilon > 0$, then it suffices to prove that there exists a basic open set in $\mathcal{T}_P$ which is contained in $B\left(K, g, \epsilon\right) \cap \mathcal{G}$. Since $\mathcal{G}$ is equicontinuous, for every $x \in X$, there exists an open neighbourhood $U_x$ of $x$ such that for all $y \in U_x$, 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\bigl(f\left(x\right), f\left(y\right)\bigr) < \frac{\epsilon}{3}
        \end{equation*}
        for every $f \in \mathcal{G}$. Since $K$ is compact, there exist $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n \in K$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            K = \bigcup_{i = 1}^nU_{x_i}.
        \end{equation*}
        Notice that 
        \begin{equation*}
            g \in B\left(\left\{x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n\right\}, g, \frac{\epsilon}{3}\right) \cap \mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{T}_P.
        \end{equation*}
        For every $x \in K$, there exists some $i \in \left\{1, 2, \cdots, n\right\}$ such that $x \in U_{x_i}$ and so if 
        \begin{equation*}
            h \in B\left(\left\{x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n\right\}, g, \frac{\epsilon}{3}\right) \cap \mathcal{G},
        \end{equation*}
        we have 
        \begin{align*}
            d\bigl(h\left(x\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) & \leq d\bigl(h\left(x\right), h\left(x_i\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(h\left(x_i\right), g\left(x_i\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(g\left(x_i\right), g\left(x\right)\bigr) \\
            & < \frac{\epsilon}{3} + \frac{\epsilon}{3} + \frac{\epsilon}{3} \\
            & = \epsilon.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            g \in B\left(\left\{x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_n\right\}, g, \frac{\epsilon}{3}\right) \cap \mathcal{G} \subseteq B\left(K, g, \epsilon\right).
        \end{equation*}
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Let us consider a special map known as the \textit{evaluation map}, which takes in a function as a first class citizen together with a point, and output the function value at the point. Intuitively, this map is likely to be continuous if the functions in its domain are continuous.
\begin{probox}{Continuity of Evaluation}{contEvaluation}
    Let $X$ be a locally compact Hausdorff space and $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space. If $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is equipped with the compact-open topology, then the evaluation map $e \colon X \times \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right) \to Y$ defined by 
    \begin{equation*}
        e\left(x, f\right) = f\left(x\right)
    \end{equation*}
    is continuous.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $V \subseteq Y$ be open. Take any $\left(x, f\right) \in e^{-1}\left(V\right)$, then $f\left(x\right) \in V$. Since $f$ is continuous, $x \in f^{-1}\left(V\right)$ is open in $X$. Pick some open neighbourhood $U_{x, f}$ of $x$ in~$f^{-1}\left(V\right)$. Since $X$ is locally compact, there exists a compact set $K$ in $f^{-1}\left(V\right)$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            x \in U_{x, f} \subseteq \widebar{U_{x, f}} \subseteq K \subseteq f^{-1}\left(V\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Since $X$ is Hausdorff, by Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, $\widebar{U_{x, f}}$ is compact in $f^{-1}\left(V\right)$, so $S\left(\widebar{U_{x, f}}, V\right)$ is open in the compact-open topology and 
        \begin{equation*}
            U_{x, f} \times S\left(\widebar{U_{x, f}}, V\right) \subseteq X \times \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is open with respect to the product topology. Take any $\left(u, g\right) \in U_{x, f} \times S\left(\widebar{U_{x, f}}, V\right)$, then $e\left(u, g\right) = g\left(u\right) \in V$ and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(x, f\right) \in U_{x, f} \times S\left(\widebar{U_{x, f}}, V\right) \subseteq e^{-1}\left(V\right).
        \end{equation*}
        Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            e^{-1}\left(V\right) = \bigcup_{\left(x, f\right) \in e^{-1}\left(V\right)}U_{x, f} \times S\left(\widebar{U_{x, f}}, V\right)
        \end{equation*}
        is open in the compact-open topology, so $e$ is continuous.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
The above results combined allow us to prove the following major theorem:
\begin{thmbox}{Arzela-Ascoli Theorem}{ArzelaAscoli}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $\left(Y, d\right)$ be a metric space. For each $\mathcal{Y} \subseteq \mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$, define 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{Y}_a \coloneqq \left\{f\left(a\right) \colon f \in \mathcal{Y}\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    for each $a \in X$. If $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is equipped with the compact-open topology, then $\widebar{\mathcal{Y}}$ is compact if it is equicontinuous and $\widebar{\mathcal{Y}_a}$ is compact for all $a \in X$. The converse is true if $X$ is locally compact and Hausdorff. 
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $\mathcal{G}$ be the closure of $\mathcal{Y}$ in $Y^X$. Note that both $Y$ and $Y^X$ are metric spaces and so they are Hausdorff. Since $\widebar{\mathcal{Y}_a}$ is compact for all $a \in X$, by Theorem \ref{thm:Tychonoff}, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{K} \coloneqq \prod_{a \in X}\widebar{\mathcal{Y}_a}
        \end{equation*}
        is compact in $Y^X$, and so it is closed by Proposition \ref{pro:compactClosed}. Observe that $\mathcal{Y} \subseteq \mathcal{K}$, so we have $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{K}$ and so by Proposition \ref{pro:closedCompact}, $\mathcal{G}$ is compact. By Proposition \ref{pro:suffCondEquicontinuity}, for every $x_0 \in X$ and every $\epsilon > 0$, there exists an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x_0$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            d\bigl(f\left(x\right), f\left(x_0\right)\bigr) < \frac{\epsilon}{3}
        \end{equation*}
        for all $x \in U$ and all $f \in \mathcal{Y}$. Take any $g \in \mathcal{G}$. For each $x \in X$, define 
        \begin{align*}
            V_x & \coloneqq B\left(\left\{x\right\}, g, \frac{\epsilon}{3}\right) \cap B\left(\left\{x_0\right\}, g, \frac{\epsilon}{3}\right) \\
            & = \pi_x^{-1}\left(B_{\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\bigl(g\left(x\right)\bigr)\right) \cap \pi_{x_0}^{-1}\left(B_{\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\bigl(g\left(x_0\right)\bigr)\right) \\
            & \subseteq Y^X,
        \end{align*}
        which is an open neighbourhood of $g$. By Proposition \ref{pro:clProps}, we have $V_x \cap \mathcal{Y} \neq \varnothing$. Take any~$h \in V_x \cap \mathcal{Y}$, then 
        \begin{align*}
            d\bigl(g\left(x\right), g\left(x_0\right)\bigr) & \leq d\bigl(g\left(x\right), h\left(x\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(h\left(x\right), h\left(x_0\right)\bigr) + d\bigl(h\left(x_0\right), g\left(x_0\right)\bigr) \\
            & < \frac{\epsilon}{3} + \frac{\epsilon}{3} + \frac{\epsilon}{3} \\
            & = \epsilon.
        \end{align*}
        One can check that $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ is Hausdorff with respect to the compact-open topology. By Proposition \ref{pro:equicontinuousFamiliesConverge}, $\mathcal{G}$ is with the topology of compact convergence, and so is a subspace of $\mathcal{C}\left(X, Y\right)$ by Proposition \ref{pro:compactConvToCompactOpen}. Notice that $\mathcal{G}$ is compact in a Hausdorff space, so it is closed. Therefore, $\widebar{\mathcal{Y}} \subseteq \mathcal{G}$, and so $\widebar{\mathcal{Y}}$ is compact.
    \end{proof}
\end{thmbox}
\chapter{Connectedness}
\section{Connected Spaces}
Colloquially, when we are talking about a space or ``region'', we can distinguish between a ``contiguous'' region and a ``disconnected'' region based on whether one has to ``jump'' across ``gaps'' in order to traverse in the region. In this section, we will formalise this notion of connectedness.
\begin{dfnbox}{Separation}{separation}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. A {\color{red} \textbf{separation}} of $X$ is a pair of disjoint non-empty open subsets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $U \cup V = X$. A space is {\color{red} \textbf{connected}} if it does not contain any separation.    
\end{dfnbox}
In other words, a separation is a bipartition of a space with non-empty open sets. This is intuitive because if $2$ disjoint non-empty open sets can cover the space, then there must exists some gap in between the boundaries of the $2$ sets.

Now, consider a disconnected space $X$ formed by two connected non-empty spaces $A$ and $B$. Intuitively, both $A$ and $B$ are also closed because their closure are equal to themselves. This inspires the following characterisation of a connected space:
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Connected Spaces}{connectedSpaces}
    A topological space $X$ is connected if and only if the only sets which are both open and closed in $X$ are $X$ and $\varnothing$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We shall prove the contrapositive statement, i.e., a topological space $X$ is disconnected if and only if there exists some non-empty proper subset of $X$ which is both closed and open. Suppose that there exists some non-empty $U \subseteq X$ which is both open and closed such that $X \setminus U \neq \varnothing$. Note that $X \setminus U$ is open and disjoint from~$U$ and that $U \cup \left(X \setminus U\right) = X$, so $X$ is disconnected. Conversely, suppose that~$X$ is disconnected, then there exists disjoint non-empty open subsets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $U \cup V = X$. Note that $U = X \setminus V \neq \varnothing$ and $V = X \setminus U \neq \varnothing$, so both $U$ and $V$ are also closed proper subsets of $X$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Intuitively, if we can find a separation in a space $X$, then any connected subspace of $X$ should not be ``cut off'' by the separation.
\begin{probox}{Property of Conencted Subspaces}{connectedSubspaces}
    If $U, V$ are a separation of a topological space $X$ and $Y \subseteq X$ is a connected subspace, then either $Y \subseteq U$ or $Y \subseteq V$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We shall prove the contrapositive statement. Let $U, V$ be a separation of $X$, then $U = X \setminus V$. Suppose that $Y \not\subseteq U$ and $Y \not\subseteq V$, then it suffices to prove that $Y$ is not connected. Since $Y \not\subseteq U$, then 
        \begin{equation*}
            Y \cap \left(X \setminus U\right) = Y \cap V \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Similarly, $Y \cap U \neq \varnothing$. Since $U \cup V = X$, we have 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(Y \cap U\right) \cup \left(Y \cap V\right) = Y.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that both $Y \cap U$ and $Y \cap V$ are open in $Y$, so they form a separation of $Y$. Therefore,~$Y$ is not connected, and so if $Y$ is connected, then either $Y \subseteq U$ or $Y \subseteq V$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Naturally, connectedness should be transitive, i.e., if two connected subsets are not disjoint, then their union should be connected. 
\begin{probox}{Connectedness of the Union of Connected Subspaces}{connectedUnion}
    If $\left\{A_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ is a collection of connected subsets in a topological space $X$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha} \neq \varnothing,
    \end{equation*}
    then $\bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha}$ is connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose on contrary that $\bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ is disconnected, then there exists non-empty disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $\bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha} \subseteq U \cup V$. Without loss of generality, suppose that $U \cap \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha} \neq \varnothing$ and take $p \in U \cap \bigcap_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha}$. Note that $p \in A_{\alpha}$ for all~$\alpha \in \Lambda$, so $p \in A_{\alpha} \cap U \neq \varnothing$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$. We consider two cases. If there exists some $a \in A_{\alpha} \setminus U$ for some $\alpha \in \Lambda$, then $a \in V$. However, since $A_{\alpha}$ is connected, this means that there exists some $b \in A_{\alpha} \setminus \left(U \cup V\right)$, which implies that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha} \not\subseteq U \cup V
        \end{equation*}
        and is a contradiction. If $A_{\alpha} \subseteq U$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$, then \begin{equation*}
            \bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha} \subseteq U
        \end{equation*}
        and so 
        \begin{equation*}
            \bigcup_{\alpha \in \Lambda}A_{\alpha} \cap V = \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Since $A_{\alpha} \neq \varnothing$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$, this means that $V = \varnothing$, which is a contradiction.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
By intuition, if we slightly expand the boundary of a connected subset, then the resultant set should remain connected. 
\begin{probox}{Slightly Larger Supersets of Connected Sets Are Connected}{connectedSlightlyLarger}
    If $A$ is connected in a topological space $X$ and $A \subseteq B \subseteq \widebar{A}$ for some $B \subseteq X$, then $B$ is connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose on contrary that $B$ is disconnected, then there exist disjoint non-empty open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $B \subseteq U \cup V$. By Proposition \ref{pro:connectedSubspaces}, without loss of generality, assume that $A \subseteq U$, then $B \subseteq \widebar{A} \subseteq \widebar{U}$. Note that $V \subseteq X \setminus \widebar{U}$, so $V \cap B = \varnothing$, which is a contradiction.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Recall that continuous maps preserve open sets, so it is also natural that the image of a connected set under a continuous map is also connected.
\begin{probox}{Continuous Maps Preserve Connectedness}{connectedContinuous}
    If $f \colon X \to Y$ is continuous and $A \subseteq X$ is connected, then $f\left(A\right)$ is connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We shall prove the contrapositive statement. Suppose that $f\left(A\right)$ is disconnected, then there exists non-empty disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq Y$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            f\left(A\right) = \bigl(U \cap f\left(A\right)\bigr) \cup \bigl(V \cap f\left(A\right)\bigr).
        \end{equation*}
        Note that $f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ and $f^{-1}\left(V\right)$ are disjoint non-empty open sets in $X$ and 
        \begin{equation*}
            A \subseteq f^{-1}\left(U\right) \cup f^{-1}\left(V\right),
        \end{equation*}
        so $A$ is disconnected.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Lastly, if we take the Cartesian product of two connected spaces, then this should not result in any new ``gap''.
\begin{probox}{Cartesian Products of Connected Spaces Are Connected}{connectedCartesian}
    If $X$ and $Y$ are connected topological spaces, then $X \times Y$ is connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We shall prove the contrapositive statement. Suppose that $X \times Y$ is disconnected, then there exists non-empty disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X \times Y$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            X \times Y = U \cup V.
        \end{equation*}
        Note that there exists non-empty open sets $U_X, V_X \subseteq X$ and non-empty open sets~$U_Y, V_Y \subseteq Y$ such that $U = U_X \times U_Y$ and $V = V_X \times V_Y$. We claim that $U_X$ and $V_X$ are disjoint or $U_Y$ and $V_Y$ are disjoint because otherwise $U$ and $V$ are not disjoint. Without loss of generality, assume that $U_X$ and $V_X$ are disjoint. Notice that for every $\left(x, y\right) \in U \cup Y$, either $x \in U_X$ or $x \in V_X$, so $X = U_X \cup V_X$ and is disconnected.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
For those with background in graph theory, the discussion by now should have been clear that connectedness in topological spaces is a generalisation of connectedness in graphs. For example:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \ref{dfn:separation} is analogous to the fact that a graph is disconnected if and only if we can break it into two subgraphs sharing no common vertex such that there is no edge between their vertices in the original graph.   
    \item \ref{pro:connectedSpaces} is analogous to the fact that if a graph can be broken up into two subgraphs sharing no common vertex such that there is no edge between their vertices in the original graph, then any connected subgraph must be contained by one of the two subgraphs.
    \item \ref{pro:connectedSubspaces} is analogous to the fact that if we augment two connected graphs by identifying some vertices, then the resulting graph is still connected.
\end{enumerate}
Therefore, it is not surprising that we can generalise the notion of a \textit{path} to topological spaces.
\begin{dfnbox}{Path}{path}
    Let $X$ be a topological space. For any $x, y \in X$, a {\color{red} \textbf{path}} from $x$ to $y$ is a continuous map~$f \colon \left[a, b\right] \to X$ such that $f\left(a\right) = x$ and $f\left(b\right) = y$. A space is {\color{red} \textbf{path connected}} if there is a path between any two points in the space.
\end{dfnbox}
Clearly, path connectedness is a stronger version of connectedness.
\begin{probox}{Path Connectedness Implies Connectedness}{pathConnectedImpliesConnected}
    A space is connected if it is path connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        We shall prove the contrapositive statement. Let $X$ be a disconnected space, then there exists non-empty disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $X = U \cup V$. Take some $u \in U$ and some $v \in V$. Suppose on contrary that there exists a continuous map~$f \colon \left[a, b\right] \to X$ such that $f\left(a\right) = u$ and $f\left(b\right) = v$. Notice that $f^{-1}\left(U\right)$ and~$f^{-1}\left(V\right)$ are disjoint non-empty open sets, so there exists some $x \in \left[a, b\right]$ such that there is no $y \in X$ with $f\left(x\right) = y$, which is not possible. Therefore, $X$ is not path connected.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
One might wonder if the converse of the above result is true. Intuitively, it should not be true. Specifically, we consider the following counter example known as the \textit{topologist's sine curve}: define 
\begin{equation*}
    S \coloneqq \left\{\left(x, y\right) \in \R^2 \colon y = \sin\frac{2\pi}{x}, x \in \left(0, 1\right]\right\}
\end{equation*}
and let $f \colon \left(0, 1\right] \to S$ be defined as 
\begin{equation*}
    f\left(t\right) = \left(t, \sin\frac{2\pi}{t}\right).
\end{equation*}
One may check that $S = f\bigl(\left(0, 1\right]\bigr)$ is path connected and thus connected. Now, consider 
\begin{equation*}
    \widebar{S} = S \cup \left(\left\{0\right\} \times \left[-1, 1\right]\right),
\end{equation*}
which is still connected but not path connected.
\begin{probox}{Topologist's Sine Curve Is Not Path Connected}{notPathConnected}
    Let $S$ be defined as
    \begin{equation*}
        S = \left\{ (x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 : y = \sin \left( \frac{2 \pi}{x} \right), 0 < x \leq 1 \right\}
    \end{equation*}
    and define
    \begin{equation*}
        \widebar{S} = S \cup \left(\left\{0\right\} \times \left[-1, 1\right]\right),
    \end{equation*}
    then $\widebar{S}$ is not path connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        It suffices to show that there exists two points in $\widebar{S}$ such that there is no path between them. We shall prove that there is no path from $\left(0, 0\right)$ to any point $\left(x, y\right) \in S$. Suppose on contrary that there exists some $f \colon \left[a, b\right] \to \widebar{S}$ which is continuous such that $f\left(a\right) = \left(0, 0\right)$ and $f\left(b\right) = \left(x, y\right)$, then $f^{-1}\left(\left\{0\right\} \times \left[-1, 1\right]\right)$ is closed in $\left[a, b\right]$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            M \coloneqq \max f^{-1}\left(\left\{0\right\} \times \left[-1, 1\right]\right)
        \end{equation*}
        and consider $g \colon \left[M, b\right] \to \widebar{S}$. Notice that there exists some $y_0 \in \left[-1, 1\right]$ such that~$g\left(M\right) = \left(0, y_0\right)$ and $g\bigl(\left(M, b\right]\bigr) \subseteq S$. Without loss of generality, assume $y_0 \geq 0$. Write $g\left(t\right) = \bigl(x\left(t\right), y\left(t\right)\bigr)$, then $x\left(M\right) = 0$ and for all $t \in \left(M, b\right]$, we have $x\left(t\right) > 0$ and $y\left(t\right) = \sin\frac{2\pi}{x\left(t\right)}$. Note that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{t \to 0^+}y\left(t\right) = y_0 \geq 0
        \end{equation*}
        because $y$ is a continuous projection. For each $n \in \N^+$, there exists some $z_n$ with 
        \begin{equation*}
            0 < z_n < x\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
        \end{equation*}
        such that $\sin\frac{2\pi}{z_n} = \left(-1\right)^n$. Note that $x$ is continuous because it is a projection, so there exists some $t_n \in \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right)$ such that $x\left(t_n\right) = z_n$. This means that $\left\{t_n\right\}_{n \in \N^+}$ is a sequence such that $y\left(t_n\right) = \left(-1\right)^n$ for all $n \in \N^+$. Now, notice that for any $\delta > 0$, there exists some $N \in \N^+$ such that $\frac{1}{N} < \delta$ and there exists some $k > N$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \abs{y\left(t_k\right) - y_0} > \frac{1 - y_0}{2} > 0,
        \end{equation*}
        which means that $y\left(t\right)$ does not converge to $y_0$ as $t \to 0^+$ and is a contradiction. Therefore, $\widebar{S}$ must not be path connected.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
\section{Connected Components}
Recall that in a graph, connectedness between vertices is an equivalence relation. Analogously, we can define a relation between two points in a topological space based on whether there exists a connected set containing both points.

One may check that such a relation is indeed an equivalence relation. In particular, we can partition a space using the equivalence classes.
\begin{dfnbox}{Connected Component}{cc}
    Let $\sim$ be a binary relation on a topological space $X$ such that $x \sim y$ if and only if there exists a connected subset $C \subseteq X$ with $x, y \in C$. The {\color{red} \textbf{connected components}} of $X$ are the equivalence classes in the quotient $X \slash\sim$.
\end{dfnbox}
Intuitively, $X \slash \sim$ contains all maximal connected subsets of $X$, which leads to the following obvious result:
\begin{probox}{Connected Components Are Connected}{ccConnected}
    Every connected component of a topological space is connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $C \subseteq X$ be a connected component and fix some $x_0 \in C$. For each $x \in C$, there exists some connected subset $A_x \subseteq C$ with $x_0, x \in A_x$. Therefore, 
        \begin{equation*}
