            x_0 \in \bigcap_{x \in C}A_x \neq \varnothing.
        \end{equation*}
        Notice that $C = \bigcup_{x \in C}A_x$, so by Proposition \ref{pro:connectedUnion}, $C$ is connected.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Similarly, we can define another equivalence relation using path connectedness.
\begin{dfnbox}{Path Component}{pathComponent}
    Let $\psim$ be a binary relation on a topological space $X$ such that $x \psim y$ if and only if there exists a path from $x$ to $y$ in $X$. The {\color{red} \textbf{path components}} of $X$ are the equivalence classes in the quotient $X \slash\psim$.
\end{dfnbox}
Again, one may check that $X \slash\psim$ contains all maximal path-connected subsets of $X$.
\begin{probox}{Path Components Are Path Connected}{pathConnected}
    Every path component of a topological space is path connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $X$ be a topological space and $P \subseteq X$ be a path component in $X$. By definition, $P = \left[x_0\right]_{\psim}$ for some $x_0 \in X$, where $\left[x_0\right]_{\psim}$ is the equivalence class of $x_0$ under the equivalence relation $\psim$. Take any $x, y \in P$, then $x \psim x_0$ and $y \psim x_0$. By transitivity, we have $x \psim y$ and so there exists a path from $x$ to $y$. Therefore, $P$ is path connected. 
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Recall that path connectedness is a stronger version of connectedness, so it is natural that every path component is contained in some connected component.
\begin{probox}{Connected Components Contain Path Components}{ccContainPath}
    Every path component of a topological space is contained in some connected component.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $P$ be a path component of a topological space $X$. By Proposition \ref{pro:pathConnected}, $P$ is path connected and by Proposition \ref{pro:pathConnectedImpliesConnected}, $P$ is connected. Note that for every $x \in P$, we have $x \sim x_0$ and so $P \subseteq \left[x_0\right]_{\sim}$, where $\left[x_0\right]_{\sim}$ is the equivalence class of $x_0$ under the equivalence relation $\sim$, which is a connected component of $X$.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Similar to our discussion of compactness, we can define connectedness in a local perspective.
\begin{dfnbox}{Local Connectedness}{localConnectedness}
    A topological space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{locally (path) connected at $x \in X$}} if for all open sets $U \subseteq X$ containing $x$, there exists a (path) connected open set $V \subseteq X$ such that $x \in V \subseteq U$. The space $X$ is {\color{red} \textbf{locally (path) connected}} if it is locally connected at every $x \in X$.
\end{dfnbox}
Even though local connectedness is ``local'', connectedness does not imply local connectedness.
\begin{probox}{Topologist's Sine Curve Is Not Locally Connected}{notLocallyConnected}
    Let $S$ be defined as
    \begin{equation*}
        S = \left\{ (x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 : y = \sin \left( \frac{2 \pi}{x} \right), 0 < x \leq 1 \right\}
    \end{equation*}
    and define
    \begin{equation*}
        \widebar{S} = S \cup \left(\left\{0\right\} \times \left[-1, 1\right]\right),
    \end{equation*}
    then $\widebar{S}$ is not locally connected.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        It suffices to show that there exists an open subset $U \subseteq \widebar{S}$ which contains some~$\mathbfit{p} \in \widebar{S}$ such that all open subsets of $U$ are disconnected. Take $\mathbfit{p} \coloneqq \left(0, 0\right)$ and consider
        \begin{equation*}
            U \coloneqq B_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbfit{p}\right) \cap \widebar{S}
        \end{equation*}
        which is open in $\widebar{S}$ containing $\mathbfit{p}$. Let $W \subseteq U$ be any open set containing $\mathbfit{p}$, then there exists some open set 
        \begin{equation*}
            V \subseteq B_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbfit{p}\right)
        \end{equation*}
        such that $W = V \cap \widebar{S}$. Note that $V$ contains some $B_{\epsilon}\left(\mathbfit{p}\right)$ where $0 < \epsilon < \frac{1}{2}$. Take 
        \begin{equation*}
            \delta \coloneqq \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\epsilon,
        \end{equation*}
        then $V$ contains the open square $\left(-\delta, \delta\right)^2$. Define 
        \begin{equation*}
            D_{\delta} \coloneqq \left\{x \in \left(0, \delta\right) \colon \abs{\sin\frac{2\pi}{x}} < \delta\right\},
        \end{equation*}
        then 
        \begin{equation*}
            W = \left\{\left(0, y\right) \in \R^2 \colon -\delta < y < \delta\right\} \cup \left\{\left(x, \sin\frac{2\pi}{x}\right) \in \R^2 \colon x \in D_{\delta}\right\}.
        \end{equation*}
        We claim that $W$ is disconnected. Notice that there exists some $x_0 \in \left(0, \delta\right)$ such that~$\sin\frac{2\pi}{x} = 1$. Consider 
        \begin{align*}
            X_1 & \coloneqq W \cap \left(\left(-\infty, x_0\right) \times \R^2\right), \\
            X_2 & \coloneqq W \cap \left(\left(x_0, +\infty\right) \times \R^2\right).
        \end{align*}
        Since $\left\{\left(0, y\right) \in \R^2 \colon -\delta < y < \delta\right\} \subseteq X_1$, we know that $X_1 \neq \varnothing$. Since there exists some $x' \in \left(x_0, \delta\right)$ such that $\abs{\sin\frac{2\pi}{x}} < \delta$, we know that $X_2 \neq \varnothing$. Furthermore, 
        \begin{equation*}
            \left(\left\{x_0\right\} \times \R\right) \cap W = \varnothing,
        \end{equation*}
        so $X_1 \cup X_2 = W$. Since both $X_1$ and $X_2$ are open, $W$ is disconnected. Therefore, $\widebar{S}$ is not locally connected. 
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
In the ideal case, since every point in a topological space is contained by some (path) connected component, the space is naturally locally (path) connected if each of these components is open. This motivates the following characterisation:
\begin{probox}{Characterisation of Locally (Path) Connected Spaces}{localConnectedSpaces}
    A topological space $X$ is locally (path) connected if and only if for any open subset~$U \subseteq X$, every (path) connected component of $U$ is open.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Let $U \subseteq X$ be any open set and $C \subseteq U$ be a (path) connected component. Since $X$ is locally (path) connected, then for each $c \in C \subseteq U$, there exists a (path) connected open set $V_c \subseteq X$ such that $c \in V_c \subseteq U$. Note that $V_c \subseteq C$, so $C = \bigcup_{c \in C}V_c$ is open. Conversely, suppose that for any open subset $U \subseteq X$, every (path) connected component of $U$ is open. Take any $x \in X$, then there exists some open set $U$ containing $x$. Since the (path) connected components of $U$ form a partition, there exists some open (path) connected component $C_x \subseteq U$ with $x \in C_x$. By Propositions \ref{pro:ccConnected} and \ref{pro:pathConnected}, $C_x$ is (path) connected and so $X$ is locally (path) connected.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
It turns out that there is no need to differentiate between local connectedness and local path connectedness because the components induced by the two relations are the same in a local context.
\begin{probox}{Connected Components in Locally Path Connected Spaces}{localConnectedEquiv}
    If $X$ is a locally path connected topological space, then $X \slash \sim = X \slash \psim$.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Suppose that $P$ is a path component of $X$ but not a connected component. Note that by Proposition \ref{pro:ccContainPath}, there exists some connected component $C \subseteq X$ such that~$Q \coloneqq C \setminus P \neq \varnothing$. By Proposition \ref{pro:localConnectedSpaces}, since $X$ is locally path connected, both $P$ and $C$ are open, and so $Q$ is also open. Therefore, $C = P \cup Q$ where $P$ and $Q$ are disjoint non-empty open sets, which is a contradiction because $C$ is connected by Proposition \ref{pro:ccConnected}. 
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
Note that connectedness is an equivalence relation which induces a partition of the space by its connected components, so it is natural to link connectedness to quotient topologies.
\begin{probox}{Quotient Topology over Connected Components Is Discrete}{quotientConnectedDiscrete}
    If $X$ is a locally path connected space and $\widetilde{X} \coloneqq X \slash \sim$ are the connected components of $X$, then the quotient topology on $\widetilde{X}$ is discrete.
    \tcblower
    \begin{proof}
        Since the quotient topology is unique, it suffices to prove that the discrete topology $\mathcal{P}\left(\widetilde{X}\right)$ is a quotient topology with respect to $p \colon X \to \widetilde{X}$ such that $x \in p\left(x\right)$, which is equivalent to proving that $p$ is a quotient map with respect to $\mathcal{P}\left(\widetilde{X}\right)$. Take any open set $\mathcal{S} \in \mathcal{P}\left(\widetilde{X}\right)$, then $p^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\right) = \bigcup_{C \in \mathcal{S}}C$. Note that each $C \in \mathcal{S}$ is a connected component of $X$. Since $X$ is locally path connected, then each $C \in \mathcal{S}$ is a path component of $X$. Note that $X$ is open in $X$, so every $C \in \mathcal{S}$ is open in $X$. This implies that $p^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)$ is open in $X$. Conversely, take any open set $U \subseteq X$, then 
        \begin{equation*}
            p\left(U\right) = \left\{C \in \widetilde{X} \colon C \cap U \neq \varnothing\right\} \in \mathcal{P}\left(\widetilde{X}\right),
        \end{equation*}
        which is clearly open in $\widetilde{X}$. Therefore, $p$ is a quotient map and so $\mathcal{P}\left(\widetilde{X}\right)$ is the quotient topology.
    \end{proof}
\end{probox}
